14Uniform estimates for marked correlations

14.1Several marked insertions on the restarted history

We keep the actual restarted density from Subsection 13.12. Its additive remainder is removed only in the auxiliary unmarked normalization. The marked integral itself must retain that remainder. We first prove the finite algebra that identifies all the selected insertion coefficients, then explain this mixed normalization and the extra local bound it requires.

14.1.1Selecting a cell in the marker algebra

For a finite set \(V\), let

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{A}_V =\mathbb{C} [z_v : v \in V] / (z_v^2 : v \in V) .\)

Its basis consists of \(z_A = \prod_{v \in A} z_v\), \(A \subseteq V\). Products satisfy \(z_A z_B = z_{A \cup B}\) for disjoint sets and are zero otherwise. If \(F\) has zero constant coefficient, define \(\exp_{\ast} F = \sum_{j = 0}^{|V|} F^j / j!\). Every product of more than \(|V|\) nonconstant monomials has a repeated variable, so it vanishes. Hence this finite exponential satisfies the usual addition formula for commuting elements of zero constant coefficient, by the binomial theorem. In particular, for every finite family of pieces,

\(\displaystyle \prod_{\alpha} (1 + y_{\alpha} z_{A_{\alpha}}) = \exp_{\ast} \left( \sum_{\alpha} y_{\alpha} z_{A_{\alpha}} \right) \quad (A_{\alpha} \ne \varnothing) .\) (14.1)

There is no disjointness assumption on the \(A_{\alpha}\): their overlapping products vanish on both sides.

For \(T \subseteq V\), define \(\pi_T\) by setting \(z_v = 0\) for \(v \not\in T\). Thus \(\pi_T (\sum_A c_A z_A) = \sum_{A \subseteq T} c_A z_A\). It is an algebra homomorphism, and therefore commutes with \(\exp_{\ast}\). For a finite cell set \(\mathcal{C}\) and mark set \(J\), put \(V =\mathcal{C} \times J\). The cell embedding \(\iota_c : \mathscr{A}_J \to \mathscr{A}_V\) sends \(z_j\) to \(z_{(c, j)}\); it too is an algebra homomorphism.

Fix one coarse cell \(c\) and a selected set \(T \subseteq \{c\} \times J\). The incoming insertion pieces are indexed by pairs \((c', \alpha)\). Each piece has a nonempty marker support \(A_{c', \alpha}\) lying in \(\{c' \} \times J\). Its value in a particular sector, forest, tag and Gaussian coordinate is \(y_{c', \alpha}\). The spatial geometry selects exactly those pieces for which \(A_{c, \alpha} \subseteq T\); write \(\mathcal{I}_T\) for this selected index set and write \(B_{\alpha} \subseteq J\) for their marker supports with the cell coordinate removed. Define the planted polynomial

\(\displaystyle P_T = \sum_{\alpha \in \mathcal{I}_T} y_{c, \alpha} z_{B_{\alpha}} .\)

Applying \(\pi_T\) to (14.1) proves

\(\displaystyle \pi_T \prod_{c', \alpha} (1 + y_{c', \alpha} z_{A_{c', \alpha}}) = \pi_T \iota_c (\exp_{\ast} P_T) .\) (14.2)

To check the selection explicitly, a piece from \(c' \ne c\) has at least one marker outside \(T\) and is killed. A piece from \(c\) is retained exactly when its entire marker support is contained in \(T\). A discarded monomial maps to zero, so its factor \(1 + y_{c', \alpha} z_{A_{c', \alpha}}\) maps to one. In the expanded product, every term selecting a discarded monomial vanishes. This proves the identity at all orders simultaneously.

The spatially planted value is the same masked field value as in the left side. Write it as \(\Gamma_{\alpha} (\xi) h_{\alpha} (U (\xi))\), where \(0 \leqslant \Gamma_{\alpha} \leqslant 1\) is its local cutoff. On the region where \(\Gamma_{\alpha} > 0\), the chart's healthy-field condition makes the original mask equal to one on the full inspected support. Where \(\Gamma_{\alpha} = 0\), both products are zero. Consequently this formula agrees everywhere with \(\Gamma_{\alpha} \text{masked} (h_{\alpha}) (U (\xi))\). The sector and tag are fixed during this verification. They are not chosen separately for the different readers.

All linear maps on \(\mathscr{A}_V\) are continuous because this space is finite dimensional. Hence an integrable polynomial-valued function can be integrated coefficientwise, and its integral commutes with \(\pi_T\) and \(\iota_c\). If \(\nu_t\) are the finitely many sector/tag measures and \(\lambda_t\) their scalar weights, (14.2) gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_t \lambda_t \int \pi_T \prod_{c', \alpha} (1 + y_{c', \alpha, t} z_{A_{c', \alpha}}) \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_t = \pi_T \iota_c \left( \sum_t \lambda_t \int \exp_{\ast} P_{T, t} \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_t \right) .\) (14.3)

Here integrability is provided by the common positive marker weights and the paid product envelopes: the absolute value of each coefficient is bounded by its weighted polynomial norm divided by its positive monomial weight. The Gaussian/Haar envelope bounds that norm by an integrable function. For the countable activity menu, first apply the identity to finite truncations. The summable full-support majorants and coefficientwise countable regrouping of Subsection 3.13 provide the limiting integrable coefficients. Dominated convergence uses the same Gaussian/Haar envelope for the full menu, independently of truncation; the finite-dimensional linear maps commute with those limits. Thus no interchange of a merely formal infinite series is used in (14.3).

14.1.2The marked density and its three kinds of factors

Let \(P\) denote the fine representation and \(Q\) the coarse one. Write \(\mathcal{K}\) for a finite family of lifted background packets, with full spatial supports \(X_b\) and fine flag inventories \(F_b\). Its unmarked coefficients reproduce the activities of \(P\) at each activity address. This addresswise identity is stronger than equality of their summed gases. For regular insertion pieces \(h_{\alpha}\) with nonempty marker supports, the exact polynomial density is

\(\displaystyle D_P (U) = e^{\text{exponent}_P (U)} \exp_{\ast} \left( \sum_{\alpha} z_{A_{\alpha}} \text{masked} (h_{\alpha}) (U) \right) \sum_{B \text{admissible}} \prod_{b \in B} K_b (U) .\) (14.4)

Admissibility requires pairwise disjoint full supports and the exact identity \(\bigcup_{b \in B} F_b = \text{flags}_P (U)\). It is not enough to require just disjoint inventories.

Fix the actual coarse configuration, positioned chart, forest, tag and Gaussian coordinate. The unmarked chart expansion, including its Haar Jacobian and Gaussian normalization, has the following sliced form. For each fine flag set \(F\),

\(\displaystyle \boldsymbol{1}_{\{ \text{flags}_P (U) = F\}} J (U) e^{\text{exponent}_P (U)} = \Lambda \sum_{A \text{compatible} (F)} \prod_{a \in A} p_a .\) (14.5)

Here \(J\) denotes the full scalar chart factor, and \(\Lambda\) is the same positive loading used by the unmarked construction. Each \(p_a\) has a fixed coarse support, a fine inventory and a coarse inventory. Compatibility means disjoint coarse supports, fine inventory union \(F\), coarse inventory union equal to the coarse flags, and the sector key specified by those same packets. These are precisely the data of the unmarked sector expansion of Subsection 8.29.

To obtain the marked identity, first fix a background packet family \(B\). If its full supports overlap its contribution is zero. Otherwise multiply (14.5), with \(F = \bigcup_{b \in B} F_b\), by \(\prod_{b \in B} K_b\) and by the insertion product (14.1). The indicator on the left imposes exactly the background flag test in (14.4). Expand the insertion product as a sum over its subsets. On the right we now have three independent kinds of factors: scalar body packets \(p_a\), background packets \(K_b\), and marked insertion pieces. Index their union by the disjoint union of these three alphabets. This is a bijection between the three subset choices and the subsets of the union alphabet. The flag and sector tests are unchanged. If a marker is used twice, the corresponding product is zero in \(\mathscr{A}_V\), exactly as on the expanded left side. Summing over \(B\) proves the full marked chart identity at every coefficient.

Integrate with the common Gaussian/Haar product envelopes, sum the sector partition and average the tags. The coefficientwise argument of Subsection 3.13 then applies to this same three-alphabet expansion. It gives the pushed density \(\mathcal{T}D_P\) represented by the resulting coarse polynomial activities and their regular exponential. In particular this step does not follow by repeating the one-address affine argument of Subsection 8.33; that argument alone has no mixed higher coefficients.

14.1.3Restoring the additive remainder before normalization

Put \(P^0 = \text{white} (P)\), so that its additive remainder has been deleted while its coupling, path, masks and forced activities are unchanged. In the notation of Subsection 13.12, \(P = e^{- E} P^0\). On the actual chart the additional exponent is a sum \(\sum_i f_i = - E (U)\) over local coarse supports. Suppose its support bounds \(b_i \geqslant 0\) satisfy \(|f_i | \leqslant b_i\) and have anchored weighted sum at most \(d \leqslant 1\). The functions \(f_i\) are real-valued. Let \(M_i\) be the real-valued unperturbed local exponents, with

\(\displaystyle |e^{M_i} - 1| \leqslant w_i, \qquad w_i \geqslant 0, \qquad \text{anchored weighted sum of } w_i \leqslant e \leqslant 1.\)

The weights are at least one, so individually \(b_i \leqslant d\) and \(w_i \leqslant e\). Hence \(e^{M_i} \leqslant 2\) and \(|e^{f_i} - 1| \leqslant 2 |f_i | \leqslant 2 b_i\). The latter elementary inequality holds for real \(|f_i | \leqslant 1\), by convexity of the exponential on \([0, 1]\) and \(e - 1 < 2\), and by \(1 - e^{- t} \leqslant t\) for \(t \geqslant 0\). It follows that

\(\displaystyle |e^{M_i + f_i} - 1| \leqslant |e^{M_i} - 1| + e^{M_i} |e^{f_i} - 1| \leqslant w_i + 4 b_i .\)

Thus the perturbed auxiliary bound is at most \(e + 4 d\). The base Gaussian quadratic form and the scalar loading have not changed. This proves (14.5) for \(P\) itself from the neutral chart expansion: add \(f_i\) to the same local exponents and use their exact sum \(- E (U)\). The full-support locality of these terms was established in the restarted-history construction, including the final flag tests.

For the actual scale purchases, write \(\omega\) for the permitted perturbation bound. The neutral auxiliary bound is at most \(\omega\) and \(d \leqslant \omega\), so the preceding bound is at most \(5 \omega\) and in particular at most the reserved \(6 \omega\). The two other elementary letter families have anchored bounds at most \(u^{- 1 / 2}\) each, where \(u\) is the retimed coupling scale. Since \(u^{- 1 / 2} \leqslant \omega\), their combined bound is at most \(8 \omega\). The background large-field letters retain the decay \(\exp (- \alpha \hspace{0.17em} \text{hot}^2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{shares})\); the separate purchase \(2 a_{\text{coast}} \leqslant \alpha / 4\) pays their forced inflation. With the reserved line multiplier \(L_0\), the integrated cluster bound is therefore purchased through

\(\displaystyle L_0 (e^{8 \omega} - 1) \leqslant \delta_{\text{step}} .\)

This is the local unmarked bound used by the restarted recurrence. The fixed stage constants and Gaussian envelopes in this calculation are those of the neutral compiler at the same physical parameters. The perturbation estimate does not assert derivative bounds for \(E\).

There are now two distinct, compatible uses of the representations. The body identity (14.5) is for \(P\), including \(E\). The unmarked auxiliary alphabet consists of these same corrected body packets \(p_a\), together with the archived background packets of \(P^0\). It does not use the former neutral body packets. Its loading is the same \(\Lambda\) as in (14.5).

The normalization needed here has two precise parts. Write \(Z_j\) for its zero-marker regular components. For every deleted spatial support \(D\) and every coarse configuration \(V\), require the identity

\(\displaystyle \Lambda (V) \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{G}_{Z ; D} (V) = e^{\text{exponent}_Q (V)} \prod_a (1 + p_a^{\text{pay}} (V)) \prod_{b : \hspace{0.17em} X_b^{\text{hole}} \cap D \ne \varnothing} (1 + h_b (V)) .\) (14.6)

Here \(\mathcal{G}_{Z ; D}\) is the compatible gas of the \(Z_j\) whose full supports are disjoint from \(D\). The factors \(p_a^{\text{pay}}\) and \(h_b\) are the fixed boundary and hole corrections of the unmarked regrouping, with their original masks. Secondly, let \(F\) be the unaggregated family of unmarked forced components, before any summation over addresses, and let \(K\) be the family obtained from \(F\) by attaching these same hole and boundary correction factors. The family \(F\) must embed into the forced components of this alphabet, with identical values, supports and inventories, and must exhaust its zero-marker forced components. At each activity address, that is, at each full support and flag inventory, the sum of the members of \(K\) must equal the archived activity of \(Q\). We reserve forced activities of \(Q\) for these aggregated slots. In general a slot of \(Q\) is a sum of several components and is not itself a member of \(F\) or \(K\): an address carrying components with values \(u\) and \(v\) has the single slot \(u + v\). This addresswise identity is required before applying the exclusion regrouping. These are the all-deleted-support identity and the forced-family embedding supplied by the unmarked forest and exclusion construction. They must be established for the corrected \(p_a\) and this actual coarse \(Q\); equality of the two archived fine backgrounds alone does not establish them. For the constructed fixed-coupling output they are proved in Propositions 14.1 and 14.2.

We verify that these two statements, once available, pass to the full marked alphabet. First coalesce background packets with the same address and marker mode. Their zero-marker values are exactly the archived activities at that address, by the addresswise background identity. This embeds the entire unmarked alphabet into the full alphabet, preserving values, full supports, inventories and sector keys. Every remaining elementary insertion has a nonempty marker support. If a full connected component has empty marker support, every elementary factor in it must therefore have empty marker support: its marker support is their union. Hence it comes from the unmarked alphabet. Conversely every unmarked component has the same connectedness and eligibility tests after the embedding. This is a bijection of zero-marker components preserving their full supports. It restricts to the components disjoint from any given \(D\). Thus \(\mathcal{G}_{Z ; D}\) is unchanged, and (14.6) holds for the full alphabet at every deleted support. The same component bijection transports the forced-family embedding and its exhaustion property.

Finally fix a compatible marked forced family and let \(D\) be the union of its full supports. Its residual zero-marker gas is precisely \(\mathcal{G}_{Z ; D}\). Substitute (14.6). A hole factor occurs exactly when its support meets at least one member of that forced family, since \(D\) is their union. Expanding these factors and regrouping by their incident forced components is the weight-preserving exclusion bijection of Subsection 8.31. The resulting polynomial has exponent \(\text{exponent}_Q\) and the actual coarse archived background, by the transported embedding of \(F\) and the addresswise identity for \(K\). Summing over the marked forced families therefore represents \(\mathcal{T}D_P\) with this coarse vacuum normalization. This uses (14.5) for \(P\) and (14.6) for the corrected auxiliary alphabet; it never substitutes \(\mathcal{T}D_{P^0}\) for the marked integral.

This proves the finite algebra and the additive-remainder correction needed by the all-order compiler. Its quantitative marked use still requires the common Gaussian reader estimates and their cumulant approximation, the day and night marker bounds, and the selected spatial support bounds. In particular the cell selection (14.3) must be applied with the same readers and same sector measures as the body expansion, at the purchased slice radius. Those analytic and geometric estimates are separate from the polynomial identities proved here.

14.2Owner energy at the rank threshold

The energy estimate used in the corrected block alphabet concerns the actual fine flag threshold. It also subtracts the affine squares selected by the Gaussian construction. We verify these two changes to the seed estimate explicitly.

Write \(y = r (x)\) for the recording parameter, \(\nu = \text{fineCut} (y)\) and \(\tau = \text{phase} (\beta_F (x))\) for the fine rank threshold. The retiming comparison gives, eventually,

\(\displaystyle 0 < \nu, \quad 0 < \tau, \qquad \nu / 2 \leqslant \tau \leqslant 2 \nu .\) (14.7)

The stores and seed counts remain those of the recorded configuration at \(y\). Only the fine energy rows are read at threshold \(\tau\); the other rows retain their recorded thresholds. In particular, this operation does not replace the stored flags by flags at \(\tau\).

14.2.1Paying the same seeds after changing the fine threshold

Let \(\mathcal{F}\) be the original fine seed inventory and \(r_i\) the fine plaquette chords. For a threshold \(s\), write \(\mathcal{F}_s = \{i \in \mathcal{F}: r_i > s\}\). At any \(s \in [\nu / 2, 2 \nu]\), the argument of Subsection 8.10 gives

\(\displaystyle F_s \geqslant bs \sum_{i \in \mathcal{F}_s} r_i + ds^2 |\mathcal{F} \setminus \mathcal{F}_s |, \qquad b = \lambda_f / 2, \quad d = a_f / 512.\) (14.8)

Here the guide has norm at most \(\epsilon s\), where \(\epsilon = 2 C / L^2\). The factor two comes from \(\nu \leqslant 2 s\). To verify the changed constant, a listed small plaquette has \(r_i \geqslant \nu / 4 \geqslant s / 8\). If \(\epsilon \leqslant \sqrt{a_f} / 16\), then

\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} (\sqrt{a_f} r_i -\|v_i \|)^2 \geqslant a_f s^2 / 512.\)

Impose also \(\epsilon \sqrt{a_f} \leqslant \lambda_f / 4\). The large-plaquette direct bound then remains \((3 \lambda_f / 4) sr_i\). The discarded-path argument of (8.75) uses witnesses with \(s \exp (\text{dist} / R) < r_j\) and the same original fine lattice. The recorded clean-neighbourhood conditions, \(s \geqslant \nu / 2\) and the paid path-range bound place each such witness in \(\mathcal{F}_s\). Thus the discarded rows cost at most \((\lambda_f / 4) s \sum_{\mathcal{F}_s} r_i\), provided \(\epsilon (1 + C_R) C \leqslant \lambda_f / 4\). These two estimates prove (14.8). No continuity in the clipping threshold is used.

The next elementary allocation compares the two large-defect inventories without identifying them. Let \(T \geqslant \nu\) be the very-large threshold, and let \(\mathcal{K}\) be the fine defects spent at that threshold. Put

\(\displaystyle N =\mathcal{F}_s, \quad n_0 = |\mathcal{F} \setminus N|, \quad R = \sum_{i \in N} r_i, \quad V = \sum_{i \in \mathcal{F}_{\nu}} r_i .\)

Then

\(\displaystyle V \leqslant R + 2 \nu n_0, \qquad \nu |N| \leqslant 2 R, \qquad T |\mathcal{K}| \leqslant V.\)

The first inequality bounds every term outside \(N\) by \(s \leqslant 2 \nu\); the second uses \(s \geqslant \nu / 2\); and the last uses \(\mathcal{K} \subseteq \mathcal{F}_{\nu}\) and \(r_i \geqslant T\) on \(\mathcal{K}\). Consequently, if \(m, c \geqslant 0\) and

\(\displaystyle 2 (3 m + c) \leqslant b, \qquad m + 2 (m + c) \leqslant d / 4,\)

then

\(\displaystyle m \nu^2 |\mathcal{F} | + m \nu T| \mathcal{K}| + c \nu V \leqslant F_s .\) (14.9)

Indeed the left side is at most \((3 m + c) \nu R + (3 m + 2 c) \nu^2 n_0\). The displayed conditions and \(\nu \leqslant 2 s\) bound these terms by \(bsR\) and \(ds^2 n_0\), respectively.

All predictor and coarse rows are unchanged. Apply their reserves and the hit/unhit comparisons from Sections 8.10, 8.6 and 8.9, using (14.9) for the fine reserve. One can choose

\(\displaystyle 0 < m \leqslant \min \{p / 32, a_c / 64, \lambda_f / 32, d / 32\}\)

before increasing \(L\) until \(c \leqslant m\) and the guide conditions above hold. Choose the predictor width afterward, so that its linear expense is at most \(pW / 16\). The two final smallness inequalities (8.84), now with threshold \(\nu\), hold eventually. The same cancellation as in the seed estimate proves

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{E}_{\tau} (O) \geqslant b_0 \nu^2 \hspace{0.17em} s (O), \qquad b_0 = m / 2 > 0.\) (14.10)

Here \(s (O)\) is the unchanged number of seed shares of the owner store \(O\), and \(\mathcal{E}_{\tau} (O)\) is its assigned energy with only the fine rows replaced by their \(\tau\) versions. The finite-volume, tree-slice and local seed hypotheses are those of the cited seed estimates. Their guide, geometry and comparison bounds are used on this same store and field.

14.2.2Subtracting the selected affine squares

Put \(B = \lceil (\log y)^2 \rceil\). Let \(Q\) be an annular window around \(O\), and \(H\) a subwindow selecting fine and predictor rows of \(Q\). Use the actual logarithmic signal in the local Gaussian coordinates, and denote by \(\mathcal{X}_H\) the sum of its selected affine squares. Subsection 8.11 proves that these are the actual rows, with the same forest, tag and coarse field, and that

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W} (Q) -\mathcal{X}_H \geqslant - e (y) s (O), \qquad e (y) \geqslant 0, \quad y \hspace{0.17em} e (y) \longrightarrow 0.\) (14.11)

Here \(e\) is a function of the recording parameter, evaluated at \(y = r (x)\) in this application. Since \(r (x) \to \infty\), its eventual estimates apply for all sufficiently large \(x\). In fact its local error has upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\), and the number of annular rows per share is bounded by a fixed polynomial in \(B\). This gives the asserted decay and also supplies the local chart representation of every selected row.

The same bound applies when the fine rows of \(Q\) are read at \(\tau\). To see why, the annulus has fine chord at most \(\nu / 2 \leqslant \tau\). The only possible obstruction is the exponential path-kept test. Its nontrivial range is contained in radius \(B / 50\) by the purchased path-counting bound; in that range the clean-annulus condition gives chord at most \(\nu / 2\). Outside it the right side of the test is at least the universal chord bound \(2\). Thus every relevant path is kept at both thresholds \(\nu\) and \(\tau\). Both fine rows have exactly the same quadratic formula there. The other rows never changed. This proves equality of the two annular energies, including at threshold equality.

Since \(\nu^2 \geqslant \beta_F (y)^{- 1}\) and \(\beta_F (y) / y \to 1\), (14.11) implies \(e (y) \leqslant (b_0 / 2) \nu^2\) eventually. Adding (14.10) therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{E}_{\tau} (O) +\mathcal{W}_{\tau} (Q) -\mathcal{X}_H \geqslant \frac{b_0}{2} \nu^2 s (O) \geqslant \frac{b_0}{8} \tau^2 s (O) .\) (14.12)

This estimate holds for every admitted selected window and actual chart signal, uniformly in the finite period. The threshold is chosen after its fixed window and chart constants.

14.2.3The harvested ceiling of an actual packet

We apply (14.12) to the ceiling used in the block exponent. Fix the full store \(A\), an owner packet \(O\), and separation radius \(R \geqslant 205 B\). Assume the packet ownership conditions, the actual seed conditions on \((U, V)\), positive frame likelihood, and nonzero chart interfaces. Assume also \(\text{span} \leqslant B / 50\), the coarse side at least \(100 (1 + B)\), and the purchased local chart comparison with width \(w\). The chart radius is \(w \nu\), is positive and is smaller than the first logarithmic radius. These hypotheses hold before the fine field or its coordinates are varied.

Let \(\mathcal{S}\) be the set of signed permutations of the four lattice axes: a permutation and an independent choice of reflection for each axis. Put \(\omega_{\mathcal{S}} = 1 / |\mathcal{S}|\) and write \(\text{Av}_{\sigma} \hspace{0.17em} F (\sigma) = \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \sum_{\sigma \in \mathcal{S}} F (\sigma)\). For each \(\sigma\), turn the owner and full store, the fine and coarse fields, the forest and the tag together. Let \(Q_{\sigma}\) be the annulus between radii \(2 B\) and \(200 B\) of the turned owner. Let \(H_{\sigma}\) consist of the actual Gaussian rows selected from this annulus. The span bound keeps each selected row outside the \(2 B\) core. The packet separation \(R \geqslant 205 B\) keeps the annulus away from other owners. Thus \(H_{\sigma}\) selects a subwindow of \(Q_{\sigma}\) satisfying exactly the annular hypotheses used above.

The signal in those rows is the actual signed coordinate array, not an independently chosen logarithm. On tree bonds both its increment and the fine field are the identity. On frozen bonds, the clean chart has norm at most \(K \nu\) with \(1 \leqslant K \leqslant w\); the cutoff logarithm agrees with that chart because it lies within the chosen radius. On active bonds the nonzero interfaces bound the actual coordinate by \(2 w \nu\). Therefore this same array reconstructs the turned fine field and has norm at most \(2 w \nu\) throughout every selected stencil. The chart argument in Subsection 8.11 applies with width \(2 w\). It proves the hypotheses of (14.12) for every \(H_{\sigma}\).

Let \(\mathcal{C}_{200 B} (O)\) be the oriented elementary energy addresses whose coarse anchors lie within distance \(200 B\) of \(O\). The definition of the actual ceiling charge gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle \ \begin{array}{l} \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{C}_{200 B} (O)} \text{levy}_j = \text{Av}_{\sigma} [\mathcal{W}_{\tau} (\text{anchor}_{200 B} (\sigma O)) -\mathcal{X}_{H_{\sigma}} (a_{\sigma}) +\mathcal{C}_{\sigma} (m)] . \end{array}\) (14.13)

Here \(a_{\sigma}\) is the actual array just described, \(m\) is the localized Gaussian mean, and \(\mathcal{C}_{\sigma} (m)\) is the sum of its charged fine and predictor squares. It is nonnegative. Each address on the left already includes its signed orientation, so the single factor \(\omega_{\mathcal{S}}\) normalizes the sum over those orientations.

For completeness, the identity is a finite reindexing. Partition the oriented energy addresses into fine faces, predictor bonds, coarse faces and normalization rows. Their anchored sums are the corresponding four terms of \(\mathcal{W}_{\tau}\). At a selected fine or predictor address the charged coordinate square equals the extracted affine square of the signed array. This uses the actual path bound and authorized predictor branch. At an unselected address both subtracted terms are zero. The mean squares remain with the positive sign in the definition of the levy. Summing these identities yields (14.13).

Finally the anchored energy partitions exactly into its core and annulus:

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}_{\tau} (\text{anchor}_{200 B} (\sigma O)) =\mathcal{E}_{\tau} (\sigma O) +\mathcal{W}_{\tau} (Q_{\sigma}) .\)

This is the finite disjoint partition of each of the four row inventories; the changed fine threshold is used on both sides. Apply (14.12), drop the nonnegative mean squares, and use \(s (\sigma O) = s (O)\). Averaging proves

\(\displaystyle \frac{b_0}{8} \tau^2 s (O) \leqslant \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{C}_{200 B} (O)} \text{levy}_j .\) (14.14)

This is the harvested owner-energy estimate required in Subsection 14.3. It holds for all actual compatible packets, forests, tags and coordinates with the stated seed, likelihood and interface conditions. The uniform chart, branch and seed estimates used to obtain those conditions remain the previously stated construction inputs; no assertion about the separate all-compatible warning factor is made by this energy calculation.

14.3The corrected unmarked alphabet on the actual block

The marked expansion needs one unmarked alphabet chosen before the number and locations of the insertions. We construct that alphabet from the actual block exponent. Its reconstruction identity concerns the original fine density. Its smallness and normalization estimates also use the representation with the additive remainder removed. Keeping these two uses on the same alphabet is essential.

14.3.1The fixed data and the remaining analytic inputs

Fix one step with fine and coarse tori, its actual path pair, clips and conditional Gaussian construction. Write \(P\) for the fine representation, \(\beta > 0\) for its coupling, \(\theta\) for its flag threshold, and \(J\) for the local Haar chart ratio. Fix a reference forest \(F_0\) and tag \(k_0\). Use the signed orientation set \(\mathcal{S}\) and its normalization \(\omega_{\mathcal{S}} = 1 / |\mathcal{S}|\) from Subsubsection 14.2.3. It is separate from the normalized tag average. Put \(y = r (x)\) for the recording parameter, \(B = \lceil (\log y)^2 \rceil\), and \(z = \beta_F (x)\) for the adjacent fine parameter. All the objects below use this one choice of data. In particular, the fine path and clip in the Gaussian construction are exactly those of \(P\).

The following hypotheses specify the analytic interface of this section. The actual Gaussian data have the previously established positive floors and local decompositions; their two determinant series and inverse correction have the purchased support bounds. The Gaussian coordinate and linear-residual bounds on the chart mask are respectively \(y^2\) and \(y^{- 4}\). The coarse budget radius \(B_c\) and fine radius \(B_f\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle 25 B + 30 \leqslant B_c \leqslant B^2, \qquad B_f \leqslant 2 B_c .\)

The inherited activity estimate has its positive support-exponent reserve, the actual path-counting bound, and

\(\displaystyle \Gamma \leqslant \beta_F (y)^{1 / 8}, \qquad \delta \leqslant y^{- 1 / 2}, \qquad \beta \leqslant 4 y, \qquad \theta \leqslant 2 \hspace{0.17em} \text{fineCut} (y) .\)

The fine coupling and threshold also obey \(z / 2 \leqslant \beta \leqslant 2 z\) and \(\theta = z^{- 1 / 2} \text{hot} (z)\). The coarse budget threshold is exactly \(\text{fieldCut} (y)\), the fine threshold satisfies \(\theta \geqslant \text{fineCut} (y) / 2\), and the profile width \(W_{\text{prof}}\) is positive. The chart radius is a fixed nonnegative multiple of \(\text{fineCut} (y)\), and its cutoff and logarithmic radius conditions hold. These are actual data conditions, uniform in period, forest and tag; they cannot be supplied by choosing an unrelated representation with the same nominal bounds.

Two further inputs must be distinguished. The owner-energy estimate bounds the harvested ceiling charge for the actual seeded packet whenever its chart interfaces are nonzero. The warning estimate is uniform over all compatible field/store pairs: it bounds the actual Gaussian palette and makes the warning factor vanish under the stated local field and Gaussian bounds. A comparison on posed reader patches alone is insufficient for this second assertion. Their exact applications to the harvested packets and warning factors remain prerequisites of the present construction.

14.3.2A local decomposition of the real exponent

An admissible configuration of the local calculation consists of a store, forest, tag, coarse field, frozen links and Gaussian coordinates. Denote it by \(\rho\). Compatibility means that the stored coarse flags agree with those of the coarse field. Let \(\mathcal{A} (\rho)\) be the event that the product of the chart, profile, likelihood and wire masks is nonzero. Its factors are the actual factors in the block integral, including the finite tag count in the site and bond factors. Precisely,

\(\displaystyle \prod_j \text{mask}_j = \text{profile} \cdot \# \text{Choices} \cdot \text{likelihood} \cdot \text{interfaces} .\)

This tag multiplicity is present in the pointwise sliced identity; it cancels the denominator of the normalized tag average when the block integral is reconstructed.

Let \(\mathcal{O} (\rho)\) be the finite collection of owners of the seeded components. The real exponent remaining after extraction of the scalar Gaussian normalization decomposes as

\(\displaystyle E (\rho) = \sum_{i \in \mathcal{O} (\rho)} (c_i (\rho) + r_i (\rho)) + T (\rho) .\) (14.15)

Here \(c_i\) is minus \(\beta\) times the actual ceiling charge assigned to owner \(i\), and \(r_i\) is its allocated local determinant and frozen-coordinate normalization. The remaining term is the sum of eight specified contributions: exterior ceiling charges, the linear residual, the quadratic inverse correction, exterior local determinants, the relative logarithmic determinant series, its inverse-correction logarithmic series, the local Haar logarithm, and the inherited fine/coarse remainder. This identity is obtained by partitioning the ceiling charges into owner ceilings and their complement, partitioning the local determinants in the same way, and retaining both convergent determinant series. The linear and Haar terms are sums over the active coordinates; the inverse correction is its absolutely convergent path expansion. These operations are justified by the bounds below and the determinant identities of Subsection 8.29.

For clarity, a local bound at radius \(B_c\) and exponent \(e \geqslant 0\) means nonnegative numbers \(b_j\) and nonempty supports \(X_j\) with

\(\displaystyle \sup_v \sum_{j : v \in X_j} e^{e \hspace{0.17em} \text{cov}_{B_c} (X_j)} b_j \leqslant t, \qquad |L_j (\rho) | \leqslant b_j \quad \text{on } \mathcal{A}.\) (14.16)

The functions \(L_j\) depend measurably only on the indicated local data. The cover number is the one used for the inherited activity bounds. Set

\(\displaystyle q = y^{- 1 / 8}, \qquad \nu = y^{- 5}, \qquad a_y = y^{- 1 / 24} .\)

The actual decomposition has the following bounds:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{l|c} \text{contribution to } T & \text{local bound}\\ \hline \text{exterior ceiling charges} & q\\ \text{linear residual} & q\\ \text{quadratic inverse correction} & y^4 \nu\\ \text{exterior local determinants} & q\\ \text{relative logarithmic determinant series} & \nu\\ \text{inverse-correction logarithmic series} & \nu\\ \text{local Haar logarithm} & q\\ \text{inherited fine/coarse remainder} & a_y \end{array}\)

We spell out the conversion of the earlier estimates into this table. Each exterior ceiling term is at most \(4 \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \hspace{0.17em} y^{- 1 / 4}\) on \(\mathcal{A}\). Its support lies in a coarse halo of radius \(420 B\) at its assigned address. The bounded address density and halo volume cost only a fixed polynomial in \(B\); the cover number at radius \(B\) is bounded by a fixed constant. A polynomial in \(B\) times \(y^{- 1 / 4}\) is eventually at most \(y^{- 1 / 8}\). This proves the first line. For the linear term, the product of a residual coordinate bounded by \(y^{- 4}\) and a Gaussian coordinate bounded by \(y^2\) is at most \(y^{- 2}\). Sum over the fixed color dimension and use the same halo calculation to obtain the second line. The quadratic term has two such Gaussian factors, so the inverse-correction path bound is multiplied by at most \(y^4\). The factor \(1 / 2\) in the quadratic form only improves the bound.

Outside the owner neighbourhood of radius \(200 B\) there is no frozen-coordinate charge. The actual local determinant estimate there is at most \(y^{- 1 / 4}\), and its radius-\(420 B\) support gives the fourth line by the first calculation. The two remaining determinant series have their actual word supports and bounds \(\exp (- dB)\) with fixed positive decay rates \(d\). Since \(B \geqslant (\log y)^2\), each is eventually at most \(y^{- 5}\): it suffices that \(d \log y \geqslant 5\). This gives the next two lines without discarding either series.

The extended Haar logarithm satisfies \(| \log J_{\text{ext}} (u) | \leqslant C \min (\|u\|, 4 \hspace{0.17em} \text{arc})^2\). The chart radius assumption and \(\text{fineCut} (y)^2 \leqslant y^{- 1 / 4}\) therefore give another \(Cy^{- 1 / 4}\) bound on the same local halo, proving the seventh line. This estimate remains valid for the extended chart, so no unproved restriction on \(u\) is inserted.

Finally apply Subsection 8.24 with the actual positive reserve and \(B_f \leqslant 2 B_c\). The inherited label term costs at most a fixed multiple of \(B^8 \delta\), which is at most \(y^{- 1 / 8}\) eventually. The two fine/coarse tail terms each cost \(kl \hspace{0.17em} \beta_F (y)^{- 1}\), and the quadratic, cubic and quartic path terms each cost \(k \beta_F (y)^{- 1 / 12}\). Here \(k = |\mathcal{S}|\) and \(l\) is the number of plaquette axes. The path coefficients have the required smallness because

\(\displaystyle \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \theta^2 \Gamma, \quad \beta \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \theta^3 C, \quad \beta \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \theta^4 C \leqslant \beta_F (y)^{- 1 / 8}\)

eventually, with the fixed path bound \(C\) and the hypotheses above. This is the specialization to the quadratic, cubic and quartic terms of the inherited estimate. Since \(\beta_F (y) \geqslant y\), its total bound is at most \((1 + 2 kl + 3 k) y^{- 1 / 12} \leqslant y^{- 1 / 24}\) eventually. Increasing the support radius from \(B\) to \(B_c\) decreases its cover number and preserves every preceding bound.

Thus \(T\) has total bound

\(\displaystyle t_y = q + q + y^4 \nu + (q + (\nu + \nu)) + q + a_y .\) (14.17)

For \(y \geqslant 1\), \(\nu \leqslant y^4 \nu \leqslant q \leqslant a_y\), whence \(2 t_y \leqslant 16 a_y\). Eventually \(16 y^{- 1 / 24} \leqslant y^{- 1 / 48}\). In particular \(t_y \leqslant 1\) and \(2 t_y \leqslant y^{- 1 / 48}\).

14.3.3Bounded extension and averaging

Let \(s_i\) be the number of seed shares belonging to owner \(i\). Choose \(\alpha > 0\) with \(2 \alpha \leqslant a / 2\), where \(a > 0\) is the owner-energy constant. That estimate and the local normalization bound give, on \(\mathcal{A}\) for occurring owners,

\(\displaystyle c_i \leqslant - \beta a \theta^2 s_i, \qquad |r_i | \leqslant \alpha \hspace{0.17em} \text{hot} (z)^2 s_i .\)

Indeed the normalization is bounded by \(C (1 + B)^4 \log (y) \hspace{0.17em} s_i\) with a fixed constant \(C\). The scale payment makes this at most \(\alpha \hspace{0.17em} \text{hot} (z)^2 s_i\) eventually. Since \(z \theta^2 = \text{hot} (z)^2\) and \(\beta \geqslant z / 2\),

\(\displaystyle 2 \alpha \hspace{0.17em} \text{hot} (z)^2 \leqslant \beta a \theta^2 .\)

Consequently \(c_i + r_i \leqslant - A_i\), where \(A_i = \alpha \hspace{0.17em} \text{hot} (z)^2 s_i\). The finite-volume lower estimate for the same ceiling charges also gives \(c_i \geqslant u\) with finite \(u\), uniformly over the compatible data on \(\mathcal{A}\). It need not be uniform in the volume. Set

\(\displaystyle D_i = \min (- A_i, u - A_i), \qquad b_i (\rho) = \max \{D_i, \min \{- A_i, c_i (\rho) + r_i (\rho)\}\} .\)

Then \(D_i \leqslant b_i \leqslant - A_i\) everywhere on compatible data, and \(b_i = c_i + r_i\) for occurring owners on \(\mathcal{A}\). The clamping operation is continuous, preserves locality and makes \(b_i\) bounded. This extension permits averaging before the nonzero-mask event is imposed.

Similarly replace every local summand \(L_j\) of \(T\) by its clamp to \([- b_j, b_j]\), using the bounds in (14.16). This changes no value on \(\mathcal{A}\). Coalesce terms with the same nonempty support. The sums converge absolutely: summing the site bounds over the finite torus bounds \(\sum_j b_j\), because each support contains a site and \(e \geqslant 0\). The grouped local terms \(h_X\) retain the same site bound and sum to \(T\) on \(\mathcal{A}\).

Average both the owner functions and the grouped terms over the finite signed lattice motions and the product of local Haar gauge measures. Transport supports, owners, stores, forests and tags together. The actual exponent and the nonzero-mask event are invariant under this common action. Thus averaging (14.15) gives local invariant functions \(\bar{b}_i, \bar{h}_X\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle E = \sum_{i \in \mathcal{O}} \bar{b}_i + \sum_X \bar{h}_X \quad \text{on } \mathcal{A}, \qquad \bar{b}_i \leqslant - \alpha \hspace{0.17em} \text{hot} (z)^2 s_i .\) (14.18)

Boundedness makes all these averages integrable. The action preserves seed shares and the cover number, so the averaged majorants \(Y_X\) satisfy the same site bound \(t_y\). For any site of \(X\), that bound gives \(Y_X \leqslant t_y \leqslant 1\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle |e^{\bar{h}_X} - 1| \leqslant 2 | \bar{h}_X | \leqslant 2 Y_X .\)

The auxiliary letters \(e^{\bar{h}_X} - 1\) have total local bound at most \(2 t_y \leqslant y^{- 1 / 48}\). Together with the negative owner functions, these are the corrected unmarked alphabet. This is a value estimate; it does not assert arbitrary differentiated estimates merely from bounded extension.

14.3.4The scalar normalization and the genuine density

Write \(K_0\) for the bare kinetic matrix at the fixed reference forest \(F_0\) and tag \(k_0\), \(N_f\) for the number of fine sites, and \(n_0\) for the number of free reference bonds. The Lie algebra dimension is already included in the factor \(\text{spare}\). The scalar extracted from every actual sector is

\(\displaystyle \Lambda (V) = \text{bank} (V) \hspace{0.17em} \text{pref} (\beta) e^{P.vN_f} \hspace{0.17em} \text{spare} (J, \beta)^{n_0} (\det K_0)^{- 1 / 2} > 0.\) (14.19)

All factors are positive, including the determinant by the bare kinetic floor. The forest determinant and free-bond count identities of Subsection 8.29 identify this same scalar for every actual forest and tag. No new scalar is selected after a marked family is specified.

Here is the exponent check. Let \(Q\) be the actual covariance, \(W\) its Gram precision, \(m\) its localized mean, \(Z\) its Gaussian coordinate and \(g = \beta^{- 1 / 2}\). Let \(U\) be the fine field constructed from \(m + gZ\) and the frozen links. Denote the quadratic guide by \(G_0\), the fine cut action by \(R_f\), the coarse quadratic action by \(e_0\), and the prediction mismatch cost by \(M_0\). The charge decomposition and quadratic completion give respectively

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \omega_{\mathcal{S}} \sum_j \text{levy}_j & = M_0 + (R_f - e_0) - G_0 (m + gZ) + G_0 (m),\\ - \beta G_0 (m) + \eta \cdot Z + \tfrac{1}{2} Z^{\top} (Q^{- 1} - W) Z & = \tfrac{1}{2} \text{force}_Q (Z) - \beta G_0 (m + gZ) . \end{array}\)

The inherited remainder satisfies \(P.vN_f + \text{apart} (U) = P. \text{exponent} (U) + \beta R_f\). The bank identity is \(e^{- \beta e_0} = \text{bank} (V) e^{\text{tail}_c (V)}\). Substitution into the definition of \(E\) cancels \(R_f\) and both guide terms and yields

\(\displaystyle E = E_{\det} + \text{tail}_c + \tfrac{1}{2} \text{force}_Q (Z) + \log J_{\text{ext}} + \beta e_0 - \beta M_0 + P. \text{exponent} (U) - P.vN_f .\)

Here \(E_{\det}\) includes the frozen-coordinate logarithm and both determinant logarithms. Precisely,

\(\displaystyle J. \text{scale}^{\# \text{Active}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{chartCost} (Q, \beta^{- 1 / 2}) = \text{spare} (J, \beta)^{n_0} (\det K_0)^{- 1 / 2} e^{E_{\det}},\)

by (8.216)–(8.218). Here \(\# \text{Active}\) also counts bonds. The prediction noise is \(\text{pref} (\beta) e^{- \beta M_0}\), and the product of extended Haar factors is \(e^{\log J_{\text{ext}}}\). Multiplying these identities proves the pointwise identity

\(\displaystyle \text{freight} (\rho) = \Lambda (V) e^{E (\rho)},\) (14.20)

where freight is the original fine exponential times the prediction noise, chart Jacobian, Gaussian density conversion and extended Haar factors. Thus the exponential here is that of the genuine \(P\).

14.3.5Flags, connected components and marked reuse

Expand each auxiliary exponential as \(1 + (e^{\bar{h}_X} - 1)\). Outside the owner regions the warning letter is the corresponding mask minus one; hence its factor \(1 + \text{warning}\) is exactly that mask. Within an owner region, the owner letter already includes the product of these masks. Owner regions are disjoint. Multiplying the factors therefore recovers \(e^{\sum \bar{b}_i + \sum \bar{h}_X}\) times the product of all actual masks.

There is also no multiplicity from decorating owners with fine flags. Each owner decoration is required to equal the fine flags in its assigned region, so exactly one decoration survives for each owner. On \(\mathcal{A}\), the warning/interface implication places every actual fine flag in an owner region. The union of these unique decorations is therefore exactly the actual fine flag set. Grouping overlapping letters into connected components gives a bijection between compatible component collections and the underlying letter choices, as in Subsection 14.1. It preserves both flag unions and the stored seed data. Consequently the sum with fine flag inventory \(u\) is

\(\displaystyle \boldsymbol{1}_{\{ \text{flags}_{\theta} (U) = u\}} e^{E (\rho)} \prod_j \text{mask}_j (\rho) .\)

Outside \(\mathcal{A}\) both sides vanish because the full mask product is zero; no division by a mask is used. Combining this identity with (14.20) proves the genuine body identity (14.5) for this corrected alphabet and the scalar (14.19).

For the same alphabet, apply the real additive correction and masked logarithm construction of Subsection 13.12. The required warning and product bounds, support reserve and signed/gauge transport must be those of the alphabet just constructed. Here is the deleted-support calculation on that alphabet.

Proposition 14.1. (Deleting supports from the corrected regular gas) Assume the absolute regular-cluster estimate used in (13.39) on the corrected alphabet, and use its coarse exponent and boundary correction. Then (14.6) holds at every coarse configuration and for every deleted spatial support \(D\). The hole factors are \(h_b = \exp (- c_b) - 1\), where \(c_b\) is the masked regular logarithmic entry with full support \(X_b^{\text{hole}}\) in the corrected alphabet.

Proof. Fix the coarse configuration. The unrestricted regular gas is \(\exp (\sum_b c_b)\), with \(c_b = - \ell_b\) in the convention of (13.39). Delete every elementary regular packet whose support meets \(D\). The logarithmic term for a connected tuple survives exactly when its union support avoids \(D\). The same absolute majorant applies to the restricted series, and therefore

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{G}_{Z ; D} =\mathcal{G}_{Z ; \varnothing} \exp \left( - \sum_{b : \hspace{0.17em} X_b^{\text{hole}} \cap D \ne \varnothing} c_b \right) .\)

The unpunctured factor \(\Lambda \mathcal{G}_{Z ; \varnothing}\) is the exponential of the coarse exponent times the product of the boundary factors: the scalar prefactor contributes its logarithm per coarse site, the regular sum contributes \(\sum_b c_b\), and the boundary factors retain the residual action correction with its original masks. This is the normalization of Subsection 13.12 on the corrected alphabet; when the full action correction is absorbed into the coarse exponent the boundary product is empty. Expanding the displayed exponential as the product of \(1 + h_b\) gives exactly (14.6). Absolute summability of \(c_b\) implies that of \(h_b\) by \(|e^{- c_b} - 1| \leqslant e^{|c_b |} |c_b |\). No quotient by a masked packet or by a forced activity is used.\(\Box\)

We next specify the forced activities of the output; the scalar and regular family alone would not specify them.

Proposition 14.2. (The forced output on the corrected alphabet) Use the corrected body packets above and the archived background of \(P^0\). Choose the coarse scalar and additive family by Subsection 13.12, and choose its forced activities by the construction below. Then, for every finite marker family, the unaggregated component family \(F\) of the proof satisfies the embedding and exhaustion requirements after (14.6), and its attached family \(K\) has the addresswise sums of this output \(Q\), as required there. Its scalar density is the pushforward of the actual input \(P\), including its additive remainder.

Proof. First use an empty marker family. Let \(F\) be the unaggregated family of forced components of the corrected unmarked alphabet. Attach the hole factors of Proposition 14.1 to these components, then attach the boundary factors, grouping at each step by the same support-intersection relation used in forced exclusion. Denote the resulting family by \(K\). A label retains its full support \(X\) and its flag inventory \(I \subseteq X\), not merely its value. All its values are real: the original unmarked integrands are real, integration and the forest average preserve reality, and so do the real hole and boundary factors.

For every address \((X, I)\) define

\(\displaystyle k_{X, I} (V) = \sum_{j : \hspace{0.17em} (X_j, I_j) = (X, I)} K_j (V) .\)

These sums have the component majorants already purchased for the corrected alphabet. Store \(k_{X, I}\) in one slot of the countable forced family at that address and fill the other slots by zero. Keep the scalar, coupling, path action and additive family unchanged. This defines \(Q\). In particular, its archived activity at \((X, I)\) is exactly the displayed sum. Locality, gauge invariance and the eligibility test are preserved because every summand has this same address; the triangle inequality preserves the purchased bound. No old, unrelated coarse forced activity is retained by this assignment.

Now choose any finite marker family. The unmarked elementary alphabet embeds by retaining every corrected body label and taking the zero-marker copy of each archived fine background label. Values, supports, inventories and sector data are unchanged. The union of marker sets is empty only if every constituent marker set is empty. Consequently this embedding exhausts the zero-marker components of the full alphabet. This argument also survives each attachment step: map the finite set of constituents by the embedding and leave hole and boundary labels fixed. Support intersections, compatibility and products are preserved. Conversely a component with empty marker set contains only labels in that image, so taking their unique preimages recovers the component. The two maps are inverse. Thus the attached zero-marker family is exactly \(K\), reindexed on the new marker stencil, and summing at each address proves its background identity with \(Q\). The embedding and exhaustion concern the components \(F\) and \(K\), not the aggregated slots \(k_{X, I}\) of \(Q\), which enter only through these addresswise sums.

For completeness, a boundary factor whose support meets no forced component contributes one. Exact flag coverage puts every flag in a forced inventory; such a boundary support is therefore healthy, where its correction vanishes. This justifies using the same boundary product before and after component attachment, including on flagged configurations. The regular deleted-support formula and this exact forced expansion now reconstruct the scalar density. With no markers, the body identity integrates \(P = e^{- E} P^0\), rather than \(P^0\). Reassembly followed by the two component bijections therefore gives \(Q =\mathcal{K}_{\beta} P\). All rearrangements use the purchased absolute majorants. None divides by a density or a forced activity.\(\Box\)

The output here is the representation constructed from this alphabet; the conclusion does not identify an independently prescribed coarse representation merely because its parameters agree. Only then introduce an arbitrary finite family of marked sites and its parent map. The marked decorations vary with that family; the body alphabet, genuine body identity and scalar do not. Decreasing the already purchased exponents and multipliers gives both uses in Subsection 14.4. The separate harvested-energy and all-compatible-warning prerequisites at the start remain necessary for this application.

14.4The mixed marked compiler on the restarted history

We give the bound calculation which turns the actual three-alphabet expansion of Subsection 14.1 into the upper and lower marked transitions. All marked integrals use the genuine fine representation \(P\). Its auxiliary representation \(P^0\) has only its additive remainder removed. The body packets in the two calculations are nevertheless the same corrected packets. This distinction is necessary for the normalization argument below.

14.4.1Support currency and the three letter families

Fix a fine-to-coarse block map of positive integer side \(L\), fine and coarse budget radii \(B_f, B_c\) that are positive integers, and a finite marker set \(W\). Let \(\theta : W \to \Lambda_f\) and \(u : W \to \Lambda_c\) satisfy \(u = \text{parent} \circ \theta\). Use the cover charge

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{D}_B (t, X) = \exp (t \hspace{0.17em} \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X), \qquad v = e + 5, \quad e \geqslant 0,\)

where \(\text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X\) is the minimum number of radius-\(B\) cubes covering \(X\) whose union is connected. Write \(X^{\uparrow}\) for the prescribed coarse neighborhood of the parent image of \(X\). The radius comparison and cover estimate proved in Subsection 8.24 give, when \(B_f \leqslant 2 B_c\),

\(\displaystyle L \text{cov}_{B_c} (X^{\uparrow}) \leqslant 15^4 \left( L + 10 \text{cov}_{B_f} \hspace{0.17em} X \right) .\)

Consequently, if \(10 \cdot 15^4 (v + 2) \leqslant L \sigma_f\), then

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{D}_{B_c} (v + 2, X^{\uparrow}) \leqslant T_e \mathcal{D}_{B_f} (\sigma_f, X), \qquad T_e = \exp (15^4 (v + 2)) .\) (14.21)

Indeed multiply the cover inequality by \((v + 2) / L\) and exponentiate. This pays the additive covering cost before taking any marker sum.

All nonnegative sum estimates below are first proved on finite subfamilies and then extended by monotone convergence. There are three letter families in one fixed sector and tag: body letters with empty marker support, lifted background packets, and nonempty-marker insertion letters. Coalesce background packets only when their complete activity address and marker support agree. Their zero-marker coefficients must reproduce the fine archived activity addresswise. Let \(z_a \geqslant 0\) be the body bounds. For a background packet with fine support \(X_b\) and paid bound \(A_b\), use

\(\displaystyle f_b = A_b /\mathcal{D}_{B_f} (\sigma_f, X_b) .\)

The input global packet bound is valid on every fine field, so evaluating it on the actual chart gives the pointwise bound \(f_b\), including on unhealthy fields. For a regular insertion the analogous estimate holds on its healthy support; its original mask makes its value zero elsewhere. Division by the positive cover charge is therefore valid in both cases. Positive marker weights are included in these bounds throughout.

Assume the background site sums obey \(\sum_{b : x \in X_b} A_b \leqslant \eta\). Equation 14.21 implies, for each marker \(w\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_{b : w \in F_b} f_b \mathcal{D}_{B_c} (v + 2, X_b^{\uparrow}) \leqslant T_e \eta .\) (14.22)

Here \(\theta (w) \in X_b\) whenever \(w \in F_b\). There is no factor \(L^4\) in this marker estimate.

The corresponding coarse site estimate does have that factor. Choose one genuine fine site in the nonempty supported inventory of every background packet. At a fixed coarse site at most \(L^4\) fine sites have that parent. The anchor-to-site estimate loses one cover exponent and has cost

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W} (B) = (33 B)^4 \sum_{j \geqslant 0} (j + 1)^4 e^{- j} .\)

For completeness, for an anchor \(a \in X\) and \(x \in X\) the cover number is at least \(\lfloor \text{dist} (a, x) / (8 B) \rfloor\). The number of anchors in its \(j\)th distance shell is at most \((33 B)^4 (j + 1)^4\). Summing the exponential loss proves this period-independent constant. Thus the background site bound at exponent \(v + 1\) is at most \(\mathcal{W} (B_c) L^4 T_e \eta\).

Let \(w_h \geqslant 0\) be the insertion-letter bounds, and suppose their marker-root sums at exponent \(v + 2\) are at most \(a\). If every coarse site receives at most \(g\) markers, choose one marker in each letter. The same argument gives site bound \(\mathcal{W} (B_c) ga\) at exponent \(v + 1\). On the actual replicated reader geometry \(g = n\), the number of reader labels: the parent cell is fixed by a coarse site, and only its \(n\) labels remain. Combining these bounds with a body site bound \(\delta\) gives

\(\displaystyle \Sigma = \delta +\mathcal{W} (B_c) L^4 T_e \eta +\mathcal{W} (B_c) ga.\) (14.23)

The total marker-root bound at exponent \(v + 1\) is at most \(T_e \eta + a\). Empty-marker body letters do not contribute to it.

14.4.2Payment of each connected integral

Fix multipliers \(m, l \geqslant 1\). The body-letter product estimate on the actual sector measure supplies the following stronger integral bound: if \(I\) is a collection of body letters and \(X_I\) is the product of their nonnegative absolute weights, then

\(\displaystyle M_I \int X_I \hspace{0.17em} d \nu \leqslant \prod_{a \in I} z_a .\)

Here \(M_I\) is the product of their individual inflations: a letter with nonempty forced inventory contributes \(m\), and any remaining body letter contributes \(l\). These are the paid unmarked product estimates, with the same chart and corrected body alphabet as above. The measure \(\nu\) is the Gaussian/Haar probability measure of that sector and tag. Each other letter has its pointwise bound. In addition, every insertion letter designated as a remainder satisfies one of

\(\displaystyle lh (\xi) \leqslant w_h \quad \text{for all } \xi, \qquad \text{or} \qquad h = 0 \text{ off } E_h, \quad l \nu (E_h) \leqslant 1,\) (14.24)

where \(0 \leqslant h \leqslant w_h\) and \(E_h\) is measurable.

Let \(C\) be a compatible connected collection of letters. Its full integrated coefficient, including its marker monomial, is denoted \(b_C\). Then

\(\displaystyle t_C \|b_C \|_r \leqslant \prod_{j \in C} b_j, \quad t_C = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} m, & C \text{ has nonempty forced inventory},\\ l, & C \text{ is unforced but is not entirely leading},\\ 1, & C \text{ is entirely leading} . \end{array} \right.\) (14.25)

To prove this, split its product into body, background and insertion factors. The last two products are bounded pointwise by their bounds. If the body set is nonempty, its inflation \(M_I\) is at least \(t_C\). The connection relation joins letters with intersecting full supports. If a site in a background inventory is supplied by a body inventory, both supports contain that site, so the two letters lie in the same component. Hence the inventory identity restricts to each component. In the forced case this uses the exact inventory identity: a forced site must belong to one of the body inventories. In the unforced nonleading case any body letter supplies \(l\). The paid body integral therefore proves the estimate.

If the body set is empty, compatibility forces the background set to be empty as well. A background packet has a nonempty fine inventory, whereas compatibility identifies the union of those inventories with the union supplied by the body letters. The component is now a product of insertion letters. If all are leading, its pointwise bound integrates against a probability measure. Otherwise choose one remainder letter. The first alternative in (14.24) pays \(l\) pointwise; the second restricts the whole product to \(E_h\) and pays \(l\) after integration. This proves (14.25) in every case. All integrals exist: the body-product majorant is integrable, the other factors are bounded, and the positive monomial weight can be divided out. Finite tag averaging preserves the inequality because its weights are nonnegative and sum to one.

Here is the finite connected-support estimate with the precise smallness condition used here. Put \(c_j = \text{cov}_{B_c} \hspace{0.17em} X_j\), \(a_j = b_j e^{vc_j}\) and \(\hat{b}_j = b_j e^{(v + 1) c_j}\), and assume

\(\displaystyle (2 B_c + 1)^4 \Sigma \leqslant 1.\)

The sum of \(\hat{b}_j\) over supports meeting \(X_i\) is at most \(|X_i | \Sigma \leqslant c_i\). To count rooted trees, start with \(R_i^{(0)} = a_i\) and iterate

\(\displaystyle R_i^{(q + 1)} = a_i \exp \left( \sum_{j : X_j \cap X_i \ne \varnothing} R_j^{(q)} \right) .\)

These are the nonnegative sums over rooted trees of bounded height; the exponential's factorial divides out permutations of the children. Allowing repeated labels only enlarges the sum. Induction gives \(R_i^{(q)} \leqslant a_i e^{c_i} = \hat{b}_i\) for every \(q\). Each finite connected set containing \(i\) has a spanning tree rooted at \(i\), with weight the product of its \(a_j\); its distinct labels ensure that the child permutations cancel exactly the exponential factorials. Its union charge is no larger than this product charge, by cover subadditivity. Passing monotonically to the tree limit therefore bounds the connected-set sum rooted at \(i\) by \(\hat{b}_i\). A component containing a specified site contains at least one letter whose support contains that site. Summing the rooted bound gives site bound at most \(\Sigma \leqslant e^{\Sigma} - 1\). Likewise a component carrying a specified marker contains some letter carrying it. Summing those rooted bounds gives marker-root bound at most \(T_e \eta + a\). Thus the same estimates hold simultaneously for every marker, without assigning independent bounds to overlapping subsets. This is a bound on sets of distinct elementary letters, before the later logarithmic expansion with repeated labels. Let the all-hole normalization have raised hole-correction site bound \(k\) and boundary-correction site bound \(\psi\), at exponent \(v\); the raised hole corrections have already been multiplied by \(l\). If

\(\displaystyle e^{\Sigma} - 1 + k \leqslant \psi, \qquad T_e \eta + a \leqslant y,\) (14.26)

then the resulting representation has forced bound paid by \(m\), regular nonleading bound paid by \(l\), total regular site bound at most \(\psi\), and regular marker-root bound at most \(y\). Boundary corrections retain their separately purchased bound \(\psi\). The corrections carrying no marker make no contribution to the last bound. This is the complete bound output needed in the marked propagation; the subsequent finite inventory compression is that of Subsection 14.18.

14.4.3Two complementary pieces of every leading insertion

The actual incoming leading letter \(W_h\) is split using its one local cutoff \(0 \leqslant \Gamma_h \leqslant 1\) into \(\Gamma_h W_h\) and \((1 - \Gamma_h) W_h\), with the same support and nonempty marker set. In the squarefree marker algebra their product is zero. Consequently

\(\displaystyle (1 + \Gamma_h W_h z_{F_h}) (1 + (1 - \Gamma_h) W_h z_{F_h}) = 1 + W_h z_{F_h} .\)

This is exact also off the healthy set, since \(W_h\) retains its original mask. The first piece is leading; the second is a remainder. The cutoff plateau implies that the latter vanishes outside the actual warning event. Its purchased probability is at most \(x^{- 1}\), where \(x\) is the coarse rung coupling. Thus \(lx^{- 1} \leqslant 1\) supplies the second alternative in (14.24). Each piece is bounded by the same pointwise bound, so \(2 T_e h\) pays for a leading-family marker budget \(h\) after coarse support conversion. An arbitrary regular family of budget \(z\) is designated entirely as remainder and costs \(lT_e z\); it uses the first alternative in (14.24). Hence their union has marker bound

\(\displaystyle a = lT_e z + 2 T_e h.\) (14.27)

The event estimate here is the actual joint sector warning estimate from the local Gaussian construction. A separate Gaussian sample for each factor would not prove (14.25).

All leading connected pieces are contained in one cell. Their connected reader graph has at most \(n\) vertices, and the support argument in Subsection 14.18 bounds its radius by \((2 n + 1) \rho\). On the actual elementary support radius \(3 B_c\), this is \((6 n + 3) B_c\). The purchased condition

\(\displaystyle 6 n + 3 \leqslant A / (3 + 2 n)\)

therefore puts it inside the required slice radius \(AB_c / (3 + 2 n)\). The whole-mode selection and coefficientwise integration in (14.2)–(14.3) identify its projected polynomial with the planted integral on the same cell. Its integrability also follows directly from the product of its pointwise letter bounds divided by its positive marker weight. Thus this is the actual leading polynomial, not just a majorant for it.

14.4.4Upper and lower numerical purchases

Put \(H = \exp ((\log \log s)^2)\), \(K = H^{- 1 / 8}\), and \(h = H^{- 4}\). The rung couplings \(x, y\) both lie in \([s / 2, s]\). Fix \(n, e, L\) first. The large-field allowance \(G (y)\) is positive, at most one, and decays faster than every negative power of \(H\) uniformly on this interval. The radii \(B_c = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil^2\) give \(\mathcal{W} (B_c) = H^{o (1)}\) uniformly there.

For the upper part use the exponent \(e = e_0 + 2\) and set

\(\displaystyle l = \max \left( 1, \frac{H^{- 3}}{\alpha z + H^{- q}} \right), \quad 0 \leqslant z \leqslant H^{- 4}, \qquad m = G (x)^{- 2}, \qquad a = lT_e z + 2 T_e H^{- 4} .\)

Here \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_e\) and \(q\) is fixed. If the displayed maximum is its second entry, \(lT_e z \leqslant H^{- 3} / 8\); if it is one, the same bound holds eventually since \(z \leqslant H^{- 4}\). Also \(2 T_e H^{- 4} \leqslant H^{- 3} / 4\) eventually. Thus \(a \leqslant H^{- 3} / 2\) eventually, after harmlessly enlarging the threshold. The incoming forced budget is \(\eta = G (y)\). Taking \(\delta = K / 16\), both nonbody terms in (14.23) are at most \(K / 16\) eventually. Consequently \(\Sigma \leqslant 3 K / 16 \leqslant K / 4\) and \(T_e \eta + a \leqslant H^{- 3}\). Also \(l \leqslant \max (1, H^{q - 3})\), so \(lx^{- 1} \leqslant 1\) uniformly for \(x \geqslant s / 2\) eventually.

For the lower part use \(e = e_0\), \(l = 1\), \(m = G (y)^{- 1}\), \(0 \leqslant z \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\) and incoming forced budget \(\eta = z \omega (y)\), where \(0 \leqslant \omega (y) \leqslant 1\) is the actual lower-interval attenuation. There are no designated leading letters. The regular marker budget is \(a = T_e z\). Again the two nonbody site terms are each at most \(K / 16\) eventually. The output marker budget is

\(\displaystyle T_e z \omega (y) + T_e z \leqslant 2 T_e z \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} (\alpha z + H^{1 / 2} z^2) \quad (\alpha \geqslant 8 T_e) .\)

This includes \(z = 0\) without division. The leading output is empty: with no body letters, a leading component would consist solely of insertion letters designated leading, of which there are none; with a body letter it is already nonleading.

In both cases the hole bound is \(k = K / 4\), the boundary and output site allowance is \(\psi = K\), and the tree smallness condition holds for sufficiently large \(s\). Finally \(e^{K / 4} - 1 + K / 4 \leqslant K\) for all sufficiently small \(K > 0\), for example by \(e^t - 1 \leqslant 2 t\) on \(0 \leqslant t \leqslant 1\). These calculations prove (14.26). The support purchases (14.21) for both \(e_0, e_0 + 2\) are made by choosing \(L\) after these fixed exponents and the two positive incoming support exponents \(\sigma_{\text{lower}}, \sigma_{\text{upper}}\). The separate purchases are

\(\displaystyle 10 \cdot 15^4 (e_0 + 7) \leqslant L \sigma_{\text{lower}}, \qquad 10 \cdot 15^4 (e_0 + 9) \leqslant L \sigma_{\text{upper}} .\)

In the actual lower and upper inputs these exponents are respectively \(e_0\) and \(e_0 + 2\). No existence claim is made at a zero incoming exponent. The coupling threshold is chosen after \(L\). For finitely many arities choose the maximum of their constants before that threshold.

14.4.5The exact representation used by both parts

The body product and all-hole inputs used above belong to one compiler, chosen before the marked family. Specifically its body expansion is (14.5) for the genuine \(P\), whereas its normalization is (14.6) for the corrected body alphabet together with the archived activities of \(P^0\). The equality of archived activities of \(P\) and \(P^0\) allows that alphabet to embed into the full marked alphabet, with the same full supports and inventories. It does not allow the genuine fine exponential to be replaced by that of \(P^0\). The empty-marker component bijection proved in Subsection 14.1 transports the all-hole identity to every deleted support of the full marked alphabet. Applying it at the union of the marked forced supports and using the exclusion bijection gives precisely the push of the genuine marked density, normalized by the selected coarse representation.

The unmarked compiler's purchase is made once for multipliers at least as large as both uses above, and for the larger support exponent. Decreasing a multiplier or exponent only decreases every nonnegative bound. Its body estimate, hole correction and forced embedding therefore remain valid for each call. In particular its coarse normalization is selected before the arbitrary marked background and regular family. The upper calculation adds the leading family and its complementary cutoff pieces; the lower calculation uses only the ordinary family. The same construction supplies both transitions on each rung of the actual restarted history. The coupling of the push is the genuine incoming \(P. \beta\) in both cases, by the retained course identity.

This proves the mixed integral and bound join from the actual local body and all-hole compiler purchases, the joint warning estimate and the selected geometry. It does not construct a different restarted history or deduce those purchases from an abstract Gaussian model. Their primitive analytic suppliers are the common corrected sector expansion, its unmarked all-hole normalization, and its paid product bounds; they must be supplied for the same body alphabet and coarse representation before this argument is applied.

14.5The curvature covariance on the selected tree slice

The local Gaussian comparison needs an exact identification at the flat configuration. Its reference matrix is the inverse on the selected forest, whereas the free curvature estimates are expressed using two consecutive members of the precision tower. We now identify these two descriptions. Everything in this section is on the same pair of finite tori. In particular, no limit in the period and no change of forest is involved.

14.5.1Counting coordinates and the flat precision

Write \(E_f^r\) and \(E_c^r\) for real \(r\)-cochains on the fine and coarse tori, with their counting inner products. Set

\(\displaystyle d_f : E_f^0 \longrightarrow E_f^1, \quad D : E_f^1 \longrightarrow E_f^2, \quad d_c : E_c^0 \longrightarrow E_c^1, \quad D' : E_c^1 \longrightarrow E_c^2 .\)

Thus \(Dd_f = 0\) and \(D' d_c = 0\). Matrices initially act on one colour; tensoring with the identity on the real orthonormal colour space gives the physical arrays. There is no volume factor in this convention.

Let \(M_f, M_c\) be the actual symmetric two-form metrics at the two levels, with \(M_f \geqslant aI\) and \(M_c \geqslant a' I\) for \(a, a' > 0\). Put

\(\displaystyle K = D^{\textsf{T}} M_f D, \qquad K' = (D')^{\textsf{T}} M_c D', \qquad Q = \bar{Q} + d_c R_0, \qquad A = K + \kappa Q^{\textsf{T}} Q, \quad \kappa > 0.\)

Here \(\bar{Q} : E_f^1 \to E_c^1\) is the selected averaged path map, \(R_0 : E_f^1 \to E_c^0\) is its root correction, and \(Q\) is the derivative of the actual predictor. If \(E : E_f^0 \to E_c^0\) evaluates a potential at the block roots, then

\(\displaystyle Qd_f = d_c E.\) (14.28)

Indeed the integral of a gradient along a path is the difference of its endpoint values. In \(\bar{Q} d_f\) these are the weighted endpoint averages. The root correction replaces each such average by the value at the root, giving (14.28). This also explains why a gradient with zero root values costs no energy in \(A\).

We first relate \(A\) to the flat rows used in the local comparison. Suppose the selected path coefficients are absolutely summable and attached to a two-form row \(R_f\): the sum of the coefficients with each given endpoint and plaquette type is exactly that row entry. Suppose the full factorization and symmetry identities are

\(\displaystyle M_f = \alpha I + R_f^{\textsf{T}} R_f, \qquad \alpha > 0,\) (14.29)

and the curl energy \(u^{\textsf{T}} Kv\) is invariant under the selected signed lattice motions. These are the path attachment, metric factorization and symmetry properties of the same fine bed; they must refer to that bed, not an independently chosen comparison metric.

At the zero background, with every path admitted, parallel transport in each path row is the identity. The coefficient sum in that row is therefore \(R_f\), by absolute summability and attachment. The fine linear response in channel \(s\) is \(DP_s\), where \(P_s\) is the signed permutation on link cochains. The predictor response is the signed permutation of \(Qu\) on coarse bonds. Consequently the channel's bilinear form is

\(\displaystyle \alpha \langle DP_s u, DP_s v \rangle + \langle R_f DP_s u, R_f DP_s v \rangle + \kappa \langle Qu, Qv \rangle = \langle u, Av \rangle .\)

For the equality, first use (14.29), then the stated invariance of the curl energy; the coarse signed permutation preserves the counting inner product. Each channel has this same bilinear form, so its normalized orientation average is also \(A\). Restricting to the selected tree gives the tree compression of \(A\). This identification is at zero background with all paths admitted; the estimates comparing a curved or cut row with it are separate.

14.5.2Rooted gauge projection and the slice inverse

Fix the selected forest \(F\), with one designated root in each block. Let \(I_F\) insert the coordinates of the nonforest bonds into \(E_f^1\), putting zero on every forest bond. The coordinate space of these free bonds will be denoted \(V_F\). For \(u \in E_f^1\) define \(\phi_u (x)\) as the oriented integral of \(u\) along the forest path from \(x\) back to its root. Define

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{P}u = u + d_f \phi_u .\)

The root values of \(\phi_u\) vanish. The difference of two adjacent path integrals is the negative of the intervening oriented bond value, so \(\mathcal{P}u\) vanishes on the forest. If \(u\) already vanishes there, all its forest path integrals vanish, and \(\mathcal{P}u = u\). Furthermore,

\(\displaystyle A\mathcal{P}= A, \qquad D\mathcal{P}= D.\) (14.30)

The second equality follows from \(Dd_f = 0\); the first follows from the same identity and (14.28), since \(E \phi_u = 0\).

The tree comparison of Subsection 8.12, specialized to the flat field and summed over bonds, gives finite constants \(b_1, b_2 \geqslant 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle \|u\|^2 \leqslant b_1 \|Du\|^2 + b_2 \|Qu\|^2 \quad \text{whenever } u \text{ vanishes on } F.\)

Here the block geometry and predictor branch hypotheses of that comparison are retained. In particular the fine side is at least one hundred times the macro radius and the coarse side exceeds one, as in its finite-torus application. Hence the compressed precision \(A_F = I_F^{\textsf{T}} AI_F\) is positive definite, with lower bound \(\min (a, \kappa) / (1 + b_1 + b_2)\). Define its extended covariance by

\(\displaystyle X = I_F A_F^{- 1} I_F^{\textsf{T}} .\)

For any \(u\), write \(\mathcal{P}u = I_F v\) in its free coordinates. Using (14.30) gives

\(\displaystyle XAu = XA\mathcal{P}u = I_F A_F^{- 1} I_F^{\textsf{T}} AI_F v = I_F v =\mathcal{P}u.\)

We have proved the exact identity

\(\displaystyle DXA = D.\) (14.31)

The full precision need not be invertible. Its inverse on the forest slice is enough, because curl is unchanged by the rooted gauge projection.

14.5.3Curvature matrices without inverting zero modes

For any symmetric positive definite matrix \(M\) on two-forms and any finite curl matrix \(q\), there is a matrix \(Z\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle (q^{\textsf{T}} Mq) Z = q^{\textsf{T}} .\) (14.32)

To see this, positivity gives \(\ker (q^{\textsf{T}} Mq) = \ker q\). Since \(q^{\textsf{T}} Mq\) is symmetric, its range is the orthogonal complement of this kernel, which is precisely \(\text{ran} \hspace{0.17em} q^{\textsf{T}}\). Solve each column of (14.32) in that range. Any two solutions differ by a matrix with columns in \(\ker q\). Thus \(P_M = qZ\) is independent of the choice. Moreover

\(\displaystyle P_M = Z^{\textsf{T}} (q^{\textsf{T}} Mq) Z,\)

by transposing (14.32). It is therefore symmetric and positive semidefinite.

Apply this construction on both tori, choosing

\(\displaystyle KZ = D^{\textsf{T}}, \qquad K' Z' = (D')^{\textsf{T}}, \qquad P_f = DZ, \quad P_c = D' Z' .\)

These are the finite-period free curvature matrices. This construction does not divide by the curl eigenvalue at zero momentum or discard a harmonic link direction.

Let \(A_0 : E_c^1 \to E_f^1\) be the selected one-form transport. The stationarity identities on the same beds are

\(\displaystyle KA_0 = \bar{Q}^{\textsf{T}} K', \qquad \bar{Q} A_0 = I - \kappa^{- 1} K' .\) (14.33)

For completeness, the two-form transport used below is \(J = H_f^{- 1} TH_c\), where \(H_f, H_c\) are the diagonal two-form operators of the selected beds and \(T\) is their installed tower map. Their inverse and transport identities give

\(\displaystyle DA_0 = JD' .\) (14.34)

The operators \(H_f, H_c\) in this formula are the diagonal operators; the metrics defining \(K, K'\) remain \(M_f, M_c\). This distinction is needed when the metric has undergone its prescribed repair.

Straighten \(A_0\) onto the forest by setting \(W = A_0 + d_f \Phi\), where the column potential \(\Phi\) is the backwards forest integral of \(A_0\), minus the blockwise constant root correction \(R_0 A_0\). It has root values \(E \Phi = - R_0 A_0\), and the same path difference calculation makes \(W\) vanish on the forest. Equations (14.28)–(14.33) give

\(\displaystyle QW = I - \kappa^{- 1} K', \qquad KW = Q^{\textsf{T}} K', \qquad AW = \kappa Q^{\textsf{T}}, \qquad DW = JD' .\) (14.35)

Indeed \(QW = \bar{Q} A_0\) because the root correction cancels. Also \(KW = KA_0\), while \((Q - \bar{Q})^{\textsf{T}} K' = R_0^{\textsf{T}} d_c^{\textsf{T}} K' = 0\). Adding \(\kappa Q^{\textsf{T}} QW\) to \(KW\) gives the third identity. The last follows from (14.34) and \(Dd_f = 0\).

14.5.4Exact conditional curvature covariance

The cross-covariance identity needed in the calculation is

\(\displaystyle D' QZ = P_c J^{\textsf{T}} .\) (14.36)

Transpose \(KW = Q^{\textsf{T}} K'\) and multiply by \(Z\). This yields

\(\displaystyle K' QZ = W^{\textsf{T}} KZ = W^{\textsf{T}} D^{\textsf{T}} = (D')^{\textsf{T}} J^{\textsf{T}} .\)

Since \((Z')^{\textsf{T}} K' = D'\) and \((Z')^{\textsf{T}} (D')^{\textsf{T}} = (D' Z')^{\textsf{T}} = P_c\), multiplication by \((Z')^{\textsf{T}}\) proves (14.36).

We can now compute directly. Equations (14.31) and (14.35) give \(DX (\kappa Q^{\textsf{T}}) = DW\). Also

\(\displaystyle AZ = D^{\textsf{T}} + \kappa Q^{\textsf{T}} QZ.\)

Multiplication by \(DX\) and use of (14.31) therefore show

\(\displaystyle DXD^{\textsf{T}} + DWQZ = DZ = P_f .\)

Substitute \(DW = JD'\) and (14.36). We obtain

\(\displaystyle (DI_F) A_F^{- 1} (DI_F)^{\textsf{T}} = P_f - JP_c J^{\textsf{T}} .\) (14.37)

In particular the right side is positive semidefinite, being the covariance of the curl of a centered Gaussian on \(V_F\). Tensoring every map with the colour identity proves the same identity for all physical colour coordinates and all cross-entries.

Equation (14.37) identifies the flat reference covariance on the actual tree with the tower's conditional curvature matrix. Together with the flat row identification above, it supplies the exact algebraic reference for the local Gaussian comparison. Passing from a posed, truncated local covariance to this reference still uses the local inverse and window estimates. Selecting the beds, path attachment, symmetry and stationary tower on one retained history is likewise an incoming construction requirement.
