Taking Gram products preserves such a bound: if \(L\) is the full row map and \(L_r\) its cut version, then

\(\displaystyle L^{\textsf{T}} L - L_r^{\textsf{T}} L_r = (L - L_r)^{\textsf{T}} L + L_r^{\textsf{T}} (L - L_r) .\)

The weighted row-and-column norm is submultiplicative, and both row maps have fixed bounds. Thus the corresponding precisions \(A_F, A_{F, r}\) differ by at most \(C_2 e^{- \sigma r / 4}\). The cut does not remove the direct curl or predictor terms, and every remaining path term is a nonnegative Gram form. Retaining just the identity channel in the normalized signed average gives a lower bound equal to \(1 / |\mathcal{S}|\) times its direct-curl and predictor form, where \(\mathcal{S}\) is the finite signed-channel set. Apply the tree inequality of Subsection 14.5 with direct coefficient \(\alpha > 0\) from (14.29): the cut precision has lower bound \(\min (\alpha, \kappa) / [|\mathcal{S}| (1 + b_1 + b_2)]\). Thus both cut and uncut precisions have a common fixed positive gap, without using the uncut metric factorization for the cut rows. The weighted inverse estimate of Subsection 8.13 and the identity

\(\displaystyle A_{F, r}^{- 1} - A_F^{- 1} = A_{F, r}^{- 1} (A_F - A_{F, r}) A_F^{- 1}\)

therefore give the same exponential order for the inverse difference. All norms can be taken at one smaller fixed positive width.

For the actual bare principal windows the inner radius is \(\lfloor B / 60 \rfloor\), by the panel inclusion in Subsection 9.4. The window-error proof of Subsection 8.13 then bounds the difference between the ordered bare window inverse and \(A_{F, r}^{- 1}\) by \(C_3 e^{- \gamma \lfloor B / 60 \rfloor}\), with \(\gamma > 0\). The empty store has all forest coordinates active. Hence restriction to these coordinates identifies its windows and cut flat precision with the ones just used; it introduces no further projection of an inverse. Symmetrization does not increase the zero-width norm. For \(B \geqslant 200000\),

\(\displaystyle B \leqslant 120 \lfloor B / 60 \rfloor, \qquad B \leqslant 200000 \lfloor B / 100000 \rfloor .\)

Taking \(c \leqslant \min (\gamma / 120, \sigma / 800000)\) and adding the two errors proves (14.69). Since \(B \geqslant (\log t)^2\), eventually \(e^{- cB} \leqslant t^{- 1 / 4}\).

14.10.2Reading curvature and preserving every cross-entry

Let \(L = DI_F\) be the full forest-coordinate curl matrix, with colour identity included. Each of its absolute row sums is bounded by a fixed number \(U\), uniformly in period and forest. A nonzero entry \(L_{ai}\) places the parent anchor of \(i\) within one coarse step of the plaquette anchor of \(a\). Therefore, if a plaquette \(a\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle \text{dist} (o, \text{anchor} (a)) + w (x) + 1 \leqslant W,\)

every coordinate used by its curl row is interior. The clearance condition makes all these forest coordinates active. This is why the actual restricted curl and \(L\) read precisely the same coordinates on the plaquettes under consideration.

For two such plaquettes \(a, b\), expand their covariance as a finite double sum. Applying (14.68) to each pair of used coordinates gives

\(\displaystyle | \text{Cov}_{\text{actual}} (a, b) - (LC^0 L^{\textsf{T}})_{ab} | \leqslant t^{- 1 / 4} \sum_i |L_{ai} | \sum_j |L_{bj} | \leqslant U^2 t^{- 1 / 4} .\)

No independence between coordinates or plaquettes was used. The same calculation and (14.69) give

\(\displaystyle | (LC^0 L^{\textsf{T}})_{ab} - (LA_F^{- 1} L^{\textsf{T}})_{ab} | \leqslant U^2 Ke^{- cB} \leqslant U^2 Kt^{- 1 / 4} .\)

By (14.37), the last full covariance is the colour extension of \(P_f - JP_c J^{\textsf{T}}\). Consequently, eventually in \(x\),

\(\displaystyle | \text{Cov}_{\text{actual}} (a, b) - (P_f - JP_c J^{\textsf{T}})_{ab} | \leqslant U^2 (1 + K) t^{- 1 / 4} \leqslant x^{- 1 / 8} .\) (14.70)

For the last inequality, \(t \geqslant x\) and the fixed constant \(U^2 (1 + K)\) is eventually at most \(x^{1 / 8}\). The mean estimate in (14.67) similarly implies \(|m_j - m^0_{\iota (j)} | \leqslant x^{- 7 / 8}\).

Finally, the actual covariance \(C\) is positive definite with a uniform entry bound, whether or not a pose is available. Indeed the sector gap and row bounds hold for the actual flag store, and the positive local covariance construction of Subsection 8.13 applies to either admitted window geometry. Its row bounds each entry. Enlarge this constant to at least one and take the maximum of the finitely many thresholds used above. This one choice supplies positivity and bounded entries for every permitted store, together with the mean and curvature comparison on every admissible pose, forest, tag and period.

These are the quantitative local Gaussian estimates. The nonlinear comparison between a group-valued field read and its linear Gaussian coordinate, the lost-mask probability and the selected-history telescoping error are additional steps; none is inferred from a covariance identity alone.

14.11Applying the local estimates to one marked step

The local estimates must hold for the very matrices and profiles used in the marked density identity. We now make this common choice explicit. The argument combines the preceding local results; it does not introduce a new Gaussian law or replace the selected history.

Fix a gauge group, a patch of block size \(L\), its funded tube construction, and the probability-Haar chart of Subsection 8.18. The minimizing and fallback branches are those fixed in Subsection 8.8. Let \(e \geqslant 0\) be the support exponent needed by the marked step, including its prescribed reserve, and let \(t > 0\) be its footprint allowance. The conversion from fine supports to coarse connected covers is required to have reserve \(t\) at this \(e\). Fix also the profile width \(W > 0\), the chart-radius multiplier \(d \geqslant 0\), the warning margin \(\varepsilon > 0\), the owner-energy constant \(m > 0\), and \(K > 0\) with

\(\displaystyle 2 K \leqslant m / 2, \qquad 2 a_{\text{coast}} \leqslant K / 4.\)

Here \(a_{\text{coast}}\) is the coefficient of the forced large-field allowance \(G (u) = \exp (- a_{\text{coast}} \text{hot} (u)^2)\). These are the numerical margins already used by the corrected compiler and its inherited-activity estimate.

The guide estimate is chosen before \(L\): the absolute path sums of Subsection 8.5 give \(C = 2 A (1 + a)\) simultaneously for all coarse fields, including all stores later compatible with those fields. The choices in Subsection 8.15 then impose

\(\displaystyle C / L^2 \leqslant \sqrt{\text{direct}} / 64, \qquad C + \sqrt{\text{direct}} / 64 \leqslant (W / 4) \sqrt{\text{penalty}} .\)

After \(L\) and \(W\) have been fixed, the remaining constants below may depend on this patch. They are chosen before the coupling parameter and before any torus, forest, tag, coarse field or finite family of insertions.

For a large parameter \(x\), write \(y = r (x)\) for its retiming and \(B = \lceil (\log y)^2 \rceil\). An admissible marked step consists of its actual tube, fine representation \(R\), coarse budget, chart and profiles. Its tube is funded by the fixed patch construction; its upward paths and high clips are the path and clip of \(R\). Its coarse threshold is \(\text{fieldCut} (y)\), its chart radius is \(d \hspace{0.17em} \text{fineCut} (y)\), and its coarse side is at least \(100 (1 + B)\). The remaining radius, threshold, coupling-band and inherited-law inequalities are exactly those listed in Subsection 14.3. In addition we retain the actual alongside certificate for the tube and the small additive remainder of \(R\). These identify the input to the construction; large coupling alone would not identify its paths or its remainder.

14.11.1One matrix certificate and both support inventories

For every compatible store, forest, original tag and coarse field, use the actual selected derivative matrix of Subsection 8.16. Complete its Gram matrix by the identity on the inactive link-colour coordinates and call the resulting full precision \(A\). Proposition 8.5, applied at \(y\), supplies the selected-row gap and screened covariance estimates for this actual matrix. These are derivatives at the actual replica and predictor. The transported-flat precision of Subsection 8.12 is a different construction; its bound does not by itself identify the two matrices away from the flat reference. The bounds are uniform over all of these data, including frozen coordinates; compatibility requires that the stored coarse flags equal the flags of the coarse field at threshold \(\text{fieldCut} (y)\).

Here is a simultaneous choice of constants in the local inverse and logarithmic estimates. Let \(s > 0, M_0 \geqslant 0, \eta > 0\) be their common initial Schur rate, amplitude and gap: take \(s = \sigma / 2\), \(M_0 = L_0^2\) from that proposition's row bound (by submultiplicativity of the weighted Schur norm), and its gap \(\eta\). Set \(M = \max (M_0, 1)\) and \(h = \min (\eta, 1)\). Choose the inverse rate \(Y > 0\) supplied by Subsection 8.13, with \(Y < s\), and put

\(\displaystyle m_0 = Y / 2, \quad v = m_0 / 2, \quad R_0 = (m_0 - v) / 120, \quad b = R_0 / 2, \quad \kappa = b / 2.\)

Enlarge the common amplitude \(C \geqslant 1\) to cover the precision, its inverse and the local volume sum at rate \(Y - m_0\), divided by \(\min (h / 2, 1)\). These constants depend only on the fixed patch data. The window formulas of Subsection 8.13 now bound the actual inverse and both determinant corrections with rate \(v\), amplitude \(C\) and error \(e^{- bB}\). They apply also to every admissible recut whose windows contain their radius-\(B / 60\) neighborhoods. Indeed, the missed entries have separation at least \(\lfloor B / 60 \rfloor\); for \(B \geqslant 120\),

\(\displaystyle B \leqslant 120 \lfloor B / 60 \rfloor .\)

The weighted inverse estimate therefore bounds their error by \(C^4 e^{- R_0 B} \leqslant e^{- bB}\) eventually. The same window estimate gives positivity of the recut covariance. The original selected covariance is kept fixed when these auxiliary recuts are considered.

We also need rooted bounds for two actual word inventories, of orders \(n = 0\) and \(n = 1\), with halo multiplier \(l = 20\). Set \(\theta = b / (4 (l + 2))\). In the itinerary summation of Subsection 8.23, the summation ratio has the form \(C_1 e^{- (b - \theta) B}\) and the numerator for order \(n\) is bounded by

\(\displaystyle C_2 e^{- [b - \theta (n + 1 + l)] B} .\)

The constants include the fixed cover weight at exponent \(e\). Since \(n \leqslant 1\), the exponent in brackets is at least \(3 b / 4 > \kappa\). The ratio is eventually at most \(1 / 2\), and the geometric series is therefore at most \(2 C_2 e^{- 3 bB / 4} \leqslant e^{- \kappa B}\). Intersect the two resulting tails. We obtain both rooted inventory bounds with the same \(v, C, b, \kappa\), the actual physical address map, and the complete halo supports. This is a bound for the individual ordered words through a fixed site, not just for their sum as an operator.

14.11.2The first derivative of the two long determinant families

The value-level compiler also needs first derivatives as the coarse field moves in its local logarithmic coordinates. Freeze stores, forests, tags and windows and use the actual matrix \(A (U)\), rather than selecting a new sector at each nearby field. We first justify a radius independent of the torus. The finite-patch predictor estimate of Subsection 8.8 gives a common chart radius for its retained rows. For the path rows, use the path extension bound in Subsection 11.2 before summing the slots. A fixed number of derivatives costs a polynomial in the path length; every additional spatial or derivative-coordinate weight costs an exponential in that length with an arbitrarily small fixed rate. Choose the latter rate so that \((1 + 2 j) \delta\) is at most half the incoming path rate for the required order \(j = 2\). The incoming absolute path budget absorbs the polynomial and all these weights. Summing the absolute slot bounds, including repeated links, therefore gives one entrywise derivative majorant for all rows on a fixed logarithmic ball. The local predictor rows have the same property on a smaller ball. We retain the derivative-coordinate weights in this statement. For an ordered list \(\alpha\) of atomic link-color derivatives, \(| \alpha | \leqslant 2\), put \(W_{\alpha} (o) = \prod_{a \in \alpha} e^{\delta d (o, \text{hearth} (a))}\). For a row anchored at \(o\) and a column anchored at \(z\), the bounds are

\(\displaystyle W_{\alpha} (o) e^{\delta d (o, z)} | \partial_{\alpha} M_{oz} | \leqslant C_1, \qquad W_{\alpha} (z) e^{\delta d (o, z)} | \partial_{\alpha} M_{oz} | \leqslant C_1 .\)

To verify them term by term, a derivative is zero if any atom misses the slot's complete support. Otherwise its endpoint and derivative-coordinate distances cost at most \((1 + 2| \alpha |) \delta (\ell + 5)\) for a slot of length \(\ell\). The chosen half-rate reserve pays this exponential, while the other half pays its fixed-order differentiation factor. Absolute summation over the original path labels proves both displayed bounds. The predictor contribution has fixed reach and a uniform analytic radius, so the same argument uses its finite support instead. Repeated atomic derivatives and repeated physical links are included.

Sum rows and columns at spatial rate \(\delta / 2\), leaving a decaying factor for their exponential shells. The bounded number of coordinates per site makes these sums finite uniformly in the period. For derivative order \(k = 2\), lower both spatial and derivative-coordinate rates further to \(u = (\delta / 2) / (1 + k)\). The inequality \(u + ku \leqslant \delta / 2\) pays the transfer of derivative-coordinate weights across the intermediate matrix index. For \(M^{\textsf{T}} M\), expand each ordered derivative list over its finitely many Leibniz partitions. The triangle inequality transfers the derivative-coordinate weights to the appropriate factor in each term; weighted row/column multiplication then bounds that term. Summing the finitely many partitions gives the full weighted derivative bounds for the precision. Complete inactive coordinates by the identity. All selections remain frozen; no derivative of a flag indicator occurs. Thus the estimates retain the atomic-list and support information required by the itinerary argument below.

For precision, these first-derivative bounds give constants \(\sigma, C_0, r_1 > 0\), uniform in the period and base field, such that

\(\displaystyle | (DA (H) Z)_{ij} | \leqslant C_0 S_G (\sigma / 2) e^{- \sigma d (i, j)} \|Z\|_{\infty} \qquad (\|H\|_{\infty} \leqslant r_1) .\)

Here \(S_G (\sigma / 2)\) is a uniform upper bound for the sum of the decaying reciprocal derivative-coordinate weights \(e^{- \sigma d (o, \text{hearth} (a)) / 2}\), including color multiplicity. Let \(V_0\) bound the row and column volume sums at rate \(\sigma / 2\), and set \(U_0 = V_0 C_0 S_G (\sigma / 2)\). Integrate the last estimate along the segment from \(0\) to \(H\) and sum the remaining exponential weights. The Schur norm of \(A (H) - A (0)\) is at most \(U_0 \|H\|_{\infty}\). If the base gap is \(\eta\), choose

\(\displaystyle r_0 = \min \{r_1, \eta / [2 (1 + U_0)]\} .\)

Then for every real vector \(z\) and \(\|H\|_{\infty} \leqslant r_0\),

\(\displaystyle z^{\textsf{T}} A (H) z \geqslant (\eta - U_0 \|H\|_{\infty}) |z|^2 \geqslant (\eta / 2) |z|^2 .\)

This is a uniform neighborhood gap, including fields that no longer obey the original flag condition. The flag condition was used at the base field, and the matrix formula is extended with its selections fixed.

Apply the inverse and window derivative formulas of Subsection 8.19 on this ball. Restrict once more to half its radius. The actual selected covariance agrees there, as a germ, with the symmetric window formula for the completed precision: the unused-coordinate blocks are identities and the active blocks are the original selected ones. Both this covariance and the precision have the positive floors supplied by the window estimates. Restrict the logarithmic direction further to

\(\displaystyle \|H\|_{\infty} \leqslant \min \{r_0 / 2, r_G (1) / 2\},\)

where \(r_G (1) > 0\) is the gauge-coordinate radius at order one. This radius concerns the variation of a particular matrix function. It is not the width of the replacement profiles.

Apply the differentiated word estimates of Subsection 8.23 at derivative order one, halo \(20\) and support exponent \(e\). They cover both long determinant classes on the same field neighborhood. Choose their positive decay rate \(d_0\) and fixed letter factor \(F_0\) before \(y\). The scalar conversion and the two classes together cost at most

\(\displaystyle 4 F_0 e^1 \{2 (1 + 1)\}^1 e^{- d_0 B} .\)

For large \(B\) this is at most \(e^{- d_0 B / 2}\). Thus the common neighborhood, individual word bounds, and total connected-cover bound are available simultaneously. There is no need to identify \(d_0\) with the rate \(\kappa\) of the preceding value inventories; their two positive rates can be retained separately. The order parameters used here are: scalar conversion order two, coarse-field derivative order one, and halo twenty.

14.11.3Uniformity on a whole coupling interval

The matrix estimate of Subsection 8.17 provides one bound \(\ell \geqslant 1\) for all actual pilots of this patch. It gives the Gaussian-coordinate bound \(y^2\) on the interface event, the linear-residual bound \(y^{- 4}\), and the all-compatible-palette zero-mask identity. The last conclusion retains that section's same-field response, chart, beacon, port and distant-interface receipts uniformly for every matched pilot; the bound for one posed reader alone does not establish it. The actual owner-energy estimate (14.14) supplies the other missing input to Subsection 14.3. Its path counting and clip estimate use precisely the path and clip identified above. The fixed chart and branch construction, the guide reserve, and the small span estimate supply the remaining local premises of these applications.

These assertions must hold for every \(x \in [s / 2, s]\) once the chosen station parameter \(s\) is large. There are only finitely many eventual predicates at this stage: the matrix certificate and its two inventories, the first-derivative word bounds, palette bound, owner estimate, local chart and room conditions, and the fixed numerical inequalities for the corrected alphabet. Pull the predicates in \(y\) back along \(r (x) \to \infty\). Intersect them with the predicates already expressed in \(x\), and choose one lower threshold \(x_0\). Finally require \(s / 2 \geqslant x_0\). Then every \(x \in [s / 2, s]\) satisfies them. This step is performed before quantifying over periods, fields, forests and tags because each local estimate above was uniform in those variables. It is also before the choice of any finite marked family.

14.11.4The warning estimate on the corrected alphabet

Lemma 14.12. (Neutralization preserves the joint warning estimate) Fix an admissible local tuple as above, including its patch, funded tube, path and clip, coupling, chart, mask widths and predictor branch. Purchase the selected all-pilot package of Subsection 8.17 and the actual response bounds of Subsection 8.16 on this tuple. Let \(R^{\circ}\) be obtained from \(R\) by deleting only its additive remainder. The matched pilots, endpoint palettes, Gaussian integration law and warning sockets are identical for \(R\) and \(R^{\circ}\). Consequently their all-matched palette condition has one common constant and one common threshold, uniform over fields, stores, forests, tags and periods. For every finite set \(S\) of vertices of the corrected alphabet, write \(X (S)\) for its distinct warning addresses. If the other factors have the pointwise bounds \(c_i\) used in the corrected compiler and the warning factors have pointwise bound \(T\), then

\(\displaystyle \int \left| \prod_{i \in S} f_i \right| \hspace{0.17em} d \nu \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_A \leqslant E (y)^{|X (S) |} \prod_{i \in S} c_i, \qquad c_i = T \quad \text{for warning vertices} .\) (14.71)

Here \(\nu\) is the normalized Haar factor and \(\gamma_A\) is the single centered Gaussian law of this local tuple; \(E\) is the warning bound of (8.18), at the local scale \(y\). The same assertion holds after inserting any fixed nonnegative vertex inflation factors into both sides. It applies simultaneously to the upper and lower compiler uses.

Proof. The Gaussian precision and affine mean are formed from the funded quadratic forms, the path and clip, the coupling and the selected field, forest, tag and store. The chart and its cutoffs determine the matching conditions and the sockets. None of these constructions uses the additive remainder. Deleting that remainder therefore changes none of these objects, pointwise for every choice of the selected data. In particular, this is an equality of the Gaussian laws used by the compiler, not an assertion that the two full interacting densities are equal.

The response estimate is uniform over these choices. The selected all-pilot package supplies the beacon, port and distant-interface estimates on those same choices. Proposition 8.7 therefore supplies a single palette bound and a zero socket for every matched pilot on its toned event. The preceding pointwise identities transfer both quantified statements to \(R\) without a new selection of constants. The covariance bound and local mask identity now meet the hypotheses of Subsection 8.3. Hence simultaneous nonzero sockets at the addresses \(X (S)\) force its joint coordinate excursion event. The probability of that event is at most \(E (y)^{|X (S) |}\) by (8.19).

Bound the absolute integrand by the indicator of this one event times \(\prod c_i\) and integrate over the probability measure \(\nu \otimes \gamma_A\). This proves (14.71). In particular the factors introduced by restoring the additive remainder may be included in \(S\): only their pointwise bound is used, with no independence assertion. Multiplication by fixed nonnegative vertex inflation factors proves the inflated version. A warning address is counted once even if other vertices have overlapping support; no product of marginal warning probabilities is taken. Finally both compiler uses retain the same alphabet and Gaussian law, so the argument applies to both. The finite intersection and retiming argument above makes the threshold uniform for \(x \in [s / 2, s]\).\(\Box\)

The lemma discharges the warning transfer from the neutral pricing problem to the genuine corrected alphabet. It does not supply the incoming neutral law or the small additive remainder: these remain separate hypotheses of the local application.

14.11.5The genuine density and its auxiliary bounds

Apply the corrected exponent decomposition and bounded extension of Subsection 14.3 with these actual data. Apply the mixed compiler of Subsection 14.4 using the same word inventories and full read supports. The resulting profile system and finite unmarked alphabet are chosen before an output representation \(Q\) with the prescribed coarse budget, and before the marked insertions.

Write \(R\) for the actual incoming fine representation. Setting its additive remainder to zero, with the other data fixed, gives \(R^{\circ}\) for the inherited-law, warning and estimates. The small-remainder estimate for \(R\) is a separate hypothesis. The correction construction adds this genuine remainder to the alphabet, with bound \(e + 4 d \leqslant 6 \omega\) as in Subsection 14.1. The separate purchase \(2 a_{\text{coast}} \leqslant K / 4\) pays the forced inflation against the owner decay. Thus the planted identity of Subsection 14.1 reconstructs the density of \(R\), whereas the connected-support and return estimates use \(R^{\circ}\). Both assertions concern exactly the same corrected alphabet, profiles, path, clip and scalar loading.

That loading is positive: its chart scale and Gaussian prefactor are positive, the fine coupling is positive, and its reference kinetic Gram matrix has positive determinant. For this last assertion specialize the matrix certificate to the identity coarse field and empty store, retaining the reference forest and tag; the selected flat cut Gram has the same determinant as the reference kinetic matrix. The square-root determinant in the loading is therefore nonzero. It is the original reference-forest/tag loading, not an independently normalized version of the corrected alphabet.

At a short interval of the selected history, the application requires its actual patch and funded tube to agree with the constant patch data on that interval. The inherited field at its upper end must supply \(R\) and its remainder estimate, and its neutral version must satisfy the admissible local conditions above. The interval length fixes the permitted remainder budget; the station bounds must put its coupling in \([s / 2, s]\). When these same-course conditions are supplied, the construction applies to each step of that interval.

The restart construction of Subsection 13.12 sets the new coarse coupling equal to the incoming one. Use the positive loading to choose its scalar vacuum normalization, and use the connected expansion of the same alphabet to define its masked logarithmic remainder. This yields the exact pushed density and preserves the original coarse path and clip. It does not assert equality with the old coarse additive remainder.

Finally weaken the already obtained support, size and length bounds to the two sets required for the finite-reader comparison. Such weakening only decreases the positive weights in the rooted sums. The construction for an arbitrary finite marked family is consequently available for both sets of bounds on this same step, with the same density identity and the same original partition denominator. Combining these steps with the common reader and finite-menu choices in Subsection 16.8 gives its local compiler input provided the actual short course supplies the hypotheses used above on that same history and binding. Subsection 19.5 identifies the selected family, but the local application also needs the matching interval, representation, tube and bound certificates. In particular, the existence of final trial purchases does not by itself recover those certificates for a separate local invocation.

Under the displayed local receipts, this application supplies the matrix, two-inventory, first-derivative determinant, owner and all-palette inputs of the corrected compiler at the specified fixed patch data. It retains the selected history's actual path/clip, alongside, inherited-law and remainder hypotheses until their history-level suppliers are applied. In particular, it does not assert existence of a history from local coupling inequalities alone.

For the restarted comparison course, these history-level hypotheses are verified by Proposition 13.40, starting from the exact endpoint presentation of Proposition 13.38. Its proof supplies the inflated product estimates and the genuine additive costs on this alphabet; it does not identify them merely from equality of nominal coupling bounds.

14.12The Fourier window of the actual curvature covariance

The Gaussian comparison requires estimates for the covariance selected by the construction, uniformly in its ancestry and physical period. We derive these estimates before considering the cumulative transport. Fix the positive kernel penalty and a smoothing order \(J \geqslant 4\). All constants in this section may depend on these two choices and on the finite derivative order, but not on block lengths or ancestry.

14.12.1A uniformly elliptic symbol

Let \(H (p)\) be the two-form pulse symbol of the chosen ancestry, in radian frequency \(p \in \mathbb{R}^4\). The component and positive-row constructions give constants \(\alpha, \eta > 0\) and \(\Omega < \infty\) such that its Laurent coefficients have strip cost \(\Omega\) at width \(\alpha\), its action on every finite period has quadratic lower bound \(\eta\), and \(H (0) = I\). These are properties of the original pulse, before any replacement of the curvature completion. Exponential summability implies, for every integer \(m \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_p \|D^m H (p)\| \leqslant C_m .\)

Indeed each derivative multiplies the coefficient at \(z\) by at most \(|z|^m\), and \(|z|^m e^{- \alpha |z|_1}\) is bounded. The finite-period lower bound also holds at every real frequency. First extend the quadratic bound of the real finite-period operator to complex vectors by splitting them into real and imaginary parts. Then test the period-\(n\) matrix on the vector \(e^{ip \cdot x} v\) for a grid frequency \(p\). Its quadratic form and squared norm both acquire the same factor \(n^4\), which cancels. Approximate an arbitrary real frequency by grid frequencies and use continuity. Thus

\(\displaystyle \text{Re} \langle H (p) v, v \rangle \geqslant \eta |v|^2 .\)

Put \(q_a (p) = e^{ip_a} - 1\), and let \(X (p)\) and \(U (p)\) be exterior multiplication by \(q (p)\) from one-forms to two-forms and from scalars to one-forms, respectively. Define

\(\displaystyle F (p) = X (p)^{\ast} H (p) X (p) + U (p) U (p)^{\ast}, \qquad P (p) = X (p) F (p)^{- 1} X (p)^{\ast} \quad (p \ne 0),\)

and set \(P (0) = 0\). On the cube \(|p_a | < 3 \pi / 2\), the elementary identity for exterior multiplication and contraction gives

\(\displaystyle \|Xv\|^2 + \|U^{\ast} v\|^2 = |q|^2 \|v\|^2, \qquad \text{Re} \langle Fv, v \rangle \geqslant \min (\eta, 1) |q|^2 \|v\|^2 .\)

Here \(|q| \geqslant c |p|\): the continuous function \(|e^{it} - 1| / |t|\) has a positive lower bound on \([- 3 \pi / 2, 3 \pi / 2]\), with its value at zero defined to be one. Consequently \(F (p)\) is invertible for \(p \ne 0\) in this cube.

We record the derivative estimates with their exact gain. A function has order \(s\) if, through the finite number of derivatives under consideration, its \(m\)th derivative is bounded by \(C_m |p|^{s - m}\). Products add orders by Leibniz' rule. Taylor's formula gives \(q - ip\) order two and \(H - I\) order one; \(q\) itself has order one. The exterior identity at \(q = ip\) gives

\(\displaystyle F (p) = |p|^2 I + E (p), \qquad E \text{ has order } 3.\)

Thus \(T = |p|^{- 2} F\) has order zero, \(T - I\) order one, and \(\|T^{- 1} \|\) is uniformly bounded by coercivity. Differentiating \(TT^{- 1} = I\) proves inductively that \(D^m T^{- 1} = O (|p|^{- m})\): the term containing \(D^m T^{- 1}\) is isolated and every remaining term is a product of derivatives whose orders total \(m\). Furthermore \(T^{- 1} - I = T^{- 1} (I - T)\) has order one. It follows that

\(\displaystyle P \text{ has order } 0, \qquad P - P_0 \text{ has order } 1, \quad P_0 (p) = X_0 (p) |p|^{- 2} X_0 (p)^{\ast},\) (14.72)

where \(X_0\) is exterior multiplication by \(ip\). These estimates are uniform over all the selected ancestries.

We verify that \(P\) is the actual curvature multiplier. Let \(D\) be the original one-form covariance and \(R = X^{\ast} HX\). The transport identities already proved give \(RDX^{\ast} = X^{\ast}\) and \(XU = 0\). Write \(B = UU^{\ast}\). Then \(XB = 0\), \(BX^{\ast} = 0\), and \(RB = BR = 0\). Hence \(F = R + B\) commutes with \(B\), as does its inverse, and \(XF^{- 1} B = 0\). Multiplying \(RDX^{\ast} = X^{\ast}\) on the left by \(XF^{- 1}\) yields

\(\displaystyle XF^{- 1} X^{\ast} = XF^{- 1} RDX^{\ast} = XDX^{\ast} .\)

At zero both curvature multipliers vanish. Thus no change of the finite-period covariance has been made.

14.12.2A smooth chart without changing the finite Fourier sum

Choose a smooth nondecreasing function \(g\) that is zero on \((- \infty, - 5 \pi / 4]\) and one on \([- 3 \pi / 4, \infty)\), and put

\(\displaystyle h (p) = \prod_{a = 1}^4 (g (p_a) - g (p_a - 2 \pi)) .\)

It is supported in \(|p_a | \leqslant 5 \pi / 4\), equals one near zero, and satisfies

\(\displaystyle \sum_{z \in \mathbb{Z}^4} h (p + 2 \pi z) = 1.\)

The last identity is the product of four telescoping sums; only finitely many terms are nonzero. Define the unit-frequency chart \(K (\xi) = h (2 \pi \xi) P (2 \pi \xi)\), entry by entry and with the usual Kronecker delta on colour indices. Its support lies in \(| \xi | \leqslant 2\). For an integer displacement \(x\), periodicity and the partition identity show that the exact period-\(n\) covariance is

\(\displaystyle \kappa_n (x) = n^{- 4} \sum_{z \in \mathbb{Z}^4} e^{2 \pi ix \cdot z / n} K (z / n) .\) (14.73)

The sum is finite. Grouping \(z\) by its residue modulo \(n\) proves the formula, including even \(n\) and the zero frequency. At zero we retain the actual value \(P (0) = 0\), rather than assigning a continuous value to a direction-dependent limit.

For two lists of coordinate directions, of lengths \(a, b\), let \(u = a + b\). Differencing the first endpoint multiplies \(K (\xi)\) by \(e^{2 \pi i \xi_c} - 1\); differencing the second endpoint uses \(e^{- 2 \pi i \xi_c} - 1\). Denote their product by \(m (\xi)\), and the corresponding product of \(2 \pi i \xi_c\) and \(- 2 \pi i \xi_c\) by \(m_0 (\xi)\). Repeatedly using \(e^{it} - 1 - it = O (t^2)\), with its differentiated Taylor bounds, shows that \(m - m_0\) has order \(u + 1\) and \(m, m_0\) have order \(u\). By (14.72), the remainder

\(\displaystyle G (\xi) = m (\xi) K (\xi) - m_0 (\xi) e^{- (2 \pi)^2 | \xi |^2} P_0 (2 \pi \xi)\)

satisfies, for \(0 \leqslant v \leqslant u + 6\),

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \|D^v G (\xi)\| & \leqslant C | \xi |^{u + 1 - v} & & (0 < | \xi | \leqslant 1), \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.74)} \\ \|D^v G (\xi)\| & \leqslant C | \xi |^{- 5} & & (| \xi | \geqslant 1) . \end{array}\)

For the first estimate, subtract and add \(m_0 K\) and use the product rule. The extra Gaussian differs from one to order two. Outside the unit ball the compactly supported term has bounded derivatives, and every derivative of the Gaussian term decays faster than any power. Finite maxima give the same \(C\) for all face pairs and direction lists of bounded length.

14.12.3Fourier decay and quadrature at the pole

Write \(\mathcal{F}_+ f (x) = \int e^{2 \pi ix \cdot \xi} f (\xi) \hspace{0.17em} d \xi\). We first show directly that (14.74) implies

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{F}_+ G (x) | \leqslant C' |x|^{- u - 5} \quad (|x| \geqslant 1) .\) (14.75)

Set \(r = |x|^{- 1}\) and choose a smooth radial cutoff equal to zero on the ball of radius \(r / 2\) and one outside the ball of radius \(r\). The omitted inner term has integral at most \(C \int_0^r t^{u + 1} t^3 \hspace{0.17em} dt \leqslant C' r^{u + 5}\). For the outer term integrate by parts \(u + 6\) times in the unit direction \(x / |x|\). Each term where a derivative hits the cutoff is supported on \(r / 2 \leqslant | \xi | \leqslant r\) and has integral \(O (Cr^{- 1})\). For the remaining terms, derivatives of order \(u + 6\) have integral bounded by

\(\displaystyle C' \left( \int_r^1 t^{- 5} t^3 \hspace{0.17em} dt + \int_1^{\infty} t^{- 5} t^3 \hspace{0.17em} dt \right) \leqslant C'' / r.\)

Boundary terms vanish by first inserting a distant smooth cutoff and then sending its radius to infinity; the displayed bounds control its derivatives as well. Multiplication by \(|x|^{- u - 6}\) proves (14.75).

Next let \(f = mK\). It is compactly supported, bounded by \(C\), and satisfies \(\|Df (\xi)\| \leqslant C / | \xi |\) off zero. Partition frequency space into cubes of side \(1 / n\), and use their grid corner as the sample point. Its distance from any point of that cube is at most \(2 / n\). If this distance is less than \(| \xi | / 2\), the mean value bound gives

\(\displaystyle |f (\xi) - f (z / n) | \leqslant C' / (n| \xi |) .\)

Otherwise the same estimate follows from the bound \(2 C\) on the difference, after increasing \(C'\). This also covers a sampled corner at zero. The phase difference is at most \(2 \pi |x| \hspace{0.17em} | \xi - z / n|\). Integration over a fixed enlarged support ball is legitimate because \(| \xi |^{- 1}\) is locally integrable in four dimensions. We obtain

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathcal{F}_+ f (x) - n^{- 4} \sum_z e^{2 \pi ix \cdot z / n} f (z / n) \right| \leqslant C' \frac{1 + |x|}{n} .\) (14.76)

This estimate treats the singular grid point without removing it.

Finally the Gaussian-regularized continuum term has the required Maxwell limit. In the present Fourier convention its scalar potential multiplier, for \(t > 0\) (here \(t = (2 \pi)^2\)), is \((2 \pi)^{- 2} e^{- t | \xi |^2} / | \xi |^2\). The identity

\(\displaystyle \frac{e^{- t | \xi |^2}}{| \xi |^2} = \int_t^{\infty} e^{- s | \xi |^2} \hspace{0.17em} ds\)

and the product Gaussian integral give, at \(x \ne 0\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{F}_+ \left( (2 \pi)^{- 2} \frac{e^{- t| \xi |^2}}{| \xi |^2} \right) (x) = \frac{1}{4 \pi^2 |x|^2} (1 - e^{- \pi^2 |x|^2 / t}) .\)

Fubini is justified by \(\int_t^{\infty} (\pi / s)^2 \hspace{0.17em} ds < \infty\). All polynomial frequency moments are integrable, so differentiation under the integral supplies both curls and both endpoint derivative lists, with the opposite sign at the second endpoint. The difference from the derivatives of \(1 / (4 \pi^2 |x|^2)\) is a derivative of a Gaussian times that potential. It is bounded by any prescribed inverse power for \(|x| \geqslant 1\).

Combining this fact with (14.73), (14.75) and (14.76) proves

\(\displaystyle | \Delta_x^a \Delta_y^b \kappa_n (x, y) -\mathcal{K}_{a, b} (x - y) | \leqslant C_{a, b} \left( |x - y|^{- a - b - 5} + \frac{1 + |x - y|}{n} \right), \qquad |x - y| \geqslant 1.\) (14.77)

Here each superscript denotes its specified direction list, and \(\mathcal{K}_{a, b}\) is the corresponding continuum Maxwell curvature covariance. The constants are uniform in ancestry and period. The covariance has closed rows and columns because its construction is \(dDd^{\ast}\) and \(d^2 = 0\). Its entries are real by conjugating the finite Fourier sum and replacing the frequency by its negative. No positivity of a later normal-ordered covariance is inferred.

For a fixed comparison order \(d \geqslant 1\), only \(a, b \leqslant d - 1\) are needed. The zero-order comparison can use \(d = 1\) and discard the extra data. The construction reserves their finitely many chart constants at smoothing order \(J\) before choosing the block length. At the chosen comparison level the reserved chart cost is paid by \(H^{1 / 4}\), where \(H\) is its monitor. The finite constants from the multiplier and Fourier estimates are then absorbed successively into \(H^{1 / 2}\) and \(H\). This yields (14.77) with \(C_{a, b}\) replaced by \(H\), on that same chosen history. The quantitative clearance at the chosen level remains part of the common scale selection; merely taking an unrelated level large would not establish it.

14.13Cumulative transport and the actual Gaussian comparison

We prove the low-frequency transport bounds on the same atlas as Subsection 14.12. Write \(\kappa_i\) for its original real curvature covariance and \(\Psi_i\) for the installed map from level \(i\) to level \(i + 1\), with the identity on colour coordinates. Installation sums all periodic images of the cover row. Fix a comparison order \(d \geqslant 1\) and a plateau of smoothing order \(J\) with \(d - 1 \leqslant J - 2\).

14.13.1Moving differences through an actual step

For a word \(w\) of \(j \leqslant d - 1\) coordinate directions, the selected transport has a real quotient row \(Q_w\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle \Delta_w \Psi_i = Q_w \Delta_w, \qquad \sup_x \sum_y |Q_w (x, y) | \leqslant RL_i^{- j - 2},\) (14.78)

where \(L_i = a_i / a_{i + 1}\) and \(R \geqslant 1\) depends only on the kernel penalty and adjacent smoothing orders. The quotient needs proof; a bound on the left side alone would not suffice.

Theorem 3.21, applied to the actual pulse-multiplied curvature column, gives its differentiated row strip cost \(CL_i^{- j - 2}\). The original pulse's strip cost simply multiplies the established column cost. To move coarse differences to the right, divide the symbol successively by \(e^{ip_a} - 1\). The division is regular on the real principal cube before summing aliases. On the principal alias use

\(\displaystyle e^{ip_a / L_i} - 1 = \frac{e^{ip_a} - 1}{L_i \text{avg}_{L_i} (p_a / L_i)} .\)

The denominator has a positive lower bound by the principal rail estimate. On a nonprincipal alias \(q = (p + 2 \pi l) / L_i\), the column contains the box filter \(A (q)^J\). The geometric-series identity is

\(\displaystyle (e^{iq_a} - 1) A (q) = (e^{ip_a} - 1) s_a (q),\)

where \(s_a\) is a product of the other coordinate box filters and a phase, divided by \(L_i\). For \(j\) directions use \(j\) box factors. The remaining power \(A^{J - j}\) has nonnegative degree. The remaining multiplier is continuous by the nonprincipal denominator bounds already proved. Thus the full differentiated symbol has a continuous quotient by the product of all coarse differences.

A coefficientwise division also preserves a narrower strip bound. In one coordinate use the exact finite identity

\(\displaystyle \frac{e^{inp_a} - 1}{e^{ip_a} - 1} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \sum_{h = 0}^{n - 1} e^{ihp_a}, & n > 0,\\ - \sum_{h = n}^{- 1} e^{ihp_a}, & n < 0,\\ 0, & n = 0. \end{array} \right.\)

There are \(|n|\) terms with no greater displacement. Reducing width \(\sigma\) to \(\sigma / 2\) costs at most \(2 / \sigma\), since \(|n| e^{\sigma |n| / 2} \leqslant (2 / \sigma) e^{\sigma |n|}\). This divides the symbol minus its value on \(p_a = 0\); that value vanishes by the preceding divisibility. Equality off the coordinate hyperplane extends to it by continuity. Repeat along \(w\); divisibility by the remaining factors survives at each step. There are finitely many words of length at most \(d - 1\), so one narrower strip and one cost work for all. The scalar factor \(L_i^{- j - 2}\) is unchanged. Periodization and the triangle inequality turn this cover bound into an absolute row bound; the sum over the six two-form types contributes their cardinality. This proves (14.78) with a cost chosen before \(L_i\).

Closedness is preserved on all cochains, including harmonic ones. Write the actual step as \(B_f TH_c\), using reciprocal pulse \(B\) and pulse \(H\). Its exterior transport \(C\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle Cd_c B_c = d_f B_f T, \qquad B_c H_c = I.\)

Multiplying by \(H_c\) gives \(d_f \Psi_i = Cd_c\). No global exactness of the input cochain is required. Reality follows from the real cover coefficients.

14.13.2Absolute rows of a product

For \(\ell \leqslant k\) put \(m = k - \ell\), \(\mathcal{L}= \Psi_{k - 1} \cdots \Psi_{\ell}\) and \(S = a_{\ell} / a_k = \prod_{i = \ell}^{k - 1} L_i\). If every \(j\)th difference of \(f\) is bounded by \(v\), iteration gives

\(\displaystyle \| \Delta_w \mathcal{L}f\|_{\infty} \leqslant R^m S^{- j - 2} v.\)

This holds for arbitrary real \(f\). For a specified output row, take \(f (y)\) to be the sign of \(\Delta_w \mathcal{L} (x, y)\), using zero when that entry vanishes. Then \(|f| \leqslant 1\) and every \(j\)th difference has absolute value at most \(2^j\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle S^{j + 2} \sum_y | \Delta_w \mathcal{L}(x, y) | \leqslant R^m 2^d .\) (14.79)

The sign test need not be closed. Reality and preservation of closedness pass separately to the product.

Fix \(b \geqslant 6\) and \(a > 0\). After \(R\), choose a plateau block length \(L > 1\) so that \(4 (b + 2) \log R \leqslant a \log L\). With \(m = \lceil (b + 2) \log H / \log L \rceil\) we have

\(\displaystyle R^m \leqslant RH^{a / 4} \leqslant H^{a / 2}, \qquad R^m 2^d \leqslant H^a\)

eventually. The second inequality absorbs fixed \(2^d\) into \(H^{a / 2}\). Also \(S = L^m \geqslant H^{b + 2}\). The original covariance entries have a fixed bound \(C_J\) uniform in period, by summing the bounded curvature multiplier over its normalized finite Fourier grid. Paying \(C_J \leqslant H^a\), and writing \(P = H^a\), gives

\(\displaystyle S^{j + 2} \sum_y | \Delta_w \mathcal{L}(x, y) | \leqslant P, \qquad | \kappa_{\ell} (y, z) | \leqslant P.\) (14.80)

One finite table of costs is selected before the block length, then its clearance threshold. On the allowed plateau the prescribed number of steps is exactly \(m\). All estimates therefore concern the same chosen level and history, uniformly in period.

14.13.3Mean and covariance after normal ordering

For a real closed coarse cochain \(u\) with \(\|u\|_{\infty} \leqslant P\), set \(\mu =\mathcal{L}u\) and \(B =\mathcal{L} \kappa_{\ell} \mathcal{L}^{\textsf{T}}\). Finite summation gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} S^{j + 2} | \Delta_w \mu (x) | & \leqslant P^2, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.81)} \\ S^{j + 2} S^{h + 2} | \Delta_w^x \Delta_v^y B (x, y) | & \leqslant P^3 . \end{array}\)

For the second inequality bound each covariance entry by \(P\); the remaining double sum factors into the absolute row sums of (14.80). The mean and both covariance arguments are closed, by the original closedness and its preservation.

Use \(r = H^b\), \(a = \lambda / 4\), \(0 < \lambda \leqslant 1 / 4\), and the purchased condition \(S \geqslant rH^2\). For \(j \geqslant 0\), \(r^{j + 2} H^4 \leqslant S^{j + 2}\). Hence the weighted means are at most \(H^{- 4} P^2 \leqslant H^{- 3}\). Retaining only one factor \(H^{- 4}\) for the covariance gives the convenient bound

\(\displaystyle r^{j + h + 4} | \Delta_w^x \Delta_v^y B (x, y) | \leqslant H^{- 4 + 3 \lambda / 4} .\) (14.82)

Fix distinct continuum positions \(x_i\) and their selected rounded lattice centres \(z_i\) at magnification \(r\). Required offsets have subpower size in \(H\), so normalized pair displacements stay in compact sets away from zero. The free covariance and the finitely many derivatives used here are bounded on these sets. For an off-diagonal pair, multiply (14.77) by \(r^{j + h + 4}\). The singular error is at most \(CH / r = CH^{1 - b} \leqslant H^{- 4} / 2\) eventually, since \(b \geqslant 6\). The period error is bounded by a fixed power of \(H\) divided by the lattice side. The selected mesh estimates make it at most \(H^{- 4} / 2\) eventually, uniformly in physical period. Thus the scaled error is at most \(H^{- 4}\), and the scaled covariance is bounded by \(H^{\lambda / 8}\) eventually.

Let \(K^0_{ij}\) be the polynomial test-cochain covariance built from the free jet covariance at \(z_i / r\). For both derivative orders \(j, h \leqslant d - 2\), at the origins its weighted differences differ from the corresponding free curvature derivatives by at most \(H^{- 4}\). Indeed a difference of order \(j\) kills test cochains of degree less than \(j\), and the degree-\(j\) term recovers its homogeneous curvature tensor exactly. Terms of degree \(q > j\) carry \(r^{j - q} \leqslant r^{- 1}\). Expanding the finite covariance matrix in the jet basis bounds the total error by \(C / r \leqslant H^{- 4}\) eventually. The coefficient constants are fixed and the free entries are bounded on the compact configuration set. If one endpoint has order \(d - 1\), its derivative of \(K^0_{ij}\) is zero, since all test cochains have degree at most \(d - 2\). Set \(K^0_{ii} = 0\), in accordance with free normal ordering.

The actual normal-ordered covariance is

\(\displaystyle \Gamma_{ij} (s, t) =\boldsymbol{1}_{i \ne j} \kappa_k (z_i + s, z_j + t) - B (z_i + s, z_j + t) .\) (14.83)

Its diagonal is \(- B\), generally nonzero. At origins with both \(j, h \leqslant d - 2\), the two comparison errors and (14.82) give

\(\displaystyle r^{j + h + 4} | \Delta_w^s \Delta_v^t (\Gamma_{ij} - K^0_{ij}) (0, 0) | \leqslant 2 H^{- 4} + H^{- 4 + 3 \lambda / 4} \leqslant H^{- 3}\)

eventually; only the last term is needed on the diagonal. For \(d = 1\) this range of origin derivatives is empty. At a top-order endpoint the polynomial comparison vanishes and

\(\displaystyle H^{\lambda / 8} + H^{- 4 + 3 \lambda / 4} \leqslant H^{\lambda / 4} = P.\)

These, with the mean and closedness bounds, are precisely the finite-reading estimates with \(P = H^{\lambda / 4}\) and \(E = H^{- 3}\) used in Subsection 14.14. They hold uniformly over the admitted closed real coarse backgrounds. Positive semidefiniteness of \(\Gamma\) is not assumed.

Application must retain the purchased plateau and clearance conditions and the identification of this atlas with the actual reader law. These belong to the common history and reader construction; an arbitrary symmetric model cannot replace that identification.

14.14One coordinate system for the finite Gaussian comparison

The moment estimate in Subsection 14.16 requires a common space containing both the physical jet coordinates and the discarded higher-order information. We construct that space from finite lattice readings. The covariance may be indefinite after normal ordering; only its symmetry is used in this construction.

14.14.1Recovering polynomial curvature coordinates from differences

Write \(\Delta_a f (x) = f (x + e_a) - f (x)\) on \(\mathbb{Z}^4\). A real curvature cochain is specified on positively oriented plaquettes and colour coordinates and extended antisymmetrically to all ordered pairs of axes. We call it closed when

\(\displaystyle \Delta_a f_{bc} + \Delta_b f_{ca} + \Delta_c f_{ab} = 0.\)

For an integer \(d \geqslant 0\), let \(K_d\) index a basis of the radial polynomial connection jets whose curvature degree \(j\) satisfies \(j + 2 \leqslant d\). Its basis element has grade \(g_{\alpha} = j + 2\). Let \(t_{\alpha}\) be its lattice plaquette cochain, obtained by integrating the polynomial connection along each edge and taking its discrete curl. These are the polynomial test cochains of the local section construction. They are closed. Differences of order greater than \(j\) vanish, while order-\(j\) differences at zero are the components of \(j\)! times the homogeneous curvature tensor. Edge integration does not change this highest homogeneous term.

For clarity, the finite sampling argument needs only closedness, not the divergence equation. Given a closed cochain, its order-\(j\) differences at zero form a symmetric derivative tensor. Applying the closedness equation after any \(j - 1\) differences gives the cyclic identity for this tensor. It therefore defines a homogeneous closed polynomial two-form. For \(j = 0\) this assertion just says that the tensor is antisymmetric. The radial primitive is obtained by contracting this two-form with the position vector and dividing by \(j + 2\), as in the local jet construction. Its curl is the given two-form. Thus these highest-order readings are those of a radial polynomial connection jet.

The map from the span of the \(t_{\alpha}\) of curvature degree at least \(q\) to all origin differences of orders \(q, \ldots, d - 2\) is injective. Indeed, take the largest degree with a nonzero coefficient. The differences of that order kill all smaller degrees and recover its full curvature tensor. Vanishing of those readings forces that radial connection jet, hence its basis coefficients, to be zero. Descend through the finite list of degrees. The same descent, now subtracting the recovered top-degree test cochain, shows that the origin readings through order \(d - 2\) of every closed cochain are matched by a unique linear combination of the \(t_{\alpha}\).

Choose a linear left inverse of each of these finite injective sampling maps. For a key \(\alpha\) of curvature degree \(j\), use the left inverse involving only differences of orders at least \(j\). This gives a real functional \(\ell_{\alpha}\) which is a finite linear combination of origin differences, with

\(\displaystyle \ell_{\alpha} (t_{\beta}) = \delta_{\alpha \beta}, \qquad \Pi_d f = \sum_{\alpha \in K_d} \ell_{\alpha} (f) t_{\alpha} .\) (14.84)

For closed \(f\), \(\Pi_d f\) has exactly its origin differences through order \(d - 2\). Compatibility of the different left inverses follows from uniqueness of the full expansion just proved: restriction to orders at least \(j\) deletes all lower-degree test cochains, and the left inverse therefore recovers the same remaining coefficients. The functionals are defined on arbitrary cochains; the matching assertion is only used for closed ones.

Let \(c_{\alpha}\) be the sum of the absolute values of the coefficients of \(\ell_{\alpha}\) in its difference representation, and choose \(C_d \geqslant 1\) with \(c_{\alpha} \leqslant C_d\) for every key. This is a finite constant, selected before any comparison scale or stencil radius.

14.14.2Residual coordinates and their bounds

Fix a finite set \(S\) of addresses, all with lattice distance \(|x|_1 \leqslant M\), where \(M\) is a nonnegative integer, and a radius \(r \geqslant 1\). For a cochain \(f\) define

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} L_{\alpha} f & = r^{g_{\alpha}} \ell_{\alpha} (f), \qquad \alpha \in K_d,\\ L_z f & = r^{d + 1} (f - \Pi_d f) (z), \qquad z \in S. \end{array}\)

The coordinate set is the disjoint union \(K_d \sqcup S\). Every functional \(L\) uses only finitely many values, since a finite difference does so. For every \(z \in S\) there is the exact identity

\(\displaystyle f (z) = \sum_{\alpha \in K_d} r^{- g_{\alpha}} t_{\alpha} (z) L_{\alpha} f + r^{- (d + 1)} L_z f.\) (14.85)

It is simply the definition of \(f - \Pi_d f\) and holds even when \(f\) is not closed.

Put \(q = \max (d - 1, 0)\). Suppose a closed \(f\) has the weighted origin bounds

\(\displaystyle r^{j + 2} | \Delta_w f (0) | \leqslant P \quad (j = |w| \leqslant d - 2)\)

and the weighted top-difference bounds

\(\displaystyle r^{q + 2} | \Delta_w f (x) | \leqslant P \quad (|w| = q, |x|_1 \leqslant M) .\)

All plaquette and colour components are included. Assume \(P \geqslant 0\). For physical coordinates, \(g_{\alpha} \leqslant j + 2\) for every difference entering \(\ell_{\alpha}\), so \(|L_{\alpha} f| \leqslant Pc_{\alpha}\).

For the residual bound set \(h = f - \Pi_d f\). Its origin differences below order \(q\) vanish, and its order-\(q\) differences equal those of \(f\), since \(\Pi_d f\) has degree at most \(d - 2\). If \(d = 0\) or \(1\), the key set is empty and the last assertion just means \(h = f\). An elementary discrete remainder estimate gives

\(\displaystyle |h (z) | \leqslant M^q \sup_{|w| = q, |x|_1 \leqslant M} | \Delta_w h (x) | .\)

To prove it, descend from order \(q\) to zero. At each lower order the relevant difference is zero at the origin. Sum its first differences along a shortest signed coordinate path to \(z\). The path has at most \(M\) edges and stays inside the ball. A negative step uses the forward difference at the new endpoint. Each descent therefore multiplies the bound by at most \(M\). At \(q = 0\) no descent is required, with \(M^0 = 1\). Since \(d + 1 \leqslant q + 2\) and \(r \geqslant 1\), we obtain

\(\displaystyle |L_{\alpha} f| \leqslant PC_d, \qquad |L_z f| \leqslant PM^q .\) (14.86)

If the weighted origin differences instead have bound \(E \geqslant 0\), the physical coordinates satisfy the sharper bound \(EC_d\).

Apply the same argument at both ends of a kernel \(K (z, w)\) whose rows and columns are closed. If its mixed weighted readings of the preceding types are bounded by \(P\), and the origin–origin ones by \(E\), then, for \(T \geqslant \max (C_d, M^q)\),

\(\displaystyle | (L_a \otimes L_b) K| \leqslant PT^2, \qquad | (L_{\alpha} \otimes L_{\beta}) K| \leqslant ET^2 .\) (14.87)

Indeed fix one difference at the first endpoint, divide its bound by its positive weight, and apply (14.86) at the second endpoint. Apply it again at the first. The endpoint operations commute because both are finite linear combinations of evaluations. Closedness is preserved under these finite linear combinations. This justifies both applications, including residual coordinates.

14.14.3The exact free block and the common covariance

Fix a separated configuration of \(n\) insertion points. Let \(F_{i \alpha, j \beta}\) be its free covariance in the radial jet coordinates. This is a real symmetric matrix: the underlying free kernel and the change to these coordinates are real. Normal ordering sets its same-insertion block to zero. Define the reference cochain kernel

\(\displaystyle K^0_{ij} (z, w) = \sum_{\alpha, \beta \in K_d} F_{i \alpha, j \beta} \hspace{0.17em} r^{- g_{\alpha}} t_{\alpha} (z) \hspace{0.17em} r^{- g_{\beta}} t_{\beta} (w) .\)

Its rows and columns are closed. Equation (14.84) shows that its matrix under the \(L\) coordinates is exactly \(F\) on the physical block and zero whenever either coordinate is residual. This is an exact identity, not a small-error statement.

Let \(\Gamma_{ij}\) be the actual symmetric cochain kernel, with closed rows and columns, and \(\mu_i\) its closed actual mean. Suppose the means obey the two bounds of the previous subsection with constants \(P, E\), and \(\Gamma_{ij} - K^0_{ij}\) obeys their two-endpoint versions. Define one enlarged mean and covariance by

\(\displaystyle m_{ia} = L_a \mu_i, \qquad Q_{ia, jb} = (L_a \otimes L_b) \Gamma_{ij} .\)

If \(T \geqslant \max (C_d, M^q)\), their entries satisfy

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} |m_{ia} | \leqslant PT, \qquad |m_{i \alpha} | \leqslant ET,\\ |Q_{ia, jb} - F^{\text{ext}}_{ia, jb} | \leqslant PT^2, \qquad |Q_{i \alpha, j \beta} - F_{i \alpha, j \beta} | \leqslant ET^2, \end{array}\)

where \(F^{\text{ext}}\) has zero residual rows and columns. Consequently, if

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} B \geqslant 1, \quad PT \leqslant B, \quad PT^2 \leqslant B, \quad |F_{i \alpha, j \beta} | + PT^2 \leqslant B, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.88)} \\ ET \leqslant \varepsilon, \qquad ET^2 \leqslant \varepsilon, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.89)} \end{array}\)

all enlarged entries are bounded by \(B\), while the physical block and mean are within \(\varepsilon\) of \((F, 0)\). Applying (14.85) at one and at both endpoints proves that the actual stencil mean and covariance are the linear and bilinear pullbacks of \((m, Q)\). This gives the common pullback required for every slot and every subset in the moment comparison.

Here are the purchases used there. Let \(H \to \infty\) be its monitor, \(0 < \lambda \leqslant 1 / 4\), and set

\(\displaystyle T = P = H^{\lambda / 4}, \quad E = H^{- 3}, \quad B = H^{\lambda}, \quad \varepsilon = H^{- 2} .\)

The stencil radius has subpower growth, so \(M^q \leqslant T\) eventually; also \(C_d \leqslant T\). Then \(PT^2 = H^{3 \lambda / 4} \leqslant B / 2\) eventually, while \(PT \leqslant PT^2\) since \(T \geqslant 1\). The exponents \(- 3 + \lambda / 4\) and \(- 3 + \lambda / 2\) are at most \(- 2\), which proves the two error inequalities. On a compact separated configuration set the finitely many free entries are bounded by continuity of the differentiated kernel; the same bound holds for the rounded configurations eventually. Thus \(|F| \leqslant B / 2\) eventually and all of (14.88) holds.

14.14.4The calibrated polynomial bound

The actual polynomial map on a fixed grade space has a coefficient bound of the form

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{H}u|_1 \leqslant H \hspace{0.17em} | \text{awake} (u) |_1, \qquad | \text{awake} (u) |_1 \leqslant 2 |Tu|_1 .\)

Here \(T\) is its tested coefficient map, and \(\text{awake}\) is the coefficient vector in the retained common normalization. For a target label \(v\) of grade at most \(d\), the calibration equation is

\(\displaystyle D_a Tu = \text{weight}_r \hspace{0.17em} v, \qquad a > 0, \quad a^{- 1} \leqslant 2 H, \quad r = H^b \geqslant 1.\)

\(D_a\) multiplies a basis coordinate by \(a\) to its connection degree; \(\text{weight}_r\) multiplies it by \(r\) to its grade. Let \(q_{\ast}\) be the maximum connection degree in the fixed frame, taking \(q_{\ast} = 0\) if that frame is empty. Coordinatewise inversion gives

\(\displaystyle |Tu|_1 \leqslant (2 H)^{q_{\ast}} r^d |v|_1, \qquad |\mathcal{H}u|_1 \leqslant 2^{q_{\ast} + 1} |v|_1 H^{bd + q_{\ast} + 1} \leqslant H^{bd + q_{\ast} + 2}\)

eventually. The last threshold depends on the fixed target label, and can be taken uniformly on a fixed bounded set of labels. It is uniform in the cutoff and period when the displayed input bounds are uniform. The finite loss \(q_{\ast} + 2\) is chosen before the amplification exponent \(b\).

Finally the weighted norm of the reconstruction coordinate at \(z \in S\) is exactly

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\alpha \in K_d} |t_{\alpha} (z) | + 1.\)

The powers \(r^{- g_{\alpha}}\) and \(r^{- (d + 1)}\) cancel their variable weights. Each \(t_{\alpha}\) is a polynomial of fixed degree at most \(d - 2\), so this expression is at most a fixed multiple of \((1 + M)^d + 1\). It has subpower growth in \(H\). Hence it is eventually at most \(H^{\lambda}\). If the exact calibrated low-grade identity and the fixed polynomial degree bound \(k\) hold, the weighted tail estimate of Subsection 14.16 applies with these very coordinates and this coefficient bound. Choosing \(b \geqslant (q_{\ast} + 2) + 3\) leaves its required factor \(H^{- 2}\).

The construction above proves the coordinate and bound conversion. Its application still requires the actual closed-kernel and mean difference estimates on the selected atlas, and the exact low-grade identity for its polynomial map. Those data must refer to the same readers as the joint Gaussian model. The coordinate conversion does not obtain them from covariance symmetry alone.

14.15A joint Gaussian model for the comparison observables

The leading Gaussian construction assigns a local reference model to an entire finite family of readers at once. It is important that this is one joint law: replacing it by a separate Gaussian sample for each reader would lose the cross-covariances used in the principal comparison. The model is an auxiliary leading law for a fixed block, pose, forest, and tag. It is not a claim that the complete interacting block measure is Gaussian.

14.15.1Fixed data and the common sample space

Fix adjacent coarse and fine levels, a block and its point set, a coarse configuration \(V\), a store of flags, a forest \(F\), and one channel tag \(c\). Fix also the positive scale parameter \(x\), the channel mask parameter \(q\) and width \(d\), the signed transport \(s\), its coarse anchor \(t\), the fine anchor \(o\), and a pose at that anchor. Transport the store, forest, tag, and coarse field together by this same signed map. Denote the transported data by \(A^{\sharp}, F^{\sharp}, c^{\sharp}, V^{\sharp}\). In particular, the transformed tag is the image of \(c\) under the signed channel action; it is not chosen again independently.

Let \(I = \text{In} (x, A, F)\) be the finite set of active fine-link Lie coordinates for the fixed store \(A\) and forest \(F\). Let \(\Pi\) be the centered covariance prescribed by the channel on these input coordinates. The sample space is

\(\displaystyle \Omega =\mathcal{H}_{\text{frozen}} \times \mathbb{R}^I, \qquad d \mu (H, Z) = d \pi_{\text{Haar}} (H) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{\Pi} (Z) . \) (14.90)

Here \(\mathcal{H}_{\text{frozen}}\) is the product of the compact gauge groups on the bonds frozen by the store, with normalized product Haar measure, and \(\gamma_{\Pi}\) is the centered Gaussian measure with covariance matrix \(\Pi\). The two coordinates in (14.90) are independent because the law is a product. The channel covariance and all its tags and masks remain fixed throughout the construction.

For each sample \((H, Z)\), form the channel's local drift \(a (H)\) from the frozen links, using the fixed tube, tag, mask parameter, and width. Convert \(a (H) + gZ\) to the gauge group by the fixed exponential-coordinate chart, then apply the finite-volume local link update to \(V\) while retaining \(H\) on the frozen bonds. Denote this specified update map by \(\textsf{U}_{A, F, V}\). Define the fine configuration

\(\displaystyle V_{\ast} (H, Z) = \textsf{U}_{A, F, V} \hspace{-0.17em} (H, a (H) + gZ), \qquad g = \beta^{- 1 / 2} > 0.\) (14.91)

The crucial point is that \(H, Z\), the channel, and the coarse configuration are shared by every reader. The different readers enter only through their read sets and their local masks.

The chosen pose is a single geometric input at \(o\): on its room the coarse field agrees with one represented direction, after the fixed signed transport, and the room is healthy and clear of the assigned flags. The pose supplies the gauge frame used below. It is selected before the reader index is chosen and is not allowed to vary from one read coordinate to the next. For clarity, the quantitative receipts used by this handoff are these: the channel covariance is positive semidefinite and transforms covariantly under the fixed signed transport; for every admissible pair of incidence coordinates, there are nonnegative sight and common-history errors \(\varepsilon_{\text{sight}}, \varepsilon_{\text{hist}}\) with sum at most \(\varepsilon\); for each reader \(a\), its measurable mask has values in \([0, 1]\) and integral loss at most \(\varepsilon^2\); and, whenever that mask is positive, the same pose room and local comparison provide one gauge element for the whole reader patch with coordinate error at most \(\varepsilon\). The covariance split, Markov bound, and common-sample notation below are proved here. Production of these paid sight, mask, and field-comparison receipts is the separate input supplied by the selected local construction; it is not inferred from positivity of the covariance.

When the surrounding construction averages over its finite list of tag atoms, apply this construction to each atom with its own fixed tag and covariance. Those outer weights are nonnegative and sum to one. The theorem below is uniform over the allowed atoms; it does not identify distinct tagged Gaussian measures with one another.

14.15.2Exact joint law of the transverse coordinates

Let \(\mathcal{I}\) be the fine incidence-coordinate set, and let

\(\displaystyle R : \mathbb{R}^I \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{I}}\)

be the row matrix that reads the transported fine curvature coordinates and restricts them to these incidences. Write \(Z^{\sharp}\) for the Gaussian coordinate after the fixed signed transport, the channel's forest reorganization, and the one pose gauge change. Define the centered reader vector by

\(\displaystyle \eta = RZ^{\sharp} .\) (14.92)

The exact transported channel covariance on the coordinates before the row restriction is denoted \(\Pi^{\sharp}\). The measure-preserving transport laws give

\(\displaystyle Z^{\sharp} \sim \gamma_{\Pi^{\sharp}}, \qquad \eta \sim \gamma_C, \qquad C = R \Pi^{\sharp} R^{\textsf{T}} . \) (14.93)

This includes every cross-entry: for all \(u, v \in \mathcal{I}\),

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E}_{\mu} [\eta_u \eta_v] = \sum_{r, r' \in I} R_{ur} \Pi^{\sharp}_{rr'} R_{vr'} = C_{uv} .\)

The law statement in (14.93), not just its covariance identity, follows because the first transport pushes the product sample law to the transported Gaussian channel and the remaining maps are real-linear. Thus any finite subcollection of readers has the corresponding principal submatrix of \(C\) as its exact joint covariance. No independence among reader coordinates is asserted. The covariance \(C\) may be positive semidefinite and singular; the Gaussian pushforward statement remains valid in that case.

The local estimate compares this matrix with the short reference covariance. Let \(C^{\text{short}}\) be that covariance on the same incidence coordinates. For every pair \(u, v\) in the support of the chosen reader family, the paid sight estimate compares \(C_{uv}\) with the tracked finite-volume covariance \(C^{\text{track}}_{uv}\). The common-history telescoping identity compares the latter with \(C^{\text{short}}_{uv}\). Adding these two differences gives the exact bound

\(\displaystyle |C_{uv} - C^{\text{short}}_{uv} | \leqslant |C_{uv} - C^{\text{track}}_{uv} | + |C^{\text{track}}_{uv} - C^{\text{short}}_{uv} | \leqslant \varepsilon .\) (14.94)

The two terms are bounded by \(\varepsilon_{\text{sight}}\) and \(\varepsilon_{\text{hist}}\), respectively, and their sum is at most \(\varepsilon\). The common-history identity and room-separation conditions are required for both coordinates. In particular, (14.94) holds for every pair of admissible read coordinates in the full family, not just for its diagonal entries.

14.15.3Simultaneous field comparison on one sample space

For a fine reader patch \(a\), let \(J_a\) be the chamber-coordinate set read by that patch inside the prescribed room. A patch is admissible when its reader support is contained in the coarse block tag. Its mask \(\Gamma_a : \Omega \to [0, 1]\) depends on the same frozen field and channel as (14.90). The construction gives

\(\displaystyle \int_{\Omega} (1 - \Gamma_a) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \leqslant \varepsilon^2 .\) (14.95)

By Markov's inequality,

\(\displaystyle \mu \{\Gamma_a = 0\} \leqslant \varepsilon^2, \qquad \mu \{\Gamma_a \leqslant 1 - \delta\} \leqslant \frac{\varepsilon^2}{\delta} \quad (0 < \delta \leqslant 1) .\)

The statement is per reader; it does not take a union over the reader family. When \(\Gamma_a (H, Z) > 0\), the local guard places the sample in the healthy room where its field coordinates can be compared.

Write \(\text{Basis}_a (W)\) for the local chamber basis at patch \(a\), and define the complex gauged read by

\(\displaystyle \text{Read}_{a, j} (W ; q) := \left[ \text{rigid} \hspace{-0.17em} (\text{coloring} (q, \text{Basis}_a (W))) \right]_j .\)

Thus the read is a complex coordinate after applying the patch basis and one gauge frame. Let \(\textsf{B}_{\text{parent}} (U)\) denote the coarse chamber basis constructed once from the fixed parent manner, coarse configuration \(U\), and reference tag. It is common to all fine patches; each set \(J_a\) only restricts its coordinates. Let \(\textsf{N}_{H_i}\) be the normal-read map in the fixed reference chamber frame \(H_i\). The center is the actual linear forecast

\(\displaystyle m_{a, j} := \text{Re} [\textsf{N}_{H_i} (\text{rigid} (\textsf{B}_{\text{parent}} (U)))]_j, \qquad j \in J_a .\)

It is the real part of the normal read in the reference frame, not an untransformed coordinate of \(U\). On every guarded sample there is one gauge element \(q_a (H, Z)\) for the whole patch such that

\(\displaystyle | \text{Read}_{a, j} (V_{\ast} (H, Z) ; q_a (H, Z)) - (m_{a, j} + \eta_j (H, Z)) | \leqslant \varepsilon, \qquad j \in J_a .\) (14.96)

The frame may depend on the reader and on the sample, but within one reader it is shared by all of that reader's coordinates. The same random vector \(\eta\) from (14.93) occurs for every patch, and the center \(m_{a, j}\) is the actual coarse reader value. There is no separate sampling or re-centering for each \(j\).

The geometry behind (14.96) is as follows. The fine read region is first rewritten in the common coarse frame; the block room condition places its whole stencil inside the pose room. The paid local guard then supplies the healthy-region condition. Applying the field comparison on that room produces the one gauge element \(q_a (H, Z)\) and the coordinate estimate for each \(j\) in the patch. The reader's mask is what localizes this statement: outside its support no coordinate comparison is claimed. This is exactly the scope needed for bounded chamber-local insertions.

The law (14.90), the covariance statements (14.93)–(14.94), the guard estimate (14.95), and the coordinate comparison (14.96) are the joint reader model. They are the finite-dimensional input used by the subsequent Gaussian density comparison, with a fixed measurable masked insertion. That comparison is proved in Subsection 9.3; it derives the polynomial density-jet bound and never differentiates the insertion. The Gaussian reassembly in Subsection 8.29 separately proves that the absolutely summable activity expansion restores the original block integral. This auxiliary reader model alone does not assert that identity. Finally, the response billing in Subsection 18.8 uses one uniform derivative and tail envelope over all allowed leaves and reader directions. The present section supplies the common-law and same-insertion identification required to apply those comparison and reassembly results; their primitive mean, covariance-jet, cutoff, and packet estimates remain those stated in the cited sections.

14.16From calibrated Gaussian moments to cumulants

The marked comparison uses all subsets of a finite insertion word. Its Gaussian calculation must therefore control products before taking the finite logarithm. We give the degree-dependent estimates with the powers used in the scale purchases. The covariance and calibration hypotheses are stated explicitly; their common-atlas construction is needed when this calculation is applied to the selected history.

14.16.1A dimension-independent polynomial estimate

For a polynomial \(p\) in finitely many commuting variables, write \(|p|_1\) for the sum of the absolute values of its coefficients. It is submultiplicative: expand a product, apply the triangle inequality to each coefficient, and then sum over the two original monomial indices. Let \(\mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu}\) be the Wick evaluation with symmetric covariance \(Q\) and mean \(\mu\). No positivity is needed in this algebraic calculation. The defining recurrence, for a product of \(j\) coordinate variables, is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu} (X_{i_1} \cdots X_{i_j}) = \mu_{i_1} \mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu} (X_{i_2} \cdots X_{i_j}) + \sum_{\ell = 2}^j Q_{i_1 i_{\ell}} \mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu} \left( \prod_{a \ne 1, \ell} X_{i_a} \right),\) (14.97)

with value one on the empty product. Repeated coordinate indices are allowed: their positions in this recurrence remain distinct.

Assume \(B \geqslant 1\), \(| \mu_i | \leqslant B\) and \(|Q_{ij} | \leqslant B\) for every coordinate used. Define \(C_d (B) = [2 B (d + 1)]^d\), including \(C_0 (B) = 1\). Then every polynomial of degree at most \(d\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu} (p) | \leqslant |p|_1 C_d (B) .\) (14.98)

If a second pair \((Q', \mu')\) obeys the same bounds and \(|Q_{ij} - Q'_{ij} |, | \mu_i - \mu'_i | \leqslant \varepsilon\), then

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu} (p) -\mathcal{W}_{Q', \mu'} (p) | \leqslant |p|_1 \varepsilon C_d (B) .\) (14.99)

Here \(\varepsilon \geqslant 0\) can be arbitrary.

We prove both estimates on monomials by simultaneous induction on the degree ceiling \(d\). At degree zero their assertions are respectively \(1 \leqslant 1\) and \(0 \leqslant \varepsilon\). For a nonempty monomial of degree at most \(d + 1\), the recurrence has one mean term and at most \(d\) covariance terms. Every remaining product has degree at most \(d\). Thus the first bound is at most \((d + 1) BC_d (B) \leqslant C_{d + 1} (B)\). For a difference of terms use \(ab - a' b' = a (b - b') + (a - a') b'\). The induction bounds its absolute value by \(B \varepsilon C_d (B) + \varepsilon C_d (B)\), at most \(2 B \varepsilon C_d (B)\). Summing at most \(d + 1\) such terms gives \(2 B (d + 1) \varepsilon C_d (B) \leqslant \varepsilon C_{d + 1} (B)\). The inequalities follow from \(C_d (B) \leqslant [2 B (d + 2)]^d\). The empty monomial satisfies both bounds at every degree ceiling. Finally expand \(p\) in monomials and sum their absolute coefficients. This proves (14.98) and (14.99) without a factor depending on the number of coordinate variables.

14.16.2Calibrated vertices and their products

Fix \(n\) insertion slots, a common polynomial degree bound \(k\), and possibly different grades \(g_i\) for the slots. Here \(n, k, g_i\) are nonnegative integers, and all error parameters \(\xi, \varepsilon\) are nonnegative. After substituting the actual linear reader map, write \(H_i\) for the polynomial of slot \(i\). Let \(F_i\) be its intended free polynomial, embedded in the physical coordinates of the same enlarged variable space. Suppose the grade truncation of \(H_i\) through grade \(g_i\) is exactly \(F_i\). Every variable has a nonnegative integer grade. For \(r \geqslant 1\), write

\(\displaystyle |p|_{1, r} = \sum_{\alpha} |p_{\alpha} | r^{\text{grade} (\alpha)} .\)

Every monomial of \(H_i - F_i\) has grade at least \(g_i + 1\), whence

\(\displaystyle r^{g_i + 1} |H_i - F_i |_1 \leqslant |H_i |_{1, r} .\) (14.100)

This uses the exact low-grade identity, so no difference of two large low-grade coefficients is being estimated.

Suppose the incoming polynomial has coefficient norm at most \(a_i\) and degree at most \(k\), and every substituted linear coordinate has weighted norm at most \(b \geqslant 1\). Expanding each monomial and using submultiplicativity gives \(|H_i |_{1, r} \leqslant a_i b^k\). Thus the purchase

\(\displaystyle a_i b^k \leqslant \xi r^{g_i + 1}\)

implies \(|H_i - F_i |_1 \leqslant \xi\). Also assume \(|F_i |_1 \leqslant A\), where \(A \geqslant 1\). Then \(|H_i |_1 \leqslant A + \xi\). For a subset \(I\) of slots, telescope the product one factor at a time. Every summand contains one difference \(H_i - F_i\) and \(|I| - 1\) factors whose norm is at most \(A + \xi\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \left| \prod_{i \in I} H_i - \prod_{i \in I} F_i \right|_1 \leqslant |I| \xi (A + \xi)^{|I| - 1} \leqslant n \xi (A + \xi)^n .\) (14.101)

For \(I = \varnothing\) the difference is zero. Polynomial degrees are at most \(nk\), both before and after substitution and truncation.

The actual reader covariance and mean must be the pullbacks of a common pair \((Q, \mu)\) on the enlarged variable space. Substitution then commutes with Wick evaluation. To verify this, apply (14.97) to products of substituted linear forms. Their means are the linear pullbacks of \(\mu\) and their pairings are the bilinear pullbacks of \(Q\). Induction on the number of factors gives the identity on monomials; linearity gives it on all polynomials. This establishes the needed identity without changing Gaussian variables separately for each slot.

Assume all entries of \((Q, \mu)\) are bounded by \(B \geqslant 1\), and its restriction to the physical coordinates is within \(\varepsilon\) of the free covariance with zero mean, whose entries are also bounded by \(B\). The \(F_i\) involve only these physical coordinates. Insert and subtract the Wick evaluation of \(\prod_{i \in I} F_i\) at \((Q, \mu)\). Equations (14.98)–(14.101) give, for every subset \(I\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathcal{W}_{Q, \mu} \left( \prod_{i \in I} H_i \right) -\mathcal{W}_{\text{free}} \left( \prod_{i \in I} F_i \right) \right| \leqslant \{n \xi (A + \xi)^n + A^n \varepsilon\} \hspace{0.17em} C_{nk} (B) .\) (14.102)

The unused residual coordinates do not enter the second term. They do enter the first term, and their bounds in \(B\) cannot be omitted.

14.16.3The scale powers that pay for the estimate

Put \(H = \exp ((\log \log s)^2)\) for the diverging monitor along the comparison scales, and choose

\(\displaystyle \lambda = \frac{1}{4 (nk + k + 1)}, \qquad B = H^{\lambda}, \qquad \xi = \varepsilon = H^{- 2} .\)

The parameter \(s\) is taken sufficiently large that the logarithms are defined and \(H \geqslant 1\). Since \(\lambda nk \leqslant 1 / 4\),

\(\displaystyle C_{nk} (B) \leqslant [2 (nk + 1)]^{nk} H^{1 / 4} .\)

The right side of (14.102) is therefore at most

\(\displaystyle [2 (nk + 1)]^{nk} \{n (A + 1)^n + A^n \} H^{- 7 / 4} \leqslant H^{- 3 / 2}\)

for all sufficiently large \(s\). The last threshold depends on the fixed word and degree bound. It is uniform over the finite volume and replicas once the preceding covariance and vertex hypotheses hold uniformly.
