For completeness, the grade-tail purchase follows from the same powers. If \(r = H^b\), \(a_i \leqslant H^{bg_i + E}\), the linear-coordinate substitution cost is at most \(H^{\lambda}\), and \(b \geqslant 0\), \(b \geqslant E + 3\), then

\(\displaystyle a_i (H^{\lambda})^k \leqslant H^{bg_i + E + 1 / 4} \leqslant H^{- 2} H^{b (g_i + 1)} .\)

The first inequality uses \(\lambda k \leqslant 1 / 4\); the second follows from \(E + 1 / 4 \leqslant b - 2\). Thus every grade is treated at its own truncation level while the arity and polynomial degree determine the common Gaussian growth allowance.

14.16.4Taking the finite logarithm

Use the squarefree algebra \(\mathscr{A}_{\{1, \ldots, n\}}\) from Subsection 14.1. For a polynomial \(q\) with constant coefficient one, define

\(\displaystyle \log_{\ast} q = \sum_{j = 1}^n \frac{(- 1)^{j + 1}}{j} (q - 1)^j .\)

Formal power-series identities truncated after degree \(n\) give \(\exp_{\ast} (\log_{\ast} q) = q\) and \(\log_{\ast} (\exp_{\ast} P) = P\) for \(P\) with zero constant coefficient. These identities can also be checked coefficientwise: all powers above \(n\) vanish, so the usual one-variable formal identities in the nilpotent variable \(q - 1\) are finite identities here. In particular the cumulant polynomial is uniquely determined by the moment polynomial.

We give an explicit local stability bound. Use the coefficient \(\ell^1\) norm on this algebra. If \(|q - 1|_1, |q' - 1|_1 \leqslant M\), the identity \(u^j - v^j = \sum_{a = 0}^{j - 1} u^a (u - v) v^{j - 1 - a}\) yields

\(\displaystyle | \log_{\ast} q - \log_{\ast} q' |_1 \leqslant \left( \sum_{j = 1}^n M^{j - 1} \right) |q - q' |_1 .\)

If the coefficientwise moment error is at most \(H^{- 3 / 2}\), then \(|q - q' |_1 \leqslant 2^n H^{- 3 / 2}\). The free moment polynomial \(q'\) varies continuously on the compact comparison window, which is contained in the distinct-point configuration domain, so its coefficient norm is bounded. The moment error then bounds \(|q - 1|_1\) as well. Consequently there is a constant \(C\) for this fixed word and window such that

\(\displaystyle | \log_{\ast} q - \log_{\ast} q' |_1 + H^{- 3 / 2} \leqslant (C + 1) H^{- 3 / 2} \leqslant H^{- 31 / 32}\) (14.103)

for all sufficiently large \(s\). Each coefficient of the logarithm difference has modulus at most the coefficient \(\ell^1\) norm. The factor \(2^n\) is absorbed only after \(n\) is fixed.

On the actual intermediate Gaussian history, let \(\mathcal{S}_{l, k}\) be the successive linear substitution and Gaussian integration operator from level \(k\) to level \(l\). Define the moment polynomial by

\(\displaystyle q (z) = \sum_{I \subseteq \{1, \ldots, n\}} z_I \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{S}_{l, k} \left( \prod_{i \in I} P_i \right) (y) .\)

Its constant coefficient is one, since every substitution and normalized Gaussian integration preserves one. The connected polynomial obtained by the finite partition formula is \(\log_{\ast} q\): expanding the displayed finite logarithm gives that partition formula directly, with coefficient \((- 1)^{j - 1} (j - 1)\)! for a partition into \(j\) nonempty blocks. Evaluation at the fixed intermediate field \(y\) commutes with this finite polynomial formula. This is the exact cumulant used in the planted comparison. Therefore the moment estimate for the same reader covariance and mean implies (14.103) for it.

If the planted local coefficient differs from this cumulant by at most \(H^{- 3 / 2}\), its sum with the compensating negative free cumulant is bounded by \(H^{- 31 / 32}\), by (14.103) and the triangle inequality. Signed copies and complex conjugation preserve these absolute coefficient bounds. This is the small initial remainder needed for the subsequent marked propagation. Its use still requires the local planted-coefficient error, the exact low-grade calibration, and the common reader covariance estimates at the displayed powers; they are not consequences of finite-dimensional logarithm continuity.

14.17The healthy-field comparison at the middle scale

The Gaussian comparison must approximate the actual local coefficient on each healthy configuration. We prove this by constructing one closed real curvature cochain from that configuration, and by comparing the actual polynomial transport with the transport of its covariance atlas. Both comparisons are simultaneous for every subset of the insertion word.

14.17.1A closed cochain from a healthy gauge field

Work on a lattice of integer side \(N_{\text{per}} > 0\), with a patch of integer radius \(B \geqslant 0\) about \(o\), and assume \(2 (B + 1) < N_{\text{per}}\). Let the field be healthy at threshold \(\theta \geqslant 0\) on that patch. Put it in the axial gauge based at \(o\). The path construction expresses every link in the room as a product of at most \(4 B\) conjugated plaquette holonomies. The chord of a product is at most the sum of the chords, so each such link has chord at most \(4 B \theta\). Let \(c > 0\) be the actual scale normalization. Define a Lie-algebra-valued one-cochain \(A\) by taking \(c^{- 1}\) times the curvature coordinate of this gauge-fixed link inside the room, and zero outside it. Define the two-cochain \(X = dA\) on the whole torus, and take its real colour coordinates. Then

\(\displaystyle dX = 0, \qquad \|X\|_{\infty} \leqslant 16 B \theta / c.\) (14.104)

The first assertion is the exact discrete identity \(d^2 = 0\), also across the artificial boundary of the room. The second follows by summing the four link bounds in each plaquette curl.

Let \(Y\) be \(c^{- 1}\) times the actual curvature of the gauge-fixed plaquette. For a plaquette whose base is at distance at most \(B - 2\) from \(o\), all four edges lie inside the room. The curvature map satisfies the product estimate

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{C}(ab) -\mathcal{C}(a) -\mathcal{C}(b)\| \leqslant c_G \text{chord} (a) \text{chord} (b), \qquad \mathcal{C} (a^{- 1}) = -\mathcal{C} (a) .\)

Applying it successively to the four factors of the plaquette, with their inverse orientations, costs \(1 + 2 + 3 = 6\) products of link bounds. Hence

\(\displaystyle |Y_{\nu} - X_{\nu} | \leqslant 6 c_G (4 B \theta)^2 / c\) (14.105)

for every colour coordinate in the interior stencil. This is an estimate for the same gauged field, without sampling a new field. Translations and signed coordinate changes transport the construction and preserve these absolute bounds.

At the selected middle scale the normalization satisfies \(\theta / c \leqslant \tau (t)\), where \(t\) is its coupling and \(\tau (t) = 3 \hspace{0.17em} \text{hot} (t)\). The upper comparison coupling is \(s\), with \(s / 2 \leqslant t \leqslant s\). Write \(H = \exp ((\log \log s)^2)\), taking \(s\) large enough that \(H \geqslant 1\), and fix \(0 < \lambda \leqslant 1 / 4\). The purchased numerical bounds are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} 16 B \tau (t) & \leqslant H^{\lambda / 4}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.106)} \\ 6 c_G (4 B)^2 \theta \tau (t) & \leqslant \varepsilon, \qquad \varepsilon = H^{- v}, \end{array}\)

where \(v\) is selected before the final coupling threshold. They are feasible uniformly in this interval: the patch radius and \(\tau\) grow slower than every power of \(H\), whereas \(\theta = t^{- 1 / 2} \text{hot} (t)\) decays faster than every negative power of \(H\). For example, taking logarithms compares \(- \tfrac{1}{2} \log t + o (\log t)\) with any fixed multiple of \((\log \log s)^2\); \(t \geqslant s / 2\) makes this uniform. Thus (14.104) supplies exactly a closed real background admitted by the covariance comparison, while (14.105) is at most \(\varepsilon\).

14.17.2Comparing two polynomial transports

A polynomial step consists of a finite linear substitution followed by Wick contraction. Suppose the coefficient sum of every substituted coordinate and every covariance entry is at most \(C \geqslant 1\). For degree at most \(D\), put \(A_D = [2 (D + 2) C]^D\). The Wick recurrence used in Subsection 14.16 applies equally when its mean is a linear polynomial. Submultiplicativity of coefficient sums then proves that the step preserves degree and has norm at most \(A_D\) on this finite-degree space. If a second step has the same bounds and each linear row and covariance entry differs by at most \(\varepsilon \geqslant 0\), the same induction, subtracting products one factor at a time, bounds the difference operator by \(\varepsilon A_D\). The row error here is a coefficient sum, not an entrywise error followed by an unbounded sum over sites. In the application below, purchase this per-step discrepancy at most the same \(\varepsilon = H^{- v}\) that bounds the field-coordinate error. These are two separate purchases; a bound for one does not imply the other.

For \(m\) successive steps \(T_i, T'_i\), expand

\(\displaystyle T_m \cdots T_1 - T'_m \cdots T'_1 = \sum_{j = 1}^m T'_m \cdots T'_{j + 1} (T_j - T'_j) T_{j - 1} \cdots T_1 .\)

Thus, for \(\deg p \leqslant D\),

\(\displaystyle |T_m \cdots T_1 p - T'_m \cdots T'_1 p|_1 \leqslant |p|_1 m \varepsilon A_D^m .\) (14.107)

We apply this to the actual polynomial history and the history read from its covariance atlas. Their per-step common bounds and errors are the same ones used in the marked Gaussian reader estimates.

All degree bounds \(D, K, D_{\ast}\) and the insertion count \(n\) below are nonnegative integers. Let \(p_i\) be the calibrated incoming polynomials, \(i = 1, \ldots, n\), with a fixed exponent \(z \geqslant 0\), with \(|p_i |_1 \leqslant H^z\) and degree at most \(K\). Every subset product has degree at most \(nK\) and norm at most \(H^{nz}\). Choose the block length after \(A_{nK}\) so that

\(\displaystyle 2 (b + 2) \log A_{nK} \leqslant \log L.\)

For the prescribed number \(m = \lceil (b + 2) \log H / \log L \rceil\) of steps, this gives \(A_{nK}^m \leqslant H\) eventually; also \(m \leqslant H\) eventually. Therefore all transported subset moments have norm at most \(R = H^{nz + 1}\), and their differences have norm at most \(RH \varepsilon\).

For a nonempty subset \(I\), its connected polynomial is

\(\displaystyle c_I = \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (I)} (- 1)^{| \pi | - 1} (| \pi | - 1) ! \prod_{B \in \pi} q_B,\)

where \(q_B\) is the transported moment polynomial. There are finitely many set partitions. Telescoping the product for each partition bounds the difference of the two connected polynomials by

\(\displaystyle |c_I - c'_I |_1 \leqslant D_n (R) RH \varepsilon, \quad D_n (R) = \sum_{\varnothing \ne I \subseteq \{1, \ldots, n\}} \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (I)} | \pi | !R^{| \pi | - 1} .\) (14.108)

Both have degree at most a fixed number \(D_{\ast}\), and coefficient norm at most a fixed polynomial \(Q_n (R)\) in \(R\), obtained by the same partition expansion. These constants depend on the finite word, not on the lattice period.

14.17.3Evaluation and the initial connected error

For a polynomial \(p\) of degree at most \(D_{\ast}\), two real coordinate vectors bounded by \(M \geqslant 1\) on its variables, and coordinate error at most \(\varepsilon\), monomial telescoping gives

\(\displaystyle |p (Y) - p (X) | \leqslant |p|_1 D_{\ast} M^{D_{\ast}} \varepsilon .\)

The sharper power \(M^{D_{\ast} - 1}\) is available for positive degree; the displayed bound also covers degree zero. Moreover \(|p (X) | \leqslant |p|_1 M^{D_{\ast}}\). The healthy-field construction and its numerical purchases bound both vectors by \(M = H\) on the required interior stencil. Using (14.108) therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} |c_I (Y) - c'_I (X) | & \leqslant Q_n (R) D_{\ast} H^{D_{\ast}} \varepsilon + D_n (R) RH^{D_{\ast} + 1} \varepsilon . \end{array}\)

The prefactor is bounded by a fixed power \(H^a\), with a fixed multiplicative constant. Choose \(v > a + 3 / 2\) before the final coupling threshold. The right side is then at most \(H^{- 3 / 2}\) eventually. This is the actual healthy local-coefficient error required by the cumulant comparison. The support estimates of the marked propagation put every variable used here at distance at most \(B - 2\) from the chosen base; no estimate outside the healthy stencil has been used.

The atlas connected polynomial \(c'_I (X)\) is the finite logarithm of its jointly evaluated Gaussian moments. The exact common-coordinate and calibration construction, followed by the actual covariance bounds, compares those moments with the free moments with error \(H^{- 3 / 2}\). Subsection 14.16 consequently gives

\(\displaystyle |c'_I (X) - c_I^{\text{free}} | + H^{- 3 / 2} \leqslant H^{- 31 / 32}\)

eventually. Adding the healthy local error proves the prescribed small remainder against the negative free connected coefficient. The coordinate \(X\) is common to all subsets, so this step does not mix separately chosen background realizations.

At the upper comparison level the same construction starts with singletons only. The transport has zero length there and is the identity. Hence its moment polynomial is \(\prod_i (1 + z_i p_i)\) in the squarefree marker algebra, and its finite logarithm is \(\sum_i z_i p_i\). Every connected coefficient with at least two markers vanishes. The principal singleton is the masked raw polynomial constructed from the calibrated near list. Its identification with the actual birth uses the exact decomposition (16.68) and the selected-entry identity (16.70): the supported approximation error, the mask correction, the wide list and the original forced terms are all retained. The scalar unit in the original list is unmasked. Thus the polynomial is not substituted for the whole calibrated coefficient by an equality. The algebraic reindexing adds zero higher principal coefficients and, with these remainder terms, leaves the pushed density unchanged. Shrinking the marker radius preserves regular and forced bounds, since every marker monomial acquires a smaller nonnegative weight. This identifies the top initialization used in the all-arity marked propagation with the actual calibrated insertion.

The remaining inputs in applying this chapter are the actual mixed compiler's per-step row and covariance comparison, the terminal paid insertion bound and the common purchases. The argument proves the healthy-field and polynomial comparison from those precise receipts; it does not produce them by changing the selected history.

14.18Realigning and propagating the marked comparison

The finite polynomial comparison must survive each block integration. At an intermediate level the marked expansion contains the desired principal insertion, a small regular remainder, and a forced remainder. We explain how its leading pieces are reassigned to the prescribed supports and how the resulting errors accumulate. The identities use the actual representation at that level.

14.18.1Why a connected principal component stays in one cell

Let \(\rho \geqslant 0\) be an elementary slice radius. Each elementary principal piece has a nonempty set of marked reader sites; each such reader belongs to its full support, and the full support is within distance \(\rho\) of every one of those readers. Assume distinct replica cells have reader separation strictly greater than \(2 \rho\), and

\(\displaystyle (3 + 2 n) \rho \leqslant dB.\)

Consider a connected component in the graph joining elementary pieces whose full supports intersect. Two marked readers on the same piece are at distance at most \(\rho\), since a reader lies in that piece's support. Readers on two adjacent pieces have distance at most \(2 \rho\), by using a point in the intersection. Both bounds are at most \(2 \rho\), so the readers belong to the same replica cell. Following a path of intersecting pieces shows that all marked readers of the component belong to one cell.

There are at most \(n\) distinct marker labels in that cell. Make a graph on the labels used by the component, joining labels appearing on the same or on adjacent pieces. The preceding support path gives a walk between any two of these labels. Erase cycles from the walk. The remaining simple path uses at most \(n - 1\) edges, each of geometric length at most \(2 \rho\). Thus the distance between any two marked readers is at most \(2 n \rho\). Every point in the component's full support is at distance at most \(\rho\) from one of its readers, so it is at distance at most \((2 n + 1) \rho \leqslant dB\) from every marked reader of the component. Its marker diameter is also at most \(dB\). This proves the required enlarged-support bounds independently of how many elementary pieces the component contains.

For the selected construction the elementary slice radius is \(\rho = 3 B\). The purchase \(6 n + 3 \leqslant a / (3 + 2 n)\) gives the final distance bound \((2 n + 1) \rho = (6 n + 3) B \leqslant aB / (3 + 2 n)\); it does not construct the slices. In particular it supplies the bound \(aB\) required for the enlarged full supports in the regrouping below. The selected replica separation is stronger than \(2 \rho\). Subsequent regrouping of pure principal components preserves their full supports and marker unions, so the same argument applies to the principal connected logarithm.

14.18.2Marker bounds and spatial bounds

Fix \(n \geqslant 1\) marks in each replica cell. Every principal piece has a nonempty marker subset \(I \subseteq \{1, \ldots, n\}\) and a prescribed spatial support \(X_{c, I}\) in cell \(c\). The geometric hypotheses are the following: pieces with the same marker mode have a unique prescribed support; each actual principal support is contained in that support; and two prescribed supports containing the same spatial site belong to the same replica cell. These statements are made for full inspected supports, including the masks. Let

\(\displaystyle \text{chg}_e (X) = \exp (e \text{cov}_B (X)), \qquad \text{chg}_e (X_{c, I}) \leqslant R_e,\)

where \(e \geqslant 0\) is the support exponent, \(B\) the current radius and \(\text{cov}_B\) its covering number. Use one constant marker weight \(\omega > 0\) at every mark, of every cell, replica and period. The weight of a mode \(I\) is \(\omega^{|I|}\). We do not assume \(\omega \leqslant 1\); in the application below \(\omega \geqslant 1\). Since \(I \subseteq \{1, \ldots, n\}\), every mode satisfies

\(\displaystyle \omega^{|I|} \leqslant \bar{\omega}_n, \qquad \bar{\omega}_n = \max (1, \omega)^n .\) (14.109)

For \(\omega \leqslant 1\) this is the bound \(\omega^{|I|} \leqslant 1\).

If each prescribed piece has weighted coefficient bound at most \(Z\), its total incidence bound at a fixed mark \((c, j)\) is at most \(2^n Z\). Indeed only subsets \(I\) of that same cell can contain \((c, j)\), and there are at most \(2^n\) subsets. This estimate is independent of the number of replica cells.

A second estimate converts incidence bound to spatial bound. Suppose actual pieces \(s_a\) have nonnegative bounds \(p_a\) and nonempty marker supports, and the total bound of pieces containing any fixed mark is at most \(N\). Assign piece \(a\) to its prescribed support \(X_{c (a), I (a)}\). At a spatial site \(v\), if any such support contains \(v\), all such supports lie in one cell \(c\). Charge each summand to one of its nonempty marks and then overcount by summing over every mark in that cell. It follows that

\(\displaystyle \sum_{a : \hspace{0.17em} v \in X_{c (a), I (a)}} p_a \text{chg}_e (X_{c (a), I (a)}) \leqslant nNR_e .\) (14.110)

This is why the spatial charge is proportional to \(n\), while the number of principal subset types is bounded by \(2^n\).

14.18.3Replacing the leading pieces on their prescribed supports

Write the current regular source as a sum of remainder pieces \(H_a\) and leading pieces \(S_b\). Let \(F_{c, I}\) be the desired principal source at mode \((c, I)\), with support \(X_{c, I}\). Group the \(S_b\) by this marker mode and define on its healthy region

\(\displaystyle L_{c, I} (U) = \sum_{b : \hspace{0.17em} \text{mode} (S_b) = (c, I)} S_b (U) - F_{c, I} (U) .\)

Assume \(|L_{c, I} (U) | \leqslant y\) there. Attach the full support \(X_{c, I}\) to \(L_{c, I}\) and apply its healthy mask. The incidence bound of these new regular remainders is at most

\(\displaystyle 2^n \bar{\omega}_n R_e y.\) (14.111)

Indeed the new piece at mode \((c, I)\) has value at most \(y\) on its healthy region, support charge at most \(R_e\) and marker weight \(\omega^{|I|}\), so its weighted bound is at most \(\omega^{|I|} R_e y \leqslant \bar{\omega}_n R_e y\) by (14.109). The preceding subset count applies.

The mask change also has a forced part. A leading piece may be healthy on its smaller support while its prescribed larger support is not healthy. Split its original masked value into its value on the larger healthy region and the complementary part. The latter is supported where the enlarged support contains a flag. Insert these complementary pieces into the forced alphabet, keeping their original marker modes. The identity is an identity of functions on every field: on the larger healthy region it is the defining equality for \(L_{c, I}\); on its complement the difference has just been assigned to the forced part. Apply the exact regular/forced regrouping of Subsection 8.32 to the enlarged supports. No division by an activity value is involved.

Here is its quantitative form. Suppose the previous forced spatial bound is \(s\), the leading incidence bound is \(Z\), and the new forced inflation exponent is \(g \geqslant 0\). If

\(\displaystyle e^g s + nZR_{e + 1} \leqslant \delta, \qquad (2 B + 1)^4 \delta \leqslant 1,\) (14.112)

then the regrouped forced bound is at most

\(\displaystyle e^{- g} (e^{\delta} - 1) .\) (14.113)

Here is the proof for the marker-valued factors. Work in the commutative algebra generated by the marker variables \(z_j\) with \(z_j^2 = 0\). For \(I\) a subset of markers write \(z_I = \prod_{j \in I} z_j\). The norm \(\| \sum_I a_I z_I \|_{\omega} = \sum_I |a_I | \omega^{|I|}\) is submultiplicative for every \(\omega > 0\): a pair of monomials with overlapping marker sets multiplies to zero, and otherwise, for disjoint \(I, J\), the weight \(\omega^{|I \cup J|} = \omega^{|I|} \omega^{|J|}\) is the product of their weights. No bound \(\omega \leqslant 1\) is used. Every moved piece is a single \(\ell_j = z_{I_j} h_j\) with \(I_j \ne \varnothing\). Thus \(\ell_j^2 = 0\) and \(\exp (\ell_j) = 1 + \ell_j\). The mask difference defining \(h_j\) vanishes whenever the enlarged support is healthy, so a nonzero moved factor has a flag in its full support.

Expand the exponential of the sum of the moved pieces as the product of the factors \(1 + \ell_j\) and multiply by the old forced gas. In a nonzero summand each moved factor meets an old forced support: its flag is covered by the old forced inventories. Taking connected components of the full-support intersection graph and flattening them back therefore gives the bijection of Subsection 8.32. This argument preserves the inventory and the product in the marker algebra. Overlapping marker sets contribute zero on both sides. The expansion is justified by absolute convergence in the weighted norm, since the constituent bounds are summable on the finite spatial lattice.

Assign inflated weights \(e^g p_i\) to old forced pieces of bound \(p_i\) and unchanged weights to the moved pieces. At exponent \(e + 1\) the site sum is at most \(\Gamma = e^g s + nZR_{e + 1}\) by (14.110). Every surviving connected component contains an old forced piece, so its product norm is bounded by \(e^{- g}\) times the product of the inflated weights. The distinct connected-set bound (7.35), with reserve one and counting factor \((2 B + 1)^4\), bounds the new site bound at exponent \(e\) by \(e^{- g} (e^{\Gamma} - 1)\). Since \(\Gamma \leqslant \delta\), this proves (14.113). The regrouping does not change the archived background: setting all markers to zero kills every moved factor, leaving the original background at its original address. Thus a regular remainder bound \(z\) becomes at most \(z + 2^n \bar{\omega}_n R_e y\), by (14.111), while the forced bound changes as displayed, and the polynomial density is unchanged.

14.18.4The recurrence along the comparison interval

Fix a top comparison depth \(k\), an intermediate level \(l\), a larger arity allowance \(u \geqslant \max (2, n)\), and a monitor \(H > 1\) fixed by \(k\). Here \(k, l, u, n\) are natural numbers with \(l \leqslant k\), and all scalar estimates are nonnegative. Let the tolerance exponent be \(q \geqslant 6\) and the regular step multiplier be \(\alpha \geqslant 0\). Set

\(\displaystyle d_0 = H^{- q}, \qquad d_j = (\alpha + 2)^j d_0 .\)

The principal sources are the connected polynomials of the same Gaussian history at the current level, with the same cell and marker labels. At the terminal comparison level their higher connected coefficients vanish: the formal logarithm of \(\prod_i (1 + z_i P_i)\) is \(\sum_i z_i P_i\). Its singleton terms are exactly the launched calibrated insertions. Consequently the initial marked representation has regular remainder bound \(d_0\), after the specified terminal-source purchase, which must also bound its initial forced bound by \(G_k\).

Suppose the actual one-step marked compiler at each level has the following quantitative outputs when its incoming remainder bound is \(z \geqslant d_0\). Before realignment it supplies remainder bound at most \((\alpha + 1) z\), principal incidence bound at most \(H^{- 2}\), and forced bound at most \(G_i^2\) at the coarse level. Here \(G_i = e^{- g_i} \leqslant 1\) is the required final forced bound. Its projected principal polynomial differs from the next prescribed connected polynomial by at most \(y\) on every required healthy region. Purchase

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} 2^n \bar{\omega}_n R_e y \leqslant d_0, \qquad G_i + nH^{- 2} R_{e + 1} \leqslant H^{- 1}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.114)} \\ (2 B_i + 1)^4 H^{- 1} \leqslant 1, \qquad e^{H^{- 1}} - 1 \leqslant 1. \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.115)} \end{array}\)

The forced inequality is (14.112) with \(s = G_i^2\), \(g = g_i\) and \(\delta = H^{- 1}\). Its output is at most \(G_i\). The regular bound becomes at most

\(\displaystyle (\alpha + 1) z + d_0 \leqslant (\alpha + 2) z.\)

Induction therefore bounds the remainder at level \(i\) by \(d_{k - i}\), with forced bound \(G_i\) and the exact prescribed principal polynomial. At the induction step the density is the push of the preceding marked density along the same transition kernel; the realignment changes only its representation. Hence this is an estimate on the original launched observable, not a sequence of separately chosen approximants.

The number of steps satisfies \((\alpha + 2)^{k - l} \leqslant H^{\kappa}\) for a constant \(\kappa\), by Lemma 14.45 below. It follows that \(d_{k - l} \leqslant H^{\kappa - q}\).

The marker weights
Two marker weights are used, each the same at every mark, replica and finite period. Put \(H =\mathcal{M}_k\), where \(\mathcal{M}_k = \exp \{(\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)^2 \}\) is the clock monitor at the comparison depth, as in Subsection 14.19. From the launch at the middle level \(\ell\) down to the output torus of the replica estimate, the weight is
\(\displaystyle \omega = H^{1 / (8 u)} .\) (14.116)

On the leading part of the interval, from the top \(k\) down to \(\ell\), the recurrence above is run at the smaller weight

\(\displaystyle \omega_- = H^{1 / (8 u) - U}, \qquad U \geqslant \frac{1}{8 u},\) (14.117)

where the weight exponent \(U\) is a constant fixed before the clock scale (Lemma 14.14 below says how). Every bound of the recurrence, namely the remainder bounds \(d_j\), the forced bounds \(G_i\), the principal incidence bound \(H^{- 2}\), the discrepancy contribution (14.111) and the terminal bounds, is a bound in the \(\| \cdot \|_{\omega_-}\) norm. The weight is changed exactly once, at the launch (Subsection 14.18.8), and the cost of that change is computed there.

Since \(U \geqslant 1 / (8 u)\), we have \(\omega_- \leqslant 1\), so (14.109) gives \(\omega_-^{|I|} \leqslant 1\) for every mode. Thus the first purchase in (14.114) is implied by

\(\displaystyle 2^n R_e y \leqslant d_0 = H^{- q} .\) (14.118)

At the large weight, since \(H > 1\) and \(n \leqslant u\),

\(\displaystyle \omega^{|I|} \leqslant \bar{\omega}_n = \omega^n \leqslant \omega^u = H^{1 / 8} \qquad (I \subseteq \{1, \ldots, n\}) .\) (14.119)

The incidence and spatial counts, the forced regrouping and the scalar envelope below hold for every positive weight. The one-step outputs assumed above, the principal-source bounds and the terminal-source purchase are hypotheses on bounds at the weight \(\omega_-\). When such an input is available as an unweighted bound \(b\) on the values of pieces carrying between one and \(n\) markers, with the support charge already included, it is also a bound on the \(\omega_-\)-bound, because \(\omega_- \leqslant 1\).

If the later propagation requires an extra factor \(H^{uV + 2}\) in the regular bound, purchase \(q \geqslant \kappa + uV + 2\), with \(\kappa\) the exponent of Lemma 14.45; this supplies that margin at the middle level. These two inequalities are separate purchases: a small initial error does not alone control arbitrarily many steps or an arbitrary later marker-weight change.

14.18.5Supplying the one-step hypotheses on the leading interval

We now show how the one-step hypotheses of the recurrence follow, on the leading part of the comparison interval, from the upper calculation of Subsection 14.4 and from the principal comparison of Subsection 9.3. We also show that the purchases (14.114)–(14.115) hold at large clock scale. Throughout, the top comparison depth is \(k\), the leading interval consists of the rungs \(i\) with \(\ell \leqslant i < k\), and \(s = \hat{\beta}_k\) is the clock scale. We put

\(\displaystyle H = \exp ((\log \log s)^2) =\mathcal{M}_k, \qquad \omega_- = H^{1 / (8 u) - U},\)

as in (14.117). The rung couplings \(x_i\) (coarse) and \(y_i\) (fine) of rung \(i\) lie in \([s / 2, s]\). The arity \(n \leqslant u\), the support exponent \(e\), the slice multiplier \(a\), the support radius \(Q \geqslant a\), the weight exponent \(U\), the tolerance exponent \(q \geqslant 6\) and the step multiplier \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_e\) are fixed before \(s\), in this order except that \(\alpha\) may be fixed right after \(e\) and the spare exponent \(\sigma\) of Subsection 14.18.8. Here \(T_e\) is the constant of (14.21). A function \(f\) of the clock scale has upper exponent \(c\) if for every \(\varepsilon > 0\), eventually \(|f (x) | \leqslant x^{c + \varepsilon}\) uniformly for \(x \in [s / 2, s]\). In particular \(H^c\) has upper exponent \(0\) for every real \(c\), because \((\log \log s)^2 = o (\log s)\).

Lemma 14.13. (Compiler outputs on a leading rung) Assume that, at rung \(i\), the upper calculation of Subsection 14.4 applies at the scale \(s\) with the multiplier \(\alpha\) and tolerance exponent \(q\) fixed above. Assume the incoming data are:

  • a regular remainder of marker-root bound \(z\) with \(H^{- q} \leqslant z \leqslant H^{- 4}\);

  • a leading family of marker budget at most \(H^{- 4}\);

  • a forced background of site bound at most \(G (y_i)\).

All bounds are taken at the weight \(\omega_-\). Then, after the marked push, the output has:

  1. nonleading regular marker-root bound at most \((\alpha + 1) z\);

  2. leading marker-root (principal incidence) bound at most \(H^{- 3} \leqslant H^{- 2}\);

  3. forced site bound at most \(G (x_i)^2\).

Thus the one-step hypotheses of the recurrence hold at rung \(i\) with \(G_i = G (x_i)\).

Proof. In the upper calculation the multipliers are \(l = \max (1, H^{- 3} / (\alpha z + H^{- q}))\) and \(m = G (x_i)^{- 2}\). The incoming forced budget is \(\eta = G (y_i)\), and the leading-family budget is \(h = H^{- 4}\). That calculation proves \(T_e \eta + a \leqslant H^{- 3}\) and \(\Sigma \leqslant K / 4\), with \(K = H^{- 1 / 8}\). Its rooted connected-set bound then gives every marker a total marker-root bound of at most \(T_e \eta + a\) over the components carrying it. It also gives every site a total site bound of at most \(\Sigma \leqslant e^{\Sigma} - 1\) over the components containing it. By (14.25), the integrated coefficient of a component is divided by \(t_C\):

  • \(t_C = 1\) for an entirely leading component;

  • \(t_C = l\) for an unforced component that is not entirely leading;

  • \(t_C = m\) for a component with nonempty forced inventory.

Hence:

  1. The nonleading regular components have marker-root bound at most \(H^{- 3} / l\). Since

    \(\displaystyle l \geqslant H^{- 3} / (\alpha z + H^{- q})\)

    , this is at most \(\alpha z + H^{- q} \leqslant \alpha z + z = (\alpha + 1) z\), because \(z \geqslant H^{- q}\).

  2. The entirely leading components have marker-root bound at most \(T_e \eta + a \leqslant H^{- 3} \leqslant H^{- 2}\), since \(H \geqslant 1\).

  3. The forced components have site bound at most \((e^{\Sigma} - 1) / m \leqslant (e^{K / 4} - 1) G (x_i)^2 \leqslant G (x_i)^2\), because \(e^{K / 4} - 1 \leqslant K \leqslant 1\).

The calculation of that section holds at every positive marker weight, since its bounds include the weights. So these are bounds at the weight \(\omega_-\). The multiplier \(l\) is at most \(\max (1, H^{q - 3})\), as in that section, so the compiler purchase made there covers it.\(\Box\)

The upper bound \(z \leqslant H^{- 4}\) in the lemma is preserved along the recurrence. Indeed \(d_{k - i} \leqslant d_{k - \ell} \leqslant H^{\kappa - q} \leqslant H^{- 4}\) by Lemma 14.45, once \(q \geqslant \kappa + 4\). The lower bound \(z \geqslant d_0 = H^{- q}\) is the convention of the recurrence: a smaller incoming bound may be enlarged to \(d_0\).

Lemma 14.14. (The leading budget at the small weight) Suppose that at every leading rung the prescribed principal sources have unweighted marker budget at most \(H^A\), for a constant \(A \geqslant 0\) fixed before \(s\). That is, for every mark, the sum over the modes containing it of the value bound times the support charge is at most \(H^A\). If

\(\displaystyle U \geqslant A + 4 + \frac{1}{8 u},\)

then their marker budget at the weight \(\omega_-\) is at most \(H^{- 4}\).

Proof. Every principal piece has a nonempty marker set \(I\), so its weight is \(\omega_-^{|I|} \leqslant \omega_- = H^{1 / (8 u) - U} \leqslant H^{- A - 4}\), because \(\omega_- \leqslant 1\). Multiplying the unweighted budget by this factor gives the claim.\(\Box\)

Lemma 14.15. (The unweighted principal bound) Assume the hypotheses of Subsection 14.17 at every rung of the leading interval:

  • the calibrated incoming polynomials, with \(|p_i |_1 \leqslant H^z\) and degree at most \(K\);

  • the polynomial-transport purchase \(2 (b + 2) \log A_{nK} \leqslant \log L\);

  • the healthy-field bounds (14.106) at the rung coupling.

Then there is a constant \(A \geqslant 0\), depending only on \(n\), \(z\), \(K\) and the fixed word, such that for all large \(s\) the hypothesis of Lemma 14.14 holds: at every leading rung the prescribed principal sources have unweighted marker budget at most \(H^A\).

Proof. At rung \(i\) the prescribed principal source of mode \(I\) is the connected polynomial \(c_I\) of the subset moments transported over \(k - i \leqslant k - \ell\) steps. By maximality of \(\ell\) and the bracket of Lemma 17.14, every block length on \([\ell, k]\) is at least \(L = L_{J (k) - 1}\), so \((k - \ell - 1) \log L < (b + 2) \log H\), as in the proof of Lemma 14.45. With the transport purchase at this \(L\), \(A_{nK}^{k - i} \leqslant A_{nK} \hspace{0.17em} H^{1 / 2} \leqslant H\) eventually. By (14.107) and the norm bound \(A_{nK}\) per step, every transported subset moment has norm at most \(R = H^{nz + 1}\), as in that section. The partition expansion gives \(|c_I |_1 \leqslant Q_n (R)\) and degree at most \(D_{\ast}\). On the healthy region of its prescribed support, the coordinates are bounded by \(M = \max (1, 16 B \tau (t)) = H^{o (1)}\), uniformly for \(t \in [s / 2, s]\), by the healthy-field construction. For every fixed degree \(D_{\ast}\), therefore, \(M^{D_{\ast}} \leqslant H\) eventually (the degree-zero case is immediate). Hence \(|c_I (X) | \leqslant Q_n (H^{nz + 1}) H\). The support charge is at most \(R_e\) (Lemma 14.17), and at most \(2^n\) modes contain a given mark. Since \(Q_n\) has degree at most \(n\), the unweighted budget is at most \(2^n R_e Q_n (H^{nz + 1}) H \leqslant H^A\) eventually, where \(A = n (nz + 1) + 2\). In particular this exponent does not depend on the Taylor degree \(K\); only its threshold does. This distinction permits the later choice of Taylor order after the marker weight and remainder exponent have been fixed.\(\Box\)

Corollary 14.16. (The per-rung amplitude) Under the hypotheses of Lemma 14.15, the amplitude \(\tau\) of the masked principal insertion at every leading rung is at most \(H^A\). It therefore has upper exponent \(0\), and the amplitude hypothesis of Lemma 14.26 holds.

Proof. The insertion is the masked principal piece. It vanishes off the healthy region of its prescribed support, and on that region it is bounded by \(|c_I (X) | \leqslant H^A\), by the proof of Lemma 14.15. So \(|h| \leqslant H^A\) everywhere, and \(H^A\) has upper exponent \(0\).\(\Box\)

The launch bounds available for the funded chart bound the principal source by a fixed positive power of \(H\). This is why the leading interval is run at the weight \(\omega_-\): at the large weight \(\omega\) the budget \(H^{- 4}\) is not available, and at the top comparison level the connected polynomial \(\sum_i z_i P_i\) is not small at all.

Lemma 14.17. (A fixed bound for the support charge) For a cell \(c\) and a nonempty mode \(I\), let \(r_{c, j}\) be the reader site of mark \(j\) in cell \(c\), and define the prescribed support

\(\displaystyle X_{c, I} = \{v : |v - r_{c, j} | \leqslant QB \text{for every } j \in I\} .\)

Assume that reader sites in distinct replica cells are at distance greater than \(2 QB\). Then the geometric hypotheses of the subsection on marker and spatial bounds hold, and every prescribed support satisfies \(\text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X_{c, I} \leqslant (\lceil Q \rceil + 1)^4\). Hence one may take

\(\displaystyle R_e = \exp \{e (\lceil Q \rceil + 1)^4 \},\)

a constant fixed before \(s\).

Proof. By the first subsection, every point of the full support of a principal piece of mode \(I\) in cell \(c\) lies within distance \((2 n + 1) \rho \leqslant aB \leqslant QB\) of each of its marked readers, which are the \(r_{c, j}\) with \(j \in I\). So the actual support is contained in \(X_{c, I}\). The prescribed support depends only on the mode, by definition. If a site \(v\) lies in \(X_{c, I}\) and in \(X_{c', I'}\), then a reader of \(c\) and a reader of \(c'\) are both within \(QB\) of \(v\), hence within \(2 QB\) of each other. By the separation assumption \(c = c'\). Finally, fix one \(j \in I\). Then \(X_{c, I}\) is contained in the cube of side \(2 QB\) centred at \(r_{c, j}\). That cube is covered by a grid of at most \((\lceil Q \rceil + 1)^4\) radius-\(B\) cubes whose union is connected. Since \(\text{chg}_e\) is monotone in the cover number, the bound follows.\(\Box\)

The prescribed polynomials are reference integrals.
Fix a cell and its mark set \(J\), \(|J| = n\), and work in the marker algebra \(\mathscr{A}_J\) of Subsection 14.1. For an element \(X\) with zero constant coefficient put \(\log_{\ast} (1 + X) = \sum_{j = 1}^n (- 1)^{j - 1} X^j / j\). Since every product of more than \(n\) nonconstant monomials vanishes, \(\exp_{\ast}\) and \(\log_{\ast}\) are mutually inverse bijections between the elements with zero constant coefficient and those with constant coefficient one. This is the identity of formal power series, truncated. Moreover, if \(X = \sum_{T \ne \varnothing} q_T z_T\), then \(X^j\) is the sum over ordered \(j\)-tuples of pairwise disjoint nonempty sets \(T_1, \ldots, T_j\) of \(\prod_l q_{T_l} z_{T_1 \cup \cdots \cup T_j}\). Each unordered partition with \(j\) blocks occurs \(j\)! times, so
\(\displaystyle \log_{\ast} \left( 1 + \sum_{T \ne \varnothing} q_T z_T \right) = \sum_{I \ne \varnothing} c_I z_I, \qquad c_I = \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (I)} (- 1)^{| \pi | - 1} (| \pi | - 1) ! \prod_{B \in \pi} q_B .\) (14.120)

These are exactly the connected polynomials of Subsection 14.17.

At a leading rung \(i\) let \(D^{(i)} = \sum_I c^{(i)}_I z_I\) be the prescribed principal polynomial of the cell. The pieces that are actually integrated are masked. Write \(\chi_I\) for the mask of mode \(I\) and \(D^{(i)}_{\chi} = \sum_I \chi_I c^{(i)}_I z_I\) for the masked principal polynomial. Its moment polynomials \(Q^{(i)}_T\) are defined by

\(\displaystyle \exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi} = 1 + \sum_{T \ne \varnothing} Q^{(i)}_T z_T, \qquad Q^{(i)}_T = \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (T)} \prod_{B \in \pi} \chi_B c^{(i)}_B .\)

These are the insertions of both integrals below. The unmasked moment polynomials of \(D^{(i)}\) are not used.

Fix a healthy base configuration \(x\) of the required healthy region. Let \(\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x}\) be the raw reference integral of Subsection 9.3 at the rung with base configuration \(x\), that is, integration of the insertion against \(R_9 \chi_9 \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0}\) followed by the finite forest and tag averages. Let \(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x}\) be the raw actual-sector integral, against \(R_6 \chi_6 \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0}\) and the same averages. Both are linear, and we apply them to elements of \(\mathscr{A}_J\) coefficientwise. Their values are jets of order \(n\) at the base point of the local variation about \(x\). On these jets we use the coefficient \(\ell^1\) norm \(\| \cdot \|\) of the truncated Taylor polynomial in the parameter \(y \in \mathbb{R}^j\), \(j \leqslant n\). It is submultiplicative for the truncated product, \(\|1\| = 1\), and it dominates the absolute value of the zeroth coefficient, which is the value at \(x\). Since \(j \leqslant n\), it is equivalent to the norm \(\| \cdot \|_{J^n_0}\) of Subsection 9.3 within a factor \(c_n\) depending only on \(n\). Put

\(\displaystyle a_x = \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (1), \qquad b_x = \tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x} (1) .\)

The cutoff \(\chi_9\) can change the mass of the reference integral, and \(b_x\) may depend on the base parameter. Where \(b_x\) is invertible, the normalized reference integral is \(\mathcal{G}_{i, x} = b_x^{- 1} \tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x}\), so that \(\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (1) = 1\). We write \(\mathcal{G}_i\) for the family \((\mathcal{G}_{i, x})_x\), and similarly \(\mathcal{A}_i\), \(a\) and \(b\).

We use the following hypothesis on one common insertion.

    (CI)

    Common insertion and transport. At each leading rung of the leading interval:

      (a)

      measure identification: for the leading letters of each cell and every partition product of their pieces, the sector and tag measures of (14.3) are those of the principal comparison integral \(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x}\) on the union of the read supports of the pieces in that product, with the same joint loading and the same weights;

      (b)

      the Gaussian history is transported by the reference integrals on those insertions: at every healthy base configuration \(x\) the jet of \(D^{(i - 1)}\) at \(x\) is \(\log_{\ast} \mathcal{G}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi})\). This is the Gaussian history used at the middle level and at the launch;

      (c)

      the comparison data of Subsection 9.3, and the payment that follows it, hold uniformly over the healthy base configurations \(x\) of the required healthy region. The insertion in that comparison is \(h = Q^{(i)}_T\) at the base configuration \(x\), read on the union of the read supports of the pieces in each partition product.

In the payment paragraph of Subsection 9.3 every scale except two is a function of the rung coupling alone: the read dimension, the direction bound, the aperture, the support radius and the good-coordinate radius \(v\). The two exceptions are the primitive mean and covariance bounds \(J_0, V_0\) of (9.21), with their absolute jet bounds. These are bounds on actual and reference covariances read at the base configuration. Clause (c) therefore amounts to requiring (9.21) and those absolute jet bounds with \(J_0\) and \(V_0\), as functions of the rung coupling, taken as suprema over the healthy base configurations of the region. That section already lists the primitive comparison as an input.

Uniformity of the primitive comparison.
There is already a producer for the primitive rates: Subsection 9.4. Its conclusion is uniform in the healthy base field, provided its local geometric hypotheses hold. We record exactly the specialization needed here. At a fixed stage pair and derivative order \(n \geqslant 2\), require, for every coordinate of the retained joint domain \(I_{\ast}\), a nonwrapping allocated healthy halo of radius \(45 (1 + B) + 4\), disjoint from the sector seeds. Use the same forest, tag, selected path and local window rules as that section, including the distinct sector and reference windows. Require its positive precision and path-derivative purchases through order \(n + 1\), and its local direction bounds. These conditions must hold for the cutoff coordinates as well as the insertion coordinates.

Under these hypotheses there are functions \(C (s), v (s), t (s)\), independent of the period, forest, tag and healthy base point, such that \(C\) has upper exponent zero, \(v\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and \(t\) has every negative upper exponent. After absorbing the fixed colour-coordinate factor into \(C\), one can use

\(\displaystyle J_0 (s) = n! 2^n C (s) \{(t (s) + v (s)) v (s) + v (s)^2 \}, \qquad V_0 (s) = n! 2^n \{t (s) + 2 C (s) v (s)\} .\) (14.121)

Indeed the gauge fixed at each base point places the field in the same logarithmic ball of radius \(v (s)\). The fixed-gauge local identification in that section identifies both physical readings with the corresponding local formulas on a neighbourhood. Apply (9.27) to the mean difference and (9.28) to the covariance difference. Although the gauge depends on the base point, it is fixed during parameter differentiation and is an isometry on colour space. Thus no derivative of a base-dependent gauge choice enters these estimates. Their right sides depend only on \(s\) and the purchased stage data; taking suprema over all allowed healthy base points leaves the same bounds. The exponent rules give \(- 1\) for \(J_0\) and \(- 1 / 2\) for \(V_0\). Multiplying \(J_0\) by \(g^{- 1}\), of exponent \(1 / 2\), gives the required rescaled difference of exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The absolute jets have exponent zero by the same kernel bounds.

This discharges the analytic uniformity step of (CI)(c) under the listed local hypotheses. It does not prove those hypotheses for the chart's enlarged cutoff domain. In particular, adding distant cutoff coordinates can invalidate the allocated seed-free halo or dimension bound, and one cannot replace the prescribed reference windows by the sector windows. Checking these geometric and same-history conditions is the remaining application of this producer.

The cutoff \(\chi_9\) makes \(\mathcal{G}_i\) a smooth function of the base parameter rather than a polynomial, and the jet norm also involves its derivatives in that parameter. The bounds on the transport and on the reference mass that the comparison needs are nevertheless consequences of (CI)(c). We prove them next. Put \(\tau_n = b_n \max (1, \tau)^n\), where \(b_n\) is the Bell number and \(\tau\) the amplitude of the masked principal pieces.

Lemma 14.18. (Jet bounds for the two integrals) Assume (CI)(c). There is a polynomial \(\tilde{P}_n\), depending only on \(n\), with the following property. Put \(K_0 = c_n \tilde{P}_n (N, Q, S)\), where \(c_n\) is the norm-equivalence factor above, and

\(\displaystyle \beta_0 = 2 (n + 1) (2 K_0 + 3)^n, \qquad \gamma_0 = \beta_0 (K_0 + 1),\)

and let \(\Pi\) be \(c_n\) times the multiplier of the amplitude in (9.20), which has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). Then \(K_0, \beta_0, \gamma_0\) have upper exponent \(0\), and for all large \(s\), every leading rung and every healthy base configuration \(x\):

  1. \(\| \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (h)\| \leqslant K_0 \sup |h|\) for every bounded measurable insertion \(h\);

  2. the zeroth coefficient of \(a_x\) lies in \([1 - 2 Ne^{- v + M / 2}, 1]\);

  3. \(b_x\) is invertible in the truncated jet algebra and \(\|b_x^{- 1} \| \leqslant \beta_0\);

  4. \(\|\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (h)\| \leqslant \gamma_0 \sup |h|\) for every bounded measurable insertion \(h\); in particular \(\|\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T)\| \leqslant \gamma_0 \tau_n\).

Proof. Item 1. On \(\|z\|_{\infty} \leqslant S\), (9.18), the cutoff jet bound of hypothesis 4 of Subsection 9.3 and the product rule bound the \(J^n_0\) norm of \(R_6 \chi_6 (\cdot, z)\) by \(\tilde{P}_n (N, Q, S)\), after enlarging the polynomial. Beyond that ball the integrand vanishes on a common neighbourhood of zero. Differentiation under the integral, as justified in that section without derivatives of \(h\), and integration against the probability measure \(\gamma_{C_0}\) give \(\| \int R_6 \chi_6 h \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0} \|_{J^n_0} \leqslant \tilde{P}_n \sup |h|\). The forest and tag averages have nonnegative weights summing to one, so they preserve this bound. Passing to the Taylor \(\ell^1\) norm costs \(c_n\).

Item 2. At the base point the compensated map is the identity, \(T_{y_0} z = z\) (Subsection 8.26). So the Jacobian is one there, \(w_6 (0, z) = z\) and \(C_6 (0) = C_0\), hence \(R_6 (0, z) = 1\). The zeroth coefficient of \(a_x\) is therefore an average of the numbers \(\int \chi_6 (0, z) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0} (z)\). They lie in \([0, 1]\) because \(0 \leqslant \chi_6 \leqslant 1\). They are at least \(\gamma_{C_0} \{\|z\|_{\infty} < v\} \geqslant 1 - 2 Ne^{- v + M / 2}\), because \(\chi_6 = 1\) there by hypothesis 4 and by (9.19).

Item 3. By (9.20) with \(h = 1\), \(\|b_x - a_x \| \leqslant \Pi\). The zeroth coefficient \(b_0\) of \(b_x\) therefore satisfies \(b_0 \geqslant 1 - 2 Ne^{- v + M / 2} - \Pi \geqslant \frac{1}{2}\) for large \(s\), since \(e^{- v}\) has every negative upper exponent. Also \(\|b_x \| \leqslant K_0 + \Pi \leqslant K_0 + 1\). Write \(b_x = b_0 (1 + u)\). Then \(u\) has zero constant coefficient and \(\|u\| \leqslant 2 (K_0 + 1) + 1\). In the algebra of jets of order \(n\), a product of \(n + 1\) elements with zero constant coefficient vanishes. So \((1 + u)^{- 1} = \sum_{r = 0}^n (- u)^r\), and

\(\displaystyle \|b_x^{- 1} \| \leqslant b_0^{- 1} \sum_{r = 0}^n \|u\|^r \leqslant 2 (n + 1) (2 K_0 + 3)^n = \beta_0 .\)

Item 4. By (9.20) and item 1, \(\| \tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x} (h)\| \leqslant (K_0 + \Pi) \sup |h| \leqslant (K_0 + 1) \sup |h|\). Multiply by \(b_x^{- 1}\) and use item 3. Since \(|Q^{(i)}_T | \leqslant \tau_n\), the last claim follows.

Finally, \(N\), \(Q\) and \(S\) have upper exponent \(0\) by the payment paragraph of Subsection 9.3. A fixed polynomial in them, and a fixed power of it, also have upper exponent \(0\).\(\Box\)

Inside the reference integral the masks play no role. Write \(q^{(i)}_T = \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (T)} \prod_{B \in \pi} c^{(i)}_B\) for the unmasked moment polynomials of \(D^{(i)}\), so that \(\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)} = 1 + \sum_T q^{(i)}_T z_T\).

Lemma 14.19. (Masks under the reference cutoff) Assume (CI)(c), and that the read support of each masked principal piece contains the support \(X_{c, I}\) on which its mask \(\chi_I\) is inspected. Then, for every \(T\) and every healthy base configuration \(x\),

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T) = \tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x} (q^{(i)}_T), \qquad \text{hence} \qquad \mathcal{G}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi}) =\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}) .\)

Proof. By (CI)(c) the insertion of the comparison for \(Q^{(i)}_T\) is read on the union \(Y\) of the read supports of the pieces in its partition products. By the second identity of Subsection 9.3, wherever the reference cutoff \(\chi_9\) is nonzero, the fixed configuration is healthy on the support of the insertion, here \(Y\). Each \(X_{c, B}\) with \(B \subseteq T\) lies in \(Y\), so each mask \(\chi_B\) equals one there. Hence \(R_9 \chi_9 Q^{(i)}_T = R_9 \chi_9 q^{(i)}_T\) pointwise. Where \(\chi_9 = 0\) both sides vanish. Integrate and average. The second identity follows coefficientwise, after dividing by \(b_x\).\(\Box\)

So in (CI)(b) the masked and unmasked prescribed polynomials give the same reference transport. The masked moments are still the insertions of the actual-sector integral \(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x}\), where the actual cutoff does not force health on the larger prescribed supports. That is why the comparison in Lemma 14.26 uses \(Q^{(i)}_T\) on both sides.

Clause (b) defines reference-transported targets recursively, but this alone does not give admissible principal pieces at the next rung. The normalized cutoff integral can produce smooth nonpolynomial functions of the base configuration. At each rung one must preserve a class of insertions on which the next transport is defined and prove the required support, amplitude, jet and representation bounds there. In particular, the polynomial degree bound used in Lemma 14.15 cannot be inferred from the recursion with \(\mathcal{G}_i\) alone.

At the middle level, let \(D_Q\) be the connected polynomial of the actual polynomial history of Subsection 14.17. The healthy local error required by Lemma 14.35 concerns the actual planted leading coefficient and this same \(D_Q\). If a reference-transported intermediate history is used, its error against the planted coefficient and its error against \(D_Q\) add. For a target tolerance \(\varepsilon_{\ast}\) one must allocate \(\varepsilon_{\text{planted}} + \varepsilon_{\text{transport}} \leqslant \varepsilon_{\ast}\); two separate bounds by \(\varepsilon_{\ast}\) do not give the required bound. The following calculation gives a precise sufficient transport budget.

Lemma 14.20. (Comparing two finite transport histories) For \(0 \leqslant j \leqslant r\) let \(\mathcal{B}_j\) be normed spaces of admissible coefficient functions. Let \(G_j, T_j : \mathcal{B}_{j + 1} \to \mathcal{B}_j\) be bounded linear maps for \(j < r\), with \(\|G_j \| \leqslant C_j\). For one terminal coefficient \(f \in \mathcal{B}_r\) write \(f_j = T_j \cdots T_{r - 1} f\) and suppose \(\|(G_j - T_j) f_{j + 1} \| \leqslant \delta_j\). Then

\(\displaystyle \|G_0 \cdots G_{r - 1} f - T_0 \cdots T_{r - 1} f\| \leqslant \sum_{j = 0}^{r - 1} \delta_j \prod_{a = 0}^{j - 1} C_a .\)

Use the empty-product convention \(1\). Suppose this holds for all nonempty moment coefficients in a marker algebra on \(n\) marks. If the two resulting moments are \(1 + X\) and \(1 + Y\), and \(\|X\|, \|Y\| \leqslant R\) in its submultiplicative coefficient norm, then

\(\displaystyle \| \log_{\ast} (1 + X) - \log_{\ast} (1 + Y)\| \leqslant \left( \sum_{q = 0}^{n - 1} R^q \right) \|X - Y\| .\)

For the unweighted sum norm on marker coefficients, the first bound therefore gives a connected-coefficient error at most

\(\displaystyle (2^n - 1) \left( \sum_{q = 0}^{n - 1} R^q \right) \sum_{j = 0}^{r - 1} \delta_j \prod_{a = 0}^{j - 1} C_a .\)

Proof. Replace the maps in the product one at a time, starting from the left:

\(\displaystyle G_0 \cdots G_{r - 1} - T_0 \cdots T_{r - 1} = \sum_{j = 0}^{r - 1} G_0 \cdots G_{j - 1} (G_j - T_j) T_{j + 1} \cdots T_{r - 1} .\)

Apply this identity to \(f\) and take norms. For the logarithms use \(X^q - Y^q = \sum_{a = 0}^{q - 1} X^a (X - Y) Y^{q - 1 - a}\). Submultiplicativity bounds this by \(qR^{q - 1} \|X - Y\|\); the factor \(q\) cancels the denominator in \(\log_{\ast}\). Summing over \(1 \leqslant q \leqslant n\) proves the second bound, and summing the at most \(2^n - 1\) moment errors proves the last one.\(\Box\)

For the intended application \(G_j =\mathcal{G}_j\) and \(T_j =\mathcal{T}_j\), with the same initial moments and normalization. The spaces \(\mathcal{B}_j\) must include both histories and all intermediate functions in the telescoping identity. A jet bound for one fixed insertion is not an operator bound on such a space. If masks are removed using Lemma 14.19, that identity must hold at each intermediate insertion. After checking these domain conditions, making the last displayed quantity at most \(\varepsilon_{\text{transport}}\) gives the allocated middle-level error. Neither the required operator products nor this error purchase has been established here for the actual chart history.

The polynomial step of Subsection 14.17 is a finite linear substitution followed by Wick contraction with the reference covariance. Write \(\mathcal{T}_i\) for it. If (b) is stated with \(\mathcal{T}_i\) in place of \(\mathcal{G}_i\), the proof below acquires the additional term \(\max_T \|\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T) -\mathcal{T}_i (Q^{(i)}_T)\|\). A small difference does not replace the norm bound of Lemma 14.18(4) by itself. That route needs such a bound for one of the two transports and a bound on the difference with upper exponent below zero. By Lemma 14.19, no insertion mask survives in the reference integral. The remaining difference includes both the reference cutoff and the nonlinear physical coordinate reading. The local comparison below treats these separately; no affineness of the compensated coordinate map is required.

Lemma 14.21. (The prescribed polynomial is the reference integral) Under (CI)(b), at every leading rung and every healthy base configuration \(x\), the moment polynomials of the jet of \(D^{(i - 1)}\) at \(x\) are \(\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T)\):

\(\displaystyle \exp_{\ast} D^{(i - 1)} = 1 + \sum_{T \ne \varnothing} \mathcal{G}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T) \hspace{0.17em} z_T .\)

Proof. Apply the linear map \(\mathcal{G}_{i, x}\) coefficientwise to \(\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi}\), use \(\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (1) = 1\), and then \(\exp_{\ast} \log_{\ast} = \text{id}\) on elements with constant coefficient one.\(\Box\)

Clause (a) of (CI) is a consequence of the realignment and of the planted identity. Only the first leading rung needs a separate check.

Lemma 14.22. (The common insertion after realignment) Let \(i\) be a leading rung. Suppose that the leading family entering rung \(i\) is the output of the realignment of Subsection 14.18.3 at the preceding rung, with desired principal sources \(F_{c, I} = c^{(i)}_I\), or, at the first leading rung, that it consists of one piece \(\chi_I c^{(i)}_I z_{(c, I)}\) per cell \(c\) and mode \(I\). Assume (CI)(a) at rung \(i\). Then the entirely leading components of each cell \(c\), produced by the compiler through (14.2)–(14.3), integrate to \(\iota_c \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi})\), with the masked insertions \(Q^{(i)}_T\).

Proof. The leading family. On the healthy region of \(X_{c, I}\), the realignment writes the sum of the leading pieces of mode \((c, I)\) as \(F_{c, I} + L_{c, I}\). It moves \(L_{c, I}\), with the mask of \(X_{c, I}\), into the regular remainder, and moves the part of the original masked values off that healthy region into the forced alphabet. What remains designated leading at mode \((c, I)\) is therefore the single piece \(\chi_I F_{c, I} z_{(c, I)} = \chi_I c^{(i)}_I z_{(c, I)}\), where \(\chi_I\) is the healthy mask of \(X_{c, I}\). In the cell \(c\) the leading family is thus \(\iota_c (D^{(i)}_{\chi})\). At the first leading rung this is the hypothesis.

The integral. Apply (14.2) and (14.3) to the leading letters alone, with \(T = \{c\} \times J\). The selected pieces are those of cell \(c\), so the planted polynomial is \(P_T = D^{(i)}_{\chi}\). Its values are the masked values, as shown after (14.2). By (14.1) the product of the factors \(1 + \chi_I c^{(i)}_I z_I\) is \(\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi}\), whose coefficients are the insertions \(Q^{(i)}_T\). The identity (14.3) holds with the sector and tag measures and weights supplied with it. By (CI)(a), for each partition product these are the measures, joint loading and weights of \(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x}\) on the union of the read supports of its pieces. By the first identity of Subsection 9.3, that integral is integration against \(R_6 \chi_6 \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0}\) followed by the forest and tag averages. The planted identity alone does not make this identification. Hence the entirely leading part of the cell integrates to \(\iota_c \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi})\), with the insertions \(Q^{(i)}_T\).\(\Box\)

Consequently, under (CI)(a), the leading part of every cell integrates as stated at every leading rung, provided that the leading family has the displayed form at the first one. There the leading family is the top-level principal insertion. By the top initialization at the end of Subsection 14.17, it consists of the calibrated singletons, one per mark, with the support, marker and masked value of the terminal birth term. All connected coefficients with at least two markers vanish. So the displayed form holds at the first rung, with \(c_{\{j\}}\) the calibrated singleton and \(c_I = 0\) for \(|I| \geqslant 2\), provided the mask of each birth term is inspected on its prescribed support \(X_{c, \{j\}}\). Otherwise a realignment with zero discrepancy at the top level moves the difference of the two masks into the forced alphabet, at the bound of the forced regrouping. This mask condition is the part of the terminal-source purchase used here.

Clause (a) concerns the measure after all sector cutoffs have been included. Projection of the Gaussian alone does not determine this measure. The following elementary identity states exactly what must be retained.

Lemma 14.23. (Projection of a sector-cut Gaussian) Let \(Z\) have a centred Gaussian law \(\gamma_C\) on a finite coordinate space, let \(H\) have a probability Haar law \(\rho\) independent of \(Z\), and let \(0 \leqslant \chi (Z, H) \leqslant 1\) be measurable. For a coordinate set \(I\) put \(X = Z_I\) and \(C_I = C|_{I \times I}\). There is a measurable function \(0 \leqslant \bar{\chi}_I (x, h) \leqslant 1\), defined almost everywhere, such that

\(\displaystyle (Z, H) \mapsto (Z_I, H) \quad \text{pushes } \quad \chi \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_C \hspace{0.17em} d \rho \quad \text{to} \quad \bar{\chi}_I \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_I} \hspace{0.17em} d \rho .\) (14.122)

It is the conditional expectation of \(\chi\) given \((Z_I, H)\). If \(\chi\) already depends only on \((Z_I, H)\), then \(\bar{\chi}_I = \chi\). For positive definite \(C\), writing \(J = I^c\), an explicit version is

\(\displaystyle \bar{\chi}_I (x, h) = \int \chi (x, z, h) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_S (z - C_{JI} C_I^{- 1} x), \qquad S = C_{JJ} - C_{JI} C_I^{- 1} C_{IJ} .\)

The pushforward identity includes the constant insertion \(1\).

Proof. Let \(\mu\) be the law of \((Z_I, H)\) and let \(\nu\) be the pushforward of \(\chi \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_C \hspace{0.17em} d \rho\). For every measurable set \(B\), \(0 \leqslant \nu (B) \leqslant \mu (B)\), so the Radon–Nikodym derivative \(d \nu / d \mu\) exists and lies between zero and one. This proves (14.122) and its characterization by conditional expectation. The unweighted marginal is \(\gamma_{C_I} \otimes \rho\), since linear projections of a Gaussian have the restricted covariance and \(H\) is independent. The identity for integrals follows first for indicators, then for simple functions and by dominated convergence for bounded measurable insertions. It also holds for every absolutely integrable insertion by truncation. If \(\chi\) is measurable in the retained coordinates it can be taken outside the conditional integral. Finally, when \(C\) is positive definite, completing the square in the joint Gaussian density gives conditional mean \(C_{JI} C_I^{- 1} x\) and covariance \(S\), proving the formula. The Radon–Nikodym argument does not require positive definiteness.\(\Box\)

For example, with independent standard Gaussian coordinates \(X, Y\), a cutoff \(\chi (X, Y) = \psi (Y)\) projects to the constant \(\int \psi \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_1\), not to \(1\). If the conditional law of the omitted variables varies with a coarse parameter, the projected cutoff can vary with that parameter too. Bounds on the full cutoff do not by themselves give its projected parameter-jet bounds.

Which sector law has to be identified.
The measure in (CI)(a) is the measure of the selected principal component, not the complete interacting block density. The distinction can already be seen in the dictionary of Subsection 8.29. Its full integrand contains the compulsory packet factors and \(\exp (\sum_i f_i)\) in addition to the Gaussian and local cutoffs. Neither factor is generally constant in the Gaussian variables. It cannot be absorbed into the common scalar \(\Lambda (y)\) of (M). Setting a body's marker variables to zero does not remove its unmarked activity; selecting the component with no body letters does.

For clarity, consider a fixed principal-only component whose integrand, before Gaussian integration, is

\(\displaystyle \prod_{a \in A} \chi_a (z, h) f_a (U (z, h)) .\)

Here the product over other selected dictionary letters is empty and therefore equals one. Let \(I\) contain every real coordinate inspected by every factor, including the \(\chi_a\). The joint integral is exactly

\(\displaystyle \int \prod_{a \in A} \chi_a (z_I, h) f_a (U (z_I, h)) \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_I} (z_I) d \rho (h) .\)

Indeed the characteristic-function marginal calculation in Subsection 8.26 projects the Gaussian; the integrand already depends only on the retained variables, so no conditional cutoff remains. If the local coordinate display is independent of a frozen Haar variable, its probability integral is one; otherwise that variable stays in \(h\). No product of marginal Gaussian laws has been substituted for this joint law. In the singleton case the same argument applies to \(\chi_a f_a\), including its entire local cutoff. The compensated change of variables then gives the first integrand of (9.16) exactly.

This calculation explains how a local principal law can have the form (M) without the full sector density having that form. To apply it to a partition product, the component construction must give the displayed principal-only integrand on the union of its reads, with the same sector, forest, tag and loading for all factors. A singleton identity alone does not establish that property for a product. Nor may its unit normalization be replaced by the full interacting partition function. These are the precise component-selection and normalization matches required in (CI)(a); enlarging a read set cannot fix a mismatch in them.

Here is a sufficient version of the chart-law input. Its domain is chosen once for a cell, before selecting a partition product.

    (M)

    Fix a leading rung and a cell. On a common neighbourhood of each healthy base parameter \(y = 0\), the planted sector/tag measure, indexed by \(t\), is

    \(\displaystyle d \nu_{t, y} = \Lambda (y) \chi_{6, t} (y, z, h) d \gamma_{C_t (y)} (z) d \rho_t (h) .\)

    The smooth factor \(\Lambda (y) > 0\) is independent of \(t\) and of the insertion, including the unit. The forest/tag weights \(\lambda_t\) are the same as those of the principal comparison, are fixed on this neighbourhood, and are nonnegative with sum one. There is a finite coordinate set \(I_{\ast}\), independent of \(y\) on this neighbourhood, containing every coordinate inspected by every partition-product insertion and by \(\chi_{6, t}\), for all retained \(t\). On this enlarged domain the compensated identities (9.16) and all comparison data (CI)(c) hold, including the read-dimension and parameter-jet bounds.

One may instead keep a smaller read domain only after proving that its cutoff-weighted pushforward in (14.122) is the law used by the principal comparison, with that law's quantitative jet bounds. A merely unweighted Gaussian marginal identity does not suffice.

Lemma 14.24. (Measure identification from the joint chart law) Under (M), every partition-product insertion \(h\), including \(h = 1\), satisfies, on the common parameter neighbourhood,

\(\displaystyle \sum_t \lambda_t \int h \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_{t, y} = \Lambda (y) \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (h) (y) .\)

Consequently (CI)(a) holds with the common loading retained. If the raw unit integral is invertible as a jet, normalization cancels that loading in the full jet algebra, not just at its zeroth coefficient.

Proof. For fixed \(t, y, h\), both the insertion and the cutoff are measurable in \((z_{I_{\ast}}, h')\), where \(h'\) denotes the frozen Haar variable. Apply Lemma 14.23 on \(I_{\ast}\): the joint Gaussian marginal has covariance \(C_t (y) |_{I_{\ast} \times I_{\ast}}\) and the cutoff is unchanged. No independence between the pieces' coordinates is used. The compensated change of variables on this same domain gives \(R_6 \chi_6 h \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_{C_0}\) by (M). Integrate the frozen variables and average with the prescribed weights. The factor \(\Lambda (y)\) can be pulled out of this average precisely because it is independent of \(t\). This proves the displayed identity, also for \(h = 1\) on the identical domain and law. Smoothness and the common dominated differentiation hypotheses give the identity of jets.

Write \(a\) for the jet of \(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (1)\) and \(L\) for the jet of \(\Lambda\). Since \(\Lambda (0) > 0\), \(L\) is invertible: divide by its constant coefficient and use the finite Neumann series for its nilpotent part. Hence, whenever \(a\) is invertible,

\(\displaystyle (La)^{- 1} \hspace{0.17em} L \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (h) = a^{- 1} \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (h) .\)

This is the required normalization, with the same unit insertion.\(\Box\)

If a loading \(\Lambda_t\) depends on the sector or tag, it cannot in general be pulled out of the average. The correct normalized integral is then

\(\displaystyle \left( \sum_t \lambda_t \Lambda_t \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_t (1) \right)^{- 1} \sum_t \lambda_t \Lambda_t \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_t (h) .\)

Any change to the averaging weights must preserve this formula and its jets. The sector expansion of Subsection 8.29 must supply (M), or this more general formula, on the actual course. Enlarging the domain to include the cutoff also requires checking the read-dimension and comparison bounds on that larger domain. Neither identification is proved by the planted marker identity alone.

By Lemma 14.22 the output leading letters of the cell are the coefficients of \(\log_{\ast} (\mathcal{A}_i (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi}))\), where \(\mathcal{A}_{i, x} = a_x^{- 1} \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x}\) is the normalized actual-sector integral. The scalar factor \(a_x\) belongs to the unmarked normalization. Its invertibility is part of the next proof.

Lemma 14.25. (Inverse of a jet close to one) Let \(a\) be a jet of order \(m\) with \(\|a - 1\| \leqslant \Pi \leqslant 1 / 2\). Then \(a\) is invertible in the truncated jet algebra, \(\|a^{- 1} \| \leqslant 2\) and \(\|a^{- 1} - 1\| \leqslant 2 \Pi\).

Proof. Put \(u = 1 - a\), so \(\|u\| \leqslant \Pi\). The series \(\sum_{k \geqslant 0} u^k\) converges absolutely by submultiplicativity, and its sum \(v\) satisfies \(av = va = 1\). Moreover \(\|v\| \leqslant (1 - \Pi)^{- 1} \leqslant 2\) and \(\|v - 1\| \leqslant \Pi (1 - \Pi)^{- 1} \leqslant 2 \Pi\).\(\Box\)

Lemma 14.26. (The per-step discrepancy) Assume (CI). Suppose that, at each leading rung, the amplitude \(\tau\) of the masked principal pieces has upper exponent \(0\), uniformly in the rungs. Then, eventually in \(s\), the actual projected polynomial and the next prescribed polynomial differ by at most \(y\) on the required healthy regions, where \(y\) satisfies (14.118). Hence the first purchase in (14.114) holds.

Proof. Fix a healthy base configuration \(x\); by (CI)(c) all constants below are uniform in \(x\). Each \(Q^{(i)}_T\) is a sum, over the at most \(b_n\) set partitions of \(T\), of products of at most \(n\) masked principal pieces. As an insertion it is measurable and bounded by \(\tau_n\). By (9.20) and the payment paragraph after it, applied with the base configuration \(x\) to the insertions \(Q^{(i)}_T\) and \(1\),

\(\displaystyle \| \tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T) - \tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T)\| \leqslant \tau_n \Pi, \qquad \|a_x - b_x \| \leqslant \Pi,\)

with \(\Pi\) as in Lemma 14.18, uniformly in the period and after the averages.

Normalization. Write \(a_x = b_x (1 + u)\) with \(u = b_x^{- 1} (a_x - b_x)\). By Lemma 14.18(3), \(\|u\| \leqslant \beta_0 \Pi\). Once \(\beta_0 \Pi \leqslant 1 / 2\), Lemma 14.25 shows that \(1 + u\) is invertible with \(\|(1 + u)^{- 1} \| \leqslant 2\). Hence \(a_x\) is invertible and \(\|a_x^{- 1} \| \leqslant 2 \beta_0\). For every insertion \(h\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{A}_{i, x} (h) -\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (h) = a_x^{- 1} \left[ (\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{i, x} - \tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x}) (h) - (a_x - b_x) \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{G}_{i, x} (h) \right],\)

as one checks by multiplying out and using \(\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i, x} = b_x \mathcal{G}_{i, x}\). With \(h = Q^{(i)}_T\) and Lemma 14.18(4),

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{A}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T) -\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T)\| \leqslant 2 \beta_0 (\tau_n \Pi + \Pi \gamma_0 \tau_n) = 2 \beta_0 (1 + \gamma_0) \tau_n \Pi .\)

Hence all coefficients of \(\mathcal{A}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi})\) and of \(\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi})\) have norm at most \(K = (1 + \gamma_0) \tau_n\) once \(2 \beta_0 (1 + \gamma_0) \Pi \leqslant 1\).

Logarithm. The map \(1 + X \mapsto \log_{\ast} (1 + X)\) is a polynomial of degree at most \(n\) in the coefficients of \(X\), with products taken in the jet algebra. On elements whose coefficients have norm at most \(K \geqslant 1\) it is Lipschitz in the sum of the coefficient norms, with constant at most \(C_n K^{n - 1}\) for a constant \(C_n\) depending only on \(n\). Hence, by (CI)(b),

\(\displaystyle | \log_{\ast} \mathcal{A}_{i, x} (\exp_{\ast} D^{(i)}_{\chi}) - D^{(i - 1)} | \leqslant C_n K^{n - 1} 2^n \cdot 2 \beta_0 (1 + \gamma_0) \tau_n \Pi = : y,\)

the norm on the left being the sum over the modes of the jet norms of the coefficients at \(x\).

Values. The zeroth coefficient of a jet product is the product of the zeroth coefficients, so the zeroth coefficient of the left side is the same expression evaluated at \(x\). The jet norm dominates it. Hence the actual projected polynomial and \(D^{(i - 1)}\) differ by at most \(y\) at every healthy base configuration \(x\), which is the bound on the required healthy regions.

Payment. Since \(\tau\), \(\beta_0\) and \(\gamma_0\) have upper exponent \(0\), so do \(\tau_n\) and \(K\), and \(y\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). By Lemma 14.17, \(2^n R_e\) is a constant, and \(H^q\) has upper exponent \(0\). Hence \(2^n R_e yH^q\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\), and is therefore at most \(1\) for all large \(s\). This is (14.118), which implies the first purchase in (14.114) because \(\omega_- \leqslant 1\).\(\Box\)

A polynomial history with a local transport error.
The compensated coordinate map need not be affine. Indeed, on the protected ball it is obtained from \(u \mapsto \log (\exp (u) M)\), as in (8.155); away from that ball it uses a smooth extension. Undoing this change of variables removes its Jacobian, but leaves the nonlinear physical insertion in the reference Gaussian integral. It does not turn that insertion into its linear curvature polynomial. Consequently (CI)(b) is a sufficient convention, not an identity supplied by the compensated change of variables. We now give an alternative which keeps the prescribed polynomial history throughout.

Lemma 14.27. (Local error in the prescribed transport) Assume (CI)(a),(c) and the amplitude hypotheses of Lemma 14.26. Replace (CI)(b) by the following condition. For each nonempty \(T \subseteq J\), let \(q^-_T\) be the moment coefficient of the prescribed polynomial at the next rung. Uniformly on its healthy region, suppose

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T) - q^-_T \| \leqslant \eta_i, \qquad \|q^-_T \| \leqslant K_i,\)

where \(K_i \geqslant 1\) has upper exponent zero, as do the principal amplitudes, and \(\eta_i\) has a strictly negative upper exponent, uniformly in the rungs. Then the conclusion of Lemma 14.26 holds with this prescribed history.

Proof. The normalization calculation in that lemma uses only (a),(c), and gives

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{A}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T) -\mathcal{G}_{i, x} (Q^{(i)}_T)\| \leqslant d_i := 2 \beta_0 (1 + \gamma_0) \tau_n \Pi .\)

Hence the actual and prescribed moment coefficients differ by at most \(d_i + \eta_i\). Increase \(K_i\) to bound both families; it still has upper exponent zero. In the coefficient sum norm on the marker algebra their nonconstant parts have norms at most \(R_i = (2^n - 1) K_i\) and their difference has norm at most \((2^n - 1) (d_i + \eta_i)\). The finite logarithm estimate of Lemma 14.20 therefore bounds the connected error by

\(\displaystyle y_i = (2^n - 1) \left( \sum_{r = 0}^{n - 1} R_i^r \right) (d_i + \eta_i) .\)

This has strictly negative upper exponent. Its value bounds the healthy local discrepancy because the jet norm dominates evaluation at zero. For every fixed \(q\), the factors \(2^n R_e H^q\) have upper exponent zero, so \(2^n R_e H^q y_i \leqslant 1\) eventually, uniformly in the rungs. This is exactly the discrepancy purchase used in the recurrence.\(\Box\)

This argument compares one step on the actual prescribed polynomial input and then realigns to the prescribed next polynomial. It does not iterate a reference cutoff operator on smooth nonpolynomial outputs. The recurrence already propagates the resulting regular and forced errors. Thus this route needs no additional invariant function space for a second, reference-transported history.

The joint Gaussian construction with separate guards.
There is a more direct route for the selected principal components. Here the cutoff belongs to each insertion, rather than to a common subprobability measure. The empty product then has integral exactly one. The following argument supplies the finite-arity comparison without assuming either (M) or exact reference transport (CI)(b).

Lemma 14.28. (Guarded joint Gaussian moments) Fix \(n, D \in \mathbb{N}\), \(A, B \geqslant 1\), and \(0 \leqslant \epsilon \leqslant 1\). For every nonempty mode \(I \subseteq \{1, \ldots, n\}\) let \(p_I\) be an invariant polynomial of degree at most \(D\) and coefficient norm at most \(A\) in a finite set of real curvature coordinates. Fix a real mean \(a\) and a real symmetric covariance array \(C_{\ast}\) on the union of these coordinates, with entries bounded by \(B\) in absolute value.

For each member \(t\) of a finite mixture let \(\mu_t\) be a probability measure carrying a jointly centred Gaussian array \(\eta_t\), with covariance \(C_t\). Require, on every coordinate used here,

\(\displaystyle |a_j | \leqslant B, \quad | (C_t)_{jk} | \leqslant B, \quad | (C_t - C_{\ast})_{jk} | \leqslant \epsilon .\)

For each mode let \(0 \leqslant \Gamma_{t, I} \leqslant 1\) be a measurable guard with \(\int (1 - \Gamma_{t, I}) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_t \leqslant \epsilon^2\). Let \(U_t\) be the actual fine configuration on this same probability space.
