On \(\Gamma_{t, I} > 0\), assume it is healthy on the full prescribed support of \(p_I\), and that, after a gauge rotation allowed to depend on \(t, I, U_t\), its normalized curvature coordinates \(\xi_{t, I}\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle | \xi_{t, I, j} - a_j - \eta_{t, j} | \leqslant \epsilon\)

on every coordinate used by \(p_I\). The polynomial's invariance must remove that rotation from its physical value. All physical evaluations and guarded products are assumed measurable.

Use the same nonnegative mixture weights \(\lambda_t\), of sum one, for every partition and for the empty product. Define, for \(S \ne \varnothing\),

\(\displaystyle M_S = \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (S)} \sum_t \lambda_t \int \prod_{I \in \pi} \Gamma_{t, I} p_I (\xi_{t, I}) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_t, \qquad M^{\ast}_S = \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P} (S)} \mathbb{E}_{C_{\ast}} \prod_{I \in \pi} p_I (a + Z),\)

and \(M_{\varnothing} = M^{\ast}_{\varnothing} = 1\). Here \(\mathbb{E}_{C_{\ast}}\) means the finite Gaussian pairing functional; \(C_{\ast}\) need not be positive. Then all the physical integrals exist. There is a polynomial \(P_{n, D}\) with positive coefficients such that

\(\displaystyle \sum_{S \ne \varnothing} |M_S - M^{\ast}_S | \leqslant \epsilon P_{n, D} (A, B), \qquad | \log_{\ast} M - \log_{\ast} M^{\ast} |_1 \leqslant \epsilon P_{n, D} (A, B),\)

after increasing the same polynomial if necessary. Its coefficients and degree are independent of the number of curvature coordinates and the number of mixture members.

Proof. Fix a partition with \(l \leqslant n\) blocks, put \(d = lD\), and abbreviate \(P = \prod_{I \in \pi} p_I\) and \(\Gamma = \prod_{I \in \pi} \Gamma_{t, I}\). The product guard satisfies

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant 1 - \Gamma \leqslant \sum_{I \in \pi} (1 - \Gamma_{t, I}), \qquad \int (1 - \Gamma) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_t \leqslant l \epsilon^2 .\)

No independence of the guards is required.

We use Gaussian moment bounds that do not count unused coordinates. For \(Y_j = a_j + \eta_{t, j}\) and every fixed integer \(r \geqslant 1\), the one-dimensional Gaussian moment formula and \(|a_j |, \text{Var} \hspace{0.17em} \eta_{t, j} \leqslant B\) give \(\mathbb{E} (1 + |Y_j |)^r \leqslant c_r (1 + B)^r\). For a product of at most \(r\) such factors, the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality bounds its expectation by the largest of these marginal \(r\)th moments, padding with factors one if needed. The bound applies also to repeated coordinates, without independence. Thus expectations of the absolute values of monomials of fixed degree, and of their squares, have bounds polynomial in \(B\) depending only on that degree.

On \(\Gamma > 0\) all the coordinate comparisons hold simultaneously. Expand each \(p_I\) into monomials and replace the factors one at a time. For every product monomial there are at most \(d\) replacements, each costing \(\epsilon\) times at most \(d - 1\) factors bounded by \(1 + |Y_j |\). The sum of absolute coefficients of the product expansion is at most \(A^l\). Integrating the preceding moment bound proves

\(\displaystyle \int \Gamma \left| \prod_{I \in \pi} p_I (\xi_{t, I}) - P (Y) \right| d \mu_t \leqslant \epsilon A^l c_d (1 + B)^d .\)

The case \(d = 0\) has zero error. This estimate and the Gaussian moment bound also prove integrability of the guarded physical product. On \(\Gamma = 0\) its value is defined as zero, so no control of physical coordinates on a failed guard is needed.

To remove the guard from \(P (Y)\), Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \int (1 - \Gamma) P (Y) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_t \right| \leqslant \left( \int (1 - \Gamma)^2 d \mu_t \right)^{1 / 2} \left( \int |P (Y) |^2 d \mu_t \right)^{1 / 2} \leqslant \sqrt{l} \hspace{0.17em} \epsilon A^l c_d' (1 + B)^d .\)

Here \((1 - \Gamma)^2 \leqslant 1 - \Gamma\), and the second moment follows by expanding \(P \bar{P}\), of degree at most \(2 d\) and coefficient norm at most \(A^{2 l}\).

Finally compare the Gaussian expectations of \(P\) with covariances \(C_t\) and \(C_{\ast}\). The mean is the same. In the pairing formula for a monomial of degree at most \(d\), subtract corresponding products one factor at a time. Every difference contains a covariance difference at most \(\epsilon\), and at most \(d\) remaining bounded mean or covariance factors. The number of pairings depends only on \(d\). Hence the difference is at most \(\epsilon A^l c_d'' (1 + B)^d\). Adding the three errors and averaging with \(\lambda_t\) preserves the bound. There are only a number depending on \(n\) of subsets and partitions. Summing gives the moment bound and bounds both families of moments by a polynomial in \(A, B\).

The constant coefficients of both moment elements are one: the empty product has no guard and every \(\mu_t\) is a probability measure. Apply the finite logarithm estimate of Lemma 14.20 in the scalar marker algebra. This proves the connected bound with an enlarged polynomial.\(\Box\)

The construction is simultaneous in the modes. Although the allowed gauge rotation can differ from one polynomial to another, the array \(\eta_t\) and its measure are common to all of them. Selecting an independent Gaussian sample for each factor would destroy the cross covariances in \(M^{\ast}_S\) and invalidate the argument.

Corollary 14.29. (The discrepancy purchase from a joint guarded law) At each leading rung and every healthy coarse configuration, suppose the principal-component integral is the moment element \(M\) in Lemma 14.28, and the prescribed next polynomial is \(\log_{\ast} M^{\ast}\) for the same mean, covariance and incoming polynomials. Let \(A, B \leqslant H^c\), with fixed \(c \geqslant 0\). For every fixed requested \(q\), one can choose \(f\) before the final coupling threshold so that \(\epsilon \leqslant H^{- f}\) implies (14.118), uniformly in the rungs and healthy configurations.

Proof. Write \(r\) for the total degree of \(P_{n, D}\) and \(C\) for the sum of its coefficients. The lemma bounds the discrepancy by \(y \leqslant CH^{cr - f}\). Choose \(f > q + cr\). Then \(2^n R_e H^q y \leqslant 2^n R_e CH^{q + cr - f} \leqslant 1\) eventually. All constants are uniform by hypothesis. No parameter derivatives of a guard have been taken. Alternatively any strictly negative upper exponent for \(\epsilon\), with exponent-zero \(A, B\), pays every such fixed monitor power.\(\Box\)

Lemma 14.30. (Reader comparison for an adjacent pair of stages) Fix an actual adjacent step, its fine block side \(L\), its predictor, the two attached paths and clips, and its precision horizon. Suppose the two stages have positive strip widths \(s_f, s_c\), strip bound bounds \(z_f, z_c\), and positive metric floors \(a_f, a_c\) on the fine and coarse beds respectively. Retain the exact factorization, stationarity and transport identities of Subsection 14.5 for these two beds. Use the common strip and estimates

\(\displaystyle s_{\wedge} = \min (s_f, s_c), \qquad z_{\vee} = \max (z_f, z_c), \qquad a = a_f .\)

Assume the local Gaussian, guard and room hypotheses of Subsection 14.9, with these bounds, including its predictor purchase before \(L\) and its actual coupling band. Then its joint reader conclusions hold on this step, whether or not its two stage indices agree. In particular, for any fixed \(f\), at sufficiently large station scale \(s\), uniformly for the reader parameter \(x \in [s / 2, s]\), the covariance and physical-coordinate errors are at most \(H^{- f}\) and each guard loses mass at most \(H^{- 2 f}\), where \(H = \exp ((\log \log s)^2)\). The choices are uniform in periods, fields, forests and tags within the stated purchased classes. They can be made simultaneously for any fixed finite list of admissible adjacent stage pairs.

Proof. Restriction to a smaller strip decreases every exponentially weighted row sum. Replacing either estimate by \(z_{\vee}\) increases its majorant. Thus both adjacent rows satisfy their original estimates at \(s_{\wedge}, z_{\vee}\). The fine precision keeps its fine metric floor \(a_f\); no equality with the coarse metric is asserted. In particular, the coarse metric and the diagonal operators in the two-form transport are not replaced by fine-stage objects.

The tree calculation in Subsection 14.5 starts with separate metrics \(M_f, M_c\) and separate curls on the two tori. Its exact conclusion is

\(\displaystyle (DI_F) A_F^{- 1} (DI_F)^{\textsf{T}} = P_f - JP_c J^{\textsf{T}} .\)

It uses the retained root-gradient, stationarity and curvature intertwining identities, not equality of stage indices. The gap on the fine tree slice uses \(a_f\) and the predictor penalty. Hence the common inverse and cover-row construction of Subsection 14.6 applies with the fixed adjacent patch and these bounds. The installed transport row uses the actual fine inverse diagonal, tower and coarse diagonal; their row-product estimate is valid at the common strip width. The two omitted tails therefore have bounds \(Ce^{- ch_x}\) with \(C < \infty\), \(c > 0\) fixed by this pair and \(h_x = \lfloor \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil / 10000 \rfloor\). They are eventually at most \(x^{- 1 / 8}\), uniformly in the period.

The actual-to-flat column comparison and removal of the bare path cut in Subsection 14.10 use the same fine tree precision and the same adjacent predictor. Their proofs give the actual reader covariance error \(x^{- 1 / 8}\) and the corresponding mean estimate for this pair. Combining the covariance error with the cover tail gives \(2 x^{- 1 / 8}\). The mean comparison retains the actual coarse coupling in its forecast. The one-pose construction and the nonlinear guarded comparison use the stipulated room and coupling inequalities; their estimates are \(x^{- 1 / 32}\) for the normalized physical error and \(x^{- 1}\) for lost guard mass. None of these arguments changes the horizon, forest, recording parameter or sample space.

Finally \(2 x^{- 1 / 8} \leqslant H^{- f}\), \(x^{- 1 / 32} \leqslant H^{- f}\) and \(x^{- 1} \leqslant H^{- 2 f}\) eventually, uniformly for \(x \in [s / 2, s]\), by (14.62). This proves the asserted joint comparison, including off-diagonal covariance entries. For a finite list of pairs first retain each pair's constants and then take the maximum of its finitely many entrance thresholds. This is not a maximum over all stage indices or all periods. A growing stage profile must place each such finite purchase before its own delay.\(\Box\)

Attachment to the actual adjacent step.
For the actual joint reader model of Subsection 14.15, the probability space is (14.90), the physical field is (14.91), and the joint incidence vector is its stated linear image of the same Gaussian coordinate. The guards are the individual reader guards, retained inside each product. With the common-trajectory and prior-purchase hypotheses of Subsection 14.9, (14.66) supplies \(|C_t - C_{\ast} | \leqslant H^{- f}\), where \(C_{\ast}\) is the prescribed short covariance. That section's last subsection supplies guard loss at most \(H^{- 2 f}\) and the normalized physical-coordinate error at most \(H^{- f}\). Its parent forecast is exactly the mean of the prescribed short step, with the actual coarse coupling retained. Thus it supplies the joint-law hypotheses of the lemma on a constant-order plateau. At a stage change use Lemma 14.30 with the actual adjacent beds, the fine block side and the common horizon. Its explicit local hypotheses must hold for that pair. The entry bounds and the prescribed polynomial coefficient bounds provide the \(H^c\) bounds, after a fixed increase of \(c\).

The component identification uses Subsection 14.4, specifically its split \(W_h = \Gamma_h W_h + (1 - \Gamma_h) W_h\) on this same measure. An entirely leading component contains no body letter; compatibility then excludes background packets. Its integrand is therefore the product of the guarded principal values. On each positive guard the fine field is healthy on the full original mask support, so the masked value is \(p_I (\xi_{t, I})\). Whole-mode selection and (14.3) sum these products over the partitions. This gives precisely \(M\), with empty coefficient one, under the compiler's same-guard and same-measure hypotheses. Invariance removes the one gauge supplied on each patch. The same mean and short covariance in the polynomial step give \(M^{\ast}\) by finite Wick contraction. Consequently Corollary 14.29 applies to these actual principal components. No claim about the full interacting block measure being Gaussian is used.

This identifies the local discrepancy supplier from the canonical local estimates, also for an adjacent pair satisfying Lemma 14.30. In the selected history the pair uses the minimum of its strip widths, the fine metric floor, the maximum of its bounds and the fine stage's block side. The actual horizon must supply the algebraic identities and room on this pair; scalar bounds cannot replace them. Separately, the upper compiler's body, all-hole and warning purchases are required at \(e_{\ast} = e + 1 + \sigma\). They do not follow from this reader comparison or from the numerical stage-boundary lemma.

This route retains the prescribed polynomial class: the reference moments are finite Wick pairings, followed by the finite connected logarithm. Its application on the selected course retains the explicit upstream hypotheses of the cited reader and compiler chapters. The health and covariance hypotheses must cover every read of every partition product. The component's unit normalization is the empty probability integral, not the mass of one insertion's guard. The existing common-cutoff route is a separate sufficient criterion; it must not be substituted for this joint construction without proving its additional measure identities.

Lemma 14.31. (The remaining step purchases) For all sufficiently large \(s\), uniformly in the rungs of the leading interval, the following hold:

  1. the second purchase in (14.114), with \(G_i = G (x_i)\);

  2. both purchases in (14.115), with \(B_i = \lceil (\log x_i)^2 \rceil^2\).

Proof. Second purchase. By Subsection 14.4, \(G\) decays faster than every negative power of \(H\) uniformly on \([s / 2, s]\). So eventually \(G (x_i) \leqslant H^{- 1} / 2\). The quantity \(nR_{e + 1}\) is a constant by Lemma 14.17, so \(nR_{e + 1} H^{- 2} \leqslant H^{- 1} / 2\) once \(H \geqslant 2 nR_{e + 1}\).

Smallness. For \(\log s \geqslant 1\) and \(x_i \leqslant s\), \(B_i \leqslant ((\log s)^2 + 1)^2 \leqslant 4 (\log s)^4\). Hence

\(\displaystyle (2 B_i + 1)^4 \leqslant 9^4 (\log s)^{16} = \exp \{4 \log 9 + 16 \log \log s\} \leqslant \exp \{(\log \log s)^2 \} = H\)

once \(\log \log s \geqslant 17\). Also \(e^{H^{- 1}} - 1 \leqslant 1\) as soon as \(H^{- 1} \leqslant \log 2\).\(\Box\)

The step count of the recurrence is Lemma 14.45 below. It uses only \(L_j \geqslant 3\) and the maximality of \(\ell\); no floor on the block lengths and no stage condition is needed.

What this supplies and what remains.
On the leading interval, the lemmas of this subsection and Lemma 14.45 give the following, all at the weight \(\omega_-\) and eventually in \(s\) uniformly in the finite period:

  • the remainder multiplier \((\alpha + 1) z\);

  • the principal incidence bound \(H^{- 2}\);

  • the forced bound \(G_i^2\) with \(G_i = G (x_i)\);

  • the leading budget \(H^{- 4}\), given the unweighted bound \(H^A\);

  • the discrepancy purchase (14.118);

  • the purchases (14.114)–(14.115);

  • the step count, with remainder \(d_{k - \ell} \leqslant H^{\kappa - q}\) at the middle level.

The following inputs are not supplied here.

  1. Applicability of the upper calculation at each leading rung. This includes its primitive body-compiler, all-hole and joint-warning purchases, which Subsection 14.4 lists as inputs on the same body alphabet and coarse representation.

  2. The hypotheses of Subsection 14.17 at every leading rung, which give the unweighted principal-source bound \(H^A\) by Lemma 14.15, and the terminal-source purchase: the launched insertions must give the initial remainder bound \(d_0\) and forced bound \(G_k\) at the weight \(\omega_-\), and, for the leading family of calibrated singletons, masks inspected on the prescribed supports \(X_{c, \{j\}}\).

  3. The common insertion (CI) of Lemma 14.26, or its clauses (a),(c) together with the local transport error of Lemma 14.27, or the joint guarded law of Corollary 14.29. That the compiler integrates the masked moments \(Q^{(i)}_T\) follows from the realignment by Lemma 14.22, given the form of the top-level principal insertion in item 2 and the measure identification (CI)(a) of the planted sector and tag measures with those of the principal comparison. The remaining clauses say that the Gaussian history is transported by the reference integrals on those same insertions and is the one used at the middle level, and that the comparison data are uniform over the healthy base configurations. The bounded transport and the inverse bound for the reference mass follow from the latter by Lemma 14.18, and the normalization is then handled by Lemma 14.25. The amplitude bound at every leading rung follows from item 2 by Corollary 14.16.

  4. The reader separation \(2 QB\) between distinct replica cells assumed in Lemma 14.17.

The rungs below the middle level use the lower calculation of Subsection 14.4 at the large weight \(\omega\), after the launch of Subsection 14.18.8.

14.18.6The scalar envelope used in the replica estimate

We record the final elementary step linking a paid marked representation to Subsection 14.19. Suppose a polynomial density on a torus of \(q_0\) sites has the form

\(\displaystyle D (U) = e^{\text{exponent}_P (U)} \exp_{\ast} \left( \sum_a z_{I_a} h_a (U) \right) \sum_{B \text{admissible}} \prod_{b \in B} K_b (U) .\)

The \(I_a\) are nonempty, and each background packet has nonempty full spatial support. Let \(\| \cdot \|_{\boldsymbol{r}}\) denote the coefficient \(\ell^1\) norm weighted by positive marker weights. Assume regular incidence bound at most \(\ell\) per mark and forced spatial bound at most \(L\) per site, with nonnegative support exponent. If there are \(M_0\) marks in total, double counting gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_a \|z_{I_a} h_a \|_{\boldsymbol{r}} \leqslant M_0 \ell, \qquad \sum_b \|K_b \|_{\boldsymbol{r}} \leqslant q_0 L.\)

A nonempty support contributes at least once to the corresponding incidence sum. Dropping the support weight is legitimate because it is at least one. Masks can only lower the regular value bounds. Submultiplicativity gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \exp_{\ast} \left( \sum_a z_{I_a} h_a \right) \right|_{\boldsymbol{r}} \leqslant e^{M_0 \ell} .\)

Dropping the admissibility restrictions in the nonnegative majorant bounds the forced sum by \(\prod_b (1 +\|K_b \|_{\boldsymbol{r}}) \leqslant e^{q_0 L}\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle \|D (U)\|_{\boldsymbol{r}} \leqslant \exp \{ \text{exponent}_P (U) + q_0 L + M_0 \ell\} .\) (14.123)

For the replica estimate, first complete any later propagation to the selected output torus whose site count is exactly the number \(q_0\) of replica cells. This equality is a property of that selected output level, not of an arbitrary intermediate torus. At this output there are \(M_0 = q_0 n\) marks. Thus \(L, \ell \leqslant 1\) give per-site expense at most \(1 + n\), exactly the arity dependence in the determinant ticket. The weight of the coefficient carrying a chosen nonempty subset \(I\) in each of the \(q_0\) cells is the \(q_0\)th power of its single-cell weight. At the constant weight (14.116) this is \(\omega^{q_0 |I|}\), and since the coefficient norm dominates each weighted coefficient, (14.123) gives pointwise, for the coefficient \(D_I^{\otimes} (U)\) of the monomial carrying \(I\) in every cell,

\(\displaystyle \omega^{q_0 |I|} \hspace{0.17em} | D_I^{\otimes} (U) | \leqslant \exp \{ \text{exponent}_P (U) + q_0 (L + n \ell)\} .\)

With \(u = M\) the marker allowance of Subsection 14.19, \(\omega =\mathcal{M}_k^{1 / (8 M)} = e^{2 I_k}\) is the weight \(a\) used there, because \(I_k = \log \mathcal{M}_k / (16 M)\). Hence this is the coefficient bound \(a^{q_0 |I|}\) with per-site expense \(X = L + n \ell \leqslant 1 + M\) required there, on the same pushed density. Apply the normalization estimate and then take the \(q_0\)th root. The denominator is the partition function of the same pushed density, preserved by the unmarked coefficient identity at every step.

The recurrence above states its one-step hypotheses quantitatively. The joint Gaussian reader estimate supplies the discrepancy \(y\) and the principal source bounds; the mixed compiler supplies the exact marked push and the pre-realignment bounds. The selected scale construction must buy all of these on the common history, including the later propagation below \(l\). Neither the recurrence nor the scalar envelope asserts those upstream estimates without their corresponding purchases.

14.18.7Transport to the output torus

We complete the propagation in the application to the pairwise receipt. The chart has level \(k\), middle level \(\ell\) and root depth \(w \leqslant \ell\), and the arity is \(n = 2\). We identify the marked density at the output, show that its pushed version below the middle level keeps bounds at most one, and conclude with the coefficient bound of Subsection 14.19. As before, \(s = \hat{\beta}_k\), \(H =\mathcal{M}_k\), \(\omega = H^{1 / (8 u)}\), and the fixed constants \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_e\) and \(q \geqslant 6\) are those of Subsection 14.18.5.

The invoice and its parts.
Let \(f_0, f_1\) be the two finite-volume insertions of a fit configuration, as in Subsection 18.22. Let \(S_0 (T)\) (\(T \subseteq \{0, 1\}\)) be the reference moments at the rounded configuration, with \(S_0 (\varnothing) = 1\). In the marker algebra \(\mathscr{A}_{\{0, 1\}}\) of Subsection 14.1, the polynomial \(\sum_T S_0 (T) z_T\) has constant coefficient one. So it is \(\exp_{\ast} D\) for a unique \(D\) with zero constant coefficient, namely
\(\displaystyle D = j_0 z_0 + j_1 z_1 + \kappa z_0 z_1, \qquad j_c = S_0 (\{c\}), \quad \kappa = S_0 (\{0, 1\}) - j_0 j_1 .\)

Indeed \(\exp_{\ast} D = 1 + D + D^2 / 2\) and \(D^2 = 2 j_0 j_1 z_0 z_1\). The invoice is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{I}= \exp_{\ast} (- D) \hspace{0.17em} (1 + f_0 z_0) (1 + f_1 z_1) .\)

Lemma 14.32. (The parts of the invoice) The coefficients of \(\mathcal{I}\) are

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{I}_{\{c\}} = f_c - j_c \quad (c = 0, 1), \qquad \mathcal{I}_{\{0, 1\}} = (f_0 - j_0) (f_1 - j_1) - \kappa,\)

that is, \(\mathcal{I}_s = \Phi_s\) for every nonempty \(s\).

Proof. First, \(\exp_{\ast} (- D) = 1 - D + D^2 / 2 = 1 - j_0 z_0 - j_1 z_1 + (j_0 j_1 - \kappa) z_0 z_1\). Multiply by \(1 + f_0 z_0 + f_1 z_1 + f_0 f_1 z_0 z_1\), using \(z_c^2 = 0\). The coefficient of \(z_c\) is \(f_c - j_c\). The coefficient of \(z_0 z_1\) is

\(\displaystyle f_0 f_1 - j_0 f_1 - j_1 f_0 + j_0 j_1 - \kappa = (f_0 - j_0) (f_1 - j_1) - \kappa .\)

\(\Box\)

Cells and sites.
The cells of the centered tiling at depth \(w\) of the level-\(N\) torus are indexed by the sites of the level-\(w\) torus, one cell for each site. Hence the number \(q_0\) of replica cells equals the site count of the level-\(w\) torus. This is the equality required in the scalar envelope: the output torus is the level-\(w\) torus.
Propagation below the middle level.
On the rungs \(j\) with \(w \leqslant j < \ell\) there are no leading letters, and the lower calculation of Subsection 14.4 applies. All bounds from the launch at \(\ell\) downwards are taken at the large weight \(\omega = H^{1 / (8 u)}\). With incoming regular marker-root bound \(z \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\) and incoming forced budget \(z \hspace{0.17em} \omega_j\), where \(\omega_j \in [0, 1]\) is the actual lower-interval attenuation, the lower calculation gives two outputs:

  • regular marker-root bound at most \(\tfrac{1}{2} (\alpha z + H^{1 / 2} z^2) \leqslant \alpha z + H^{1 / 2} z^2\);

  • forced site bound at most \((e^{\Sigma} - 1) / m \leqslant G (y_j)\), where \(m = G (y_j)^{- 1}\) and \(y_j\) is the fine coupling of the rung.

These require the rung couplings to lie in \([s / 2, s]\).

Lemma 14.33. (Lower propagation) Assume the following.

  1. At level \(\ell\) the regular marker-root bound is at most \(z_0 \leqslant H^{- 3 / 4}\), and there are no leading letters.

  2. Every rung of \([w, \ell)\) satisfies the hypotheses of the lower calculation, with couplings in \([s / 2, s]\).

  3. The forced input of each lower rung is admissible: its forced budget is at most \(z_t \hspace{0.17em} \omega_j\) along the iteration below. Bounds are upper bounds, so \(z_t\) may be enlarged for this.

  4. The lower step count satisfies

    \(\displaystyle (\alpha + 1)^{\ell - w} \leqslant H^{1 / 4} .\) (14.124)

Let \(z_t\) be the regular bound after \(t\) lower steps, so that level \(\ell - t\) carries \(z_t\). Then \(z_t \leqslant (\alpha + 1)^t z_0 \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2} \leqslant 1\) for \(0 \leqslant t \leqslant \ell - w\), and every forced site bound on \([w, \ell]\) is at most \(1\).

Proof. If \(H^{1 / 2} z_t \leqslant 1\), then

\(\displaystyle z_{t + 1} \leqslant (\alpha + H^{1 / 2} z_t) z_t \leqslant (\alpha + 1) z_t .\)

By induction \(z_t \leqslant (\alpha + 1)^t z_0\) for \(t \leqslant \ell - w\), as long as \(H^{1 / 2} z_t \leqslant 1\). This condition holds throughout, because \((\alpha + 1)^t z_0 \leqslant H^{1 / 4} H^{- 3 / 4} = H^{- 1 / 2}\) by (14.124). That is also the domain bound \(z \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\) of the lower calculation. The forced outputs of the lower rungs are at most \(G (y_j) \leqslant 1\). At \(t = 0\) the forced bound is the one delivered by the launch, which is at most \(1\) by Proposition 14.43.\(\Box\)

By Lemma 14.44 below, the lower count (14.124) holds for all large \(s\), uniformly in the chart, and by Lemma 14.46 hypothesis 3 can be dropped.

Proposition 14.34. (The coefficient bound at the output) Assume the following.

  1. The launch receipt of Proposition 14.43 at the middle level.

  2. The hypotheses of Lemma 14.33.

  3. The representation at the output level \(w\) is lawful, with the suppositions of Subsection 14.19 at the stage of level \(w\).

Then, at the weight \(\omega = H^{1 / (8 u)}\), the following hold for every nonempty \(s \subseteq \{0, 1\}\):

\(\displaystyle \omega^{q_0 |s|} \hspace{0.17em} | \mathcal{D}_s^{\otimes} (U) | \leqslant \exp \{ \text{exponent}_P (U) + q_0 (1 + n)\} .\)

Here \(\mathcal{D}\) is the push to the level-\(w\) torus of the replicated marked density of the invoice \(\mathcal{I}\), and \(\mathcal{D}_s^{\otimes}\) is its coefficient carrying \(s\) in every cell. The normalizing denominator is the partition function of the same pushed density, by the unmarked coefficient identity at every step. Taking \(u = M\), so that \(\omega = a\), this is the coefficient bound \(a^{q_0 |s|}\) with per-site expense \(X \leqslant 1 + n \leqslant 1 + M\) required by (14.133). Consequently, for \(u = M\) and \(k \geqslant K_M\) of Proposition 14.50, the root test of \(\Phi_s\) on the centered tiling at depth \(w\) is at most \(\mathcal{M}_k^{- 1 / (16 M)}\).

Proof. By Lemma 14.33, at level \(w\) the regular incidence bound per mark is \(\ell_w \leqslant 1\) and the forced site bound is \(L_w \leqslant 1\). By the cells-and-sites paragraph, the output torus has \(q_0\) sites, and there are \(M_0 = q_0 n\) marks. The scalar envelope (14.123) and the paragraph after it give the displayed inequality with expense \(L_w + n \ell_w \leqslant 1 + n\). By Lemma 14.32, the coefficients of the invoice are the \(\Phi_s\), so the replicated coefficient is that of \(\Phi_s\) in every cell. The last statement is Proposition 14.50, applied with \(w (k) = w\), together with (14.133) and the lawful representation at \(w\). For general \(u\) the weight is \(\omega = H^{1 / (8 u)}\) and the same argument gives the bound \(H^{1 / (16 M) - |s| / (8 u)}\) only; the root conclusion uses \(u = M\).\(\Box\)

14.18.8The launch at the middle level

At the middle level \(\ell\) the representation produced by the leading interval is converted into one suitable for the lower interval. Three things change at once: the marker weight is raised from \(\omega_-\) to \(\omega\); the leading family is replaced by its corrected form; and the leading designation is dropped, so that the corrected pieces become ordinary regular letters. We keep the notation of Subsections 14.18.5 and 14.18.7. In particular \(\mathcal{I}= \exp_{\ast} (- D_R) \prod_c (1 + f_c z_c)\) is the invoice, with \(D_R = D\) the reference cumulant polynomial.

The reference factor commutes with every push.
The polynomial \(\exp_{\ast} (- D_R)\) has field-independent coefficients. Every block integration and every push acts linearly and coefficientwise on the marker algebra. So each of them commutes with multiplication by \(\exp_{\ast} (- D_R)\). Hence the pushed invoice at any level is \(\exp_{\ast} (- D_R)\) times the pushed product of the insertions. At the middle level the latter has principal part \(\exp_{\ast} (D_Q)\), with \(D_Q\) the connected polynomial of the same Gaussian history. The invoice is therefore launched at the middle level with corrected principal polynomial \(D_Q - D_R\). This is an identity of densities. It does not by itself give a priced representation, for the following reason.
The failed mask.
For one mode, let \(\chi\) be the healthy mask of its prescribed support, \(W\) the actual leading coefficient and \(R\) the corresponding coefficient of \(D_R\). The leading letter carries \(\chi W\), while the reference factor contributes \(R\) everywhere. Hence
\(\displaystyle \chi W - R = \chi (W - R) - (1 - \chi) R.\)

The first term is a regular letter controlled by the healthy bound on \(W - R\) (Lemma 14.35). The second term is supported where the prescribed support contains a flag. It must be moved into the forced alphabet and regrouped with the forced background, as in the mask change of the realignment subsection. Its bound is controlled by a bound on the reference coefficients \(R\), not by the healthy bound.

The corrected budget.
The bound on the corrected pieces is already proved in Subsection 14.16. There the free moment polynomial \(q'\) is the reference moment polynomial \(1 + X_R\), and the moment polynomial \(q\) of the same Gaussian history is \(1 + X_Q\). If the coefficientwise moment error is at most \(H^{- 3 / 2}\), then (14.103) gives \(\|D_Q - D_R \|_1 + H^{- 3 / 2} \leqslant H^{- 31 / 32}\) for all large \(s\). That moment error is (14.102) at the powers of its scale paragraph. The actual healthy local error between a planted leading coefficient and the coefficient of \(D_Q\) is at most \(H^{- 3 / 2}\) by Subsection 14.17. Both statements hold under the hypotheses stated in those sections.

Lemma 14.35. (Corrected leading pieces) Assume the moment error \(H^{- 3 / 2}\) of Subsection 14.16 and the healthy local error \(H^{- 3 / 2}\) of Subsection 14.17, at the middle level. Then, for all large \(s\), every corrected leading piece at that level, that is, an actual leading piece minus the corresponding coefficient of \(D_R\), is at most \(H^{- 31 / 32}\) on its healthy region. Its incidence bound at the weight \(\omega\) is at most

\(\displaystyle h = c \hspace{0.17em} H^{- 27 / 32}, \qquad c = 2^n R_e .\)

Proof. The first assertion is the triangle inequality applied to the healthy local error and (14.103), as in the last paragraph of Subsection 14.16. Each coefficient is bounded by the coefficient \(\ell^1\) norm. For the bound, a piece of mode \(I\) carries weight \(\omega^{|I|} \leqslant H^{1 / 8}\) by (14.119), and support charge at most \(R_e\) by Lemma 14.17. At most \(2^n\) modes contain a given mark. Hence the incidence bound is at most \(2^n R_e H^{1 / 8} H^{- 31 / 32} = cH^{- 27 / 32}\).\(\Box\)

Lemma 14.36. (Raising the regular marker weight) For each regular letter let \(I\) be its marker set, \(X\) its full support, and \(t (I)\) the number of replica cells represented in \(I\). Suppose the marker-root sum of letters with \(t (I) = 1\) is at most \(p_1\) at weight \(\omega_-\) and support exponent \(e\). Suppose the corresponding sum of letters with \(t (I) \geqslant 2\) is at most \(p_2\) at weight \(\omega_-\) and support exponent \(e + \sigma\), where \(\sigma > 0\), and that each of these letters satisfies

\(\displaystyle \sigma \text{cov}_B (X) \geqslant Un \hspace{0.17em} t (I) \log H.\)

Then the marker-root bound at weight \(\omega\) and support exponent \(e\) is at most \(H^{Un} p_1 + p_2\).

Proof. Empty marker sets contribute to neither sum. For a letter with \(t (I) = 1\), \(|I| \leqslant n\), so raising the weight costs at most \(H^{Un}\). For a letter with \(t (I) \geqslant 2\), \(|I| \leqslant nt (I)\). Raising the weight and lowering the support exponent together multiply its bound by

\(\displaystyle H^{U |I|} \exp \{- \sigma \text{cov}_B (X)\} \leqslant 1.\)

Summing at each fixed marker gives the claim, uniformly in the number of replica cells.\(\Box\)

The recurrence at exponent \(e\) supplies neither the bound of the multi-cell letters with a spare exponent \(\sigma\) nor the support inequality of the lemma. Both are obtained in Lemma 14.41 below, from one spare-exponent run of the leading interval and from the geometry of the halos.

The forced background is not covered by this lemma: a forced component may contain marked pieces from several cells, and its weight may then grow by more than \(H^{Un}\). Its weight change is paid together with the failed-mask pieces, from a spare support exponent and from the smallness of the forced background.

Geometric and pricing hypotheses at the launch.
For a cell \(c\) let \(Y_c = \bigcup_I X_{c, I}\) be its halo, the union of its prescribed supports. For a support \(X\) let \(t (X)\) be the number of cells \(c\) with \(X \cap Y_c \ne \varnothing\). We assume:
    (F)

    at the middle level, the forced background has site bound at most \(\eta_-\) at the weight \(\omega_-\) and at the support exponent \(e + 1 + \sigma\), for a fixed spare exponent \(\sigma > 0\). The full support of every marked forced piece contains the reader sites of its marks;

    (S)

    there is \(\lambda = \lambda (s)\) with \(\lambda / \log H \to \infty\) such that every full support \(X\) of a forced piece with \(t (X) \geqslant 2\) satisfies \(\text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X \geqslant \lambda \hspace{0.17em} t (X)\).

The site-bound clause of (F), with \(\eta_- = G (x_{\ell})\), and the bound of the regular remainder used in Lemma 14.41 both come from running the leading interval at the larger exponent \(e_{\ast} = e + 1 + \sigma\). We fix \(\sigma \in (0, 1]\) together with \(e\), before \(\alpha\). The inputs of Subsection 14.18.5 are then required at \(e_{\ast}\):

    (E)

    The leading interval at the exponent \(e_{\ast}\). The inputs listed at the end of Subsection 14.18.5, namely the applicability of the upper calculation at each leading rung and the terminal-source purchase, hold with the support exponent \(e_{\ast}\) in place of \(e\). The step multiplier satisfies \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_{e_{\ast}}\).

This replaces the run at the exponent \(e\): by monotonicity of \(\text{chg}_e\) in \(e\), every bound at \(e_{\ast}\) bounds the bound of the same letters at \(e\).

Lemma 14.37. (The run at the exponent \(e_{\ast}\)) Assume (E) and the remaining inputs of Subsection 14.18.5. Then, for all large \(s\), the conclusions of that subsection hold at the support exponent \(e_{\ast}\). In particular, at the middle level the nonleading regular remainder has marker-root bound at most \(H^{\kappa - q}\), and the realigned forced background has site bound at most \(G (x_{\ell})\), both at the weight \(\omega_-\) and the exponent \(e_{\ast}\). The exponent \(\kappa\) is the same as at \(e\).

Proof. That subsection uses the support exponent only through the constants \(R_e\), \(R_{e + 1}\) and \(T_e\), and through the inputs, which (E) supplies at \(e_{\ast}\). The constants \(R_{e_{\ast}}\) and \(R_{e_{\ast} + 1}\) of Lemma 14.17 are fixed before \(s\). The multiplier condition \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_{e_{\ast}}\) is part of (E), and it implies \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_e\) because \(T_e\) is increasing in \(e\). Every purchase of the subsection is an eventual inequality in \(s\) with these constants. Hence the proofs apply verbatim at \(e_{\ast}\). The exponent \(\kappa\) of Lemma 14.45 depends only on \(\alpha\) and \(\bar{b}\).\(\Box\)

The support purchase of the upper calculation at a fixed exponent does not need to be bought separately on the selected history. In Subsection 14.4 it is the block-length condition \(10 \cdot 15^4 (e_{\ast} + 7) \leqslant Le_{\ast}\), since the incoming exponent of the upper calculation is its own exponent. The selected block sides tend to infinity. Indeed, (19.10) gives \(\log L_j \geqslant j^2 \lambda_j\) for \(j \geqslant j_{\ast}\), and \(\lambda_j \geqslant \log 101\) because \(\lambda_j\) includes the support exponent \(100\). So the condition holds at every step of a comparison window at a sufficiently large level. For the other compiler purchases one must specify the larger exponent before constructing the alphabet. There is no need to leave the spare exponent arbitrary: the weight-restoration argument only requires \(\sigma > 0\). The following choice matches the two uses of the same local compiler.

Proposition 14.38. (One compiler pays the support reserve) Let \(e > 0\) be the final support exponent. Choose \(0 < \sigma \leqslant 1\), put \(e_{\ast} = e + 1 + \sigma\) and \(E = e + 2\), and choose \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_E\). On an actual adjacent step retain the funded tube, paths, clips, alongside certificate, inherited-law and small remainder hypotheses of Subsection 14.11. Make its support and product-bound purchases for raw compiler exponent \(E\), using the local support-cost exponent \(\hat{E} = E + 8\), with the larger of the upper and lower multipliers in Subsection 14.4. Retain that construction's same-field selected all-pilot package. Lemma 14.12 supplies its all-compatible-palette and joint-warning transfer to the genuine corrected alphabet. Then the body, all-hole, joint-warning and forced-embedding inputs for the upper calculation at \(e_{\ast}\) and the lower calculation at \(e\) hold for one corrected alphabet and one coarse normalization. This choice does not depend on the marked family or on the incoming marker remainder bound \(z \in [H^{- q}, H^{- 4}]\).

Proof. The local handoff is stated for any fixed support exponent including its reserve. Apply it at \(\hat{E}\), before choosing the large coupling. Indeed the mixed compiler uses \(v = E + 5\) and cover charge \(v + 2 = E + 7\); \(\hat{E}\) leaves one further support reserve. The corresponding fine-to-coarse purchase is \(10 \cdot 15^4 (E + 7) \leqslant L \sigma_f\), with \(\sigma_f\) the incoming fine support exponent. It must be imposed before the block side is fixed. The raw compiler exponent and this larger local support-cost exponent are different parameters. Its two rooted word inventories, including the first coarse-field derivative needed for the determinant terms, use that same exponent. The itinerary ratio has positive exponential decay after including the fixed cover charge at \(\hat{E}\); increasing its entrance threshold pays both inventories. Its matrix, owner-energy and palette estimates are for the actual tube and actual representation. Thus the genuine body identity of Subsection 14.3 and its integrated product estimates use this one corrected alphabet, with its original forest/tag measure and positive loading.

The regular logarithm is formed from the same unmarked alphabet. Its absolute connected-cluster estimate is part of this compiler purchase. Proposition 14.1 then gives the identity for every deleted support, using the factors \(h_b = \exp (- c_b) - 1\) attached to the full supports of the logarithmic entries. Proposition 14.2 constructs the forced output by the same connected components and boundary factors. In particular, the normalization and forced activities are constructed before selecting the arbitrary marked family. They are not taken from a second coarse representation with similar bounds.

For the upper call the regular multiplier is

\(\displaystyle l (z) = \max (1, H^{- 3} / (\alpha z + H^{- q})) \leqslant \max (1, H^{q - 3}) .\)

Purchase the right-hand bound once, and purchase the maximum of the two forced multipliers \(G (x)^{- 2}\) and \(G (y)^{- 1}\). The owner margin in the local handoff is the one that pays this forced inflation. The corrected product and hole estimates are purchased with these multipliers, not recovered from an uninflated site sum. The upper numerical calculation then has body allowance \(K / 16\), raised hole allowance \(K / 4\) and boundary allowance \(K\), where \(K = H^{- 1 / 8}\). Its joint warning event remains the event on this same sector sample space; its mass bound \(x^{- 1}\) pays \(l (z) x^{- 1} \leqslant 1\) eventually. No estimate for a product of independent warning samples is substituted.

Now \(e_{\ast} \leqslant E\). For any full support \(X\),

\(\displaystyle \exp (e_{\ast} \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X) \leqslant \exp (E \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X) .\)

All site and rooted bounds are sums of nonnegative majorants. Lowering this exponent and either multiplier therefore preserves the body product, hole, boundary and forced-embedding estimates. The all-hole and density identities are unchanged, since these parameters occur only in their estimates. Also \(T_{e_{\ast}} \leqslant T_E\), so the same \(\alpha\) pays the numerical upper calculation at \(e_{\ast}\). Lowering to \(e\) gives the lower calculation from the identical alphabet. The local thresholds are uniform in periods, stores, fields, forests and tags. The common-band selection of the handoff therefore supplies one threshold for \(x, y \in [s / 2, s]\) on this fixed adjacent patch, before the marked family and \(z\) are chosen.\(\Box\)

This proposition identifies the required analytic purchase rather than deriving it from growth of \(L\). It uses the local compiler's actual product and all-hole estimates, with their hypotheses, for raw exponent \(E\) and the local support reserve \(\hat{E}\). Choosing \(\sigma = 1\) makes \(e_{\ast} = E\), exactly its upper exponent. For a finite list of adjacent patches make these purchases on each patch and then take the maximum of their coupling thresholds. The stage profile must include them before choosing its delays, with the guide and support conditions imposed before the respective block sides. Proposition 13.38 supplies the exact starting presentation. Proposition 13.40 then constructs the actual restarted course, verifies the alongside and neutral-law hypotheses, and proves its genuine small-remainder, inflated-product and all-hole estimates on each adjacent step. Thus the local purchase above applies on that course under the funded chart/stamp contract, also across its adjacent-stage transition. Combining it with Lemma 14.30 supplies the Gaussian-reader and compiler inputs of (14.114)–(14.115) at the common marker weight, including the spare exponent \(e_{\ast}\). This attachment does not assert the upstream construction of the funded family or the subsequent identification of the prescribed marked insertion; those are the separate hypotheses of the propagation theorem.

The second part of (F) is inherited along the history. The reader site of a mark at the top level is the site of its insertion. At a coarser level we define the reader site of the mark to be the image of the reader site of the next finer level under the block map, that is, the coarse site whose block contains it. Say that a representation contains its readers if the full support of every letter carrying a mark contains the reader site of that mark at its level.

Lemma 14.39. (Reader containment is inherited) Suppose that at the top level every insertion letter contains the reader site of its mark. Suppose also that at every step the coarse footprint of a fine support contains the projections of its points. Then every representation of the comparison interval contains its readers, before and after realignment and after the launch. In particular the second part of (F) holds.

Proof. We argue by induction along the interval. Every letter of the coarse representation produced by the compiler is the flattening of a connected component of fine letters. Its full support contains the coarse footprint of the union of their supports, and its marker set is the union of their marker sets. If each fine marked letter contains its fine readers, then the component's support contains the fine reader of each of its marks. So the coarse footprint contains its projection, which is the coarse reader. The realignment replaces leading pieces by pieces on the prescribed supports \(X_{c, I}\), and these contain the readers because they contain the full support of the principal component of that mode by Lemma 14.17. The launch uses those same modes. The complementary mask pieces keep their original supports. The forced regrouping again takes unions over components. At the launch, the failed-mask pieces live on the \(X_{c, I}\), and the regrouping takes unions. Each operation therefore preserves the property.\(\Box\)

Hypothesis (S) holds as soon as the halos of distinct cells are at \(\ell^{\infty}\)-distance at least \(D'\), with \(D' / ((2 B + 1) \log H) \to \infty\). More precisely, every support \(X\), of a forced or a regular letter, with \(t (X) \geqslant 2\) then satisfies

\(\displaystyle \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X \geqslant \lambda \hspace{0.17em} t (X), \qquad \lambda = \frac{D'}{3 (2 B + 1)} .\) (14.125)

Indeed, use a connected cover of \(X\) by radius-\(B\) cubes and pick \(x_c \in X \cap Y_c\) for each of the \(t (X) \geqslant 2\) cells. The union of the cover contains a unit-step path from each \(x_c\) to another chosen point. Stop the path at distance \(\lfloor D' / 3 \rfloor\) from \(x_c\). Its distances from \(x_c\) take at least \(\lfloor D' / 3 \rfloor + 1\) distinct values, of which a single cube can cover at most \(2 B + 1\) since distance is 1-Lipschitz. The segments stay in their respective balls of radius \(D' / 3\), and no cube can meet two such balls once \(D' / 3 > 2 B + 1\). Thus \(\text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X \geqslant t (X) D' / (3 (2 B + 1))\), also for disconnected \(X\).

For the chart of the pairwise receipt this separation holds, with room to spare. Distances below are \(\ell^{\infty}\)-distances in physical units; at level \(i\) a lattice distance is the physical distance divided by \(a_i\).

Lemma 14.40. (Halo separation for the trace chart) Consider a chart at level \(k\) with middle level \(\ell\) and root depth \(w\), a configuration fit for it, and its replicas on the centered tiling at depth \(w\). Assume that at every level \(i\) of \([\ell, k]\) each reader site of a cell lies within distance \(2 a_{\ell}\) of the centre of that cell. Then for all sufficiently large \(s\), uniformly in the chart, the following hold at every level \(i\) of \([\ell, k]\), with \(B = B_i\):

  1. reader sites of distinct cells are at lattice distance greater than \(2 QB\);

  2. the halos of distinct cells are at lattice distance at least \(D' = c_k^{10} / 2\), and \(D' / ((2 B + 1) \log H) \to \infty\).

In particular the separation hypothesis of Lemma 14.17 and hypothesis (S) hold.

Proof. The cells of the centered tiling at depth \(w\) are blocks of side \(a_w\), and the centres of distinct cells are at distance at least \(a_w\). By the hypothesis, readers of distinct cells are at physical distance at least \(a_w - 4 a_{\ell}\). Since \(a_i \leqslant a_{\ell}\) for \(\ell \leqslant i \leqslant k\), their lattice distance at level \(i\) is at least \((a_w - 4 a_{\ell}) / a_{\ell} \geqslant c_k^{10} - 4\), by the definition of the root depth, \(a_w / a_{\ell} \geqslant c_k^{10}\). A halo lies within lattice distance \(QB\) of the readers of its cell, so the halos of distinct cells are at lattice distance at least \(c_k^{10} - 4 - 2 QB\).

The rung couplings lie in \([s / 2, s]\), so \(B_i = \lceil (\log x_i)^2 \rceil^2 \leqslant 4 (\log s)^4\) for \(\log s \geqslant 1\). Also \(c_k = \lceil (\log s)^2 \rceil \geqslant (\log s)^2\), so \(c_k^{10} \geqslant (\log s)^{20}\). Hence \(c_k^{10} - 4 - 2 QB \geqslant c_k^{10} / 2 > 2 QB\) for large \(s\), which gives both separations. Finally \(\log H = (\log \log s)^2\), so

\(\displaystyle \frac{D'}{(2 B + 1) \log H} \geqslant \frac{(\log s)^{20}}{2 (8 (\log s)^4 + 1) (\log \log s)^2} \longrightarrow \infty .\)

All thresholds depend only on \(Q\) and not on the chart.\(\Box\)

The hypothesis on the readers holds for fit configurations. Every coordinate of the grid configuration \(a_k \lceil mx \rceil\) has absolute value at most \(a_{\ell} / 8 + a_k\) relative to the base point. The replicas are its images in the cells, centred and reflected or conjugated with the cells. A reader site at a coarser level \(i\) is within one lattice spacing \(a_i \leqslant a_{\ell}\) of the corresponding insertion site. So each reader is within \(a_{\ell} / 8 + a_k + a_{\ell} \leqslant 2 a_{\ell}\) of its cell centre.

Lemma 14.41. (The regular remainder at the restored weight) Assume the hypotheses of Lemma 14.37, those of Lemma 14.39, and that the halos of distinct cells are at distance at least \(D'\) at the middle level, with \(D' / ((2 B + 1) \log H) \to \infty\). Assume \(q \geqslant \kappa + Un + 2\). Then, for all large \(s\), the nonleading regular remainder at the middle level has marker-root bound at most \(H^{- 1}\) at the weight \(\omega\) and the support exponent \(e\), uniformly in the replica cells and the period.

Proof. Split the remainder as in Lemma 14.36, according to \(t (I) = 1\) or \(t (I) \geqslant 2\). Bounds are monotone in the support exponent, since \(\text{chg}_e\) is increasing in \(e\). So Lemma 14.37 gives \(p_1 \leqslant H^{\kappa - q}\) at the exponent \(e\), and \(p_2 \leqslant H^{\kappa - q}\) at the exponent \(e + \sigma\), both at the weight \(\omega_-\).

Let a regular letter have marker set \(I\), with \(t (I) \geqslant 2\), and full support \(X\). By Lemma 14.39, \(X\) contains the reader site \(r_{c, j}\) of each of its marks \((c, j)\). Since \(r_{c, j} \in X_{c, \{j\}} \subseteq Y_c\), the support meets the halo of every cell represented in \(I\), so \(t (X) \geqslant t (I) \geqslant 2\). By (14.125),

\(\displaystyle \sigma \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X \geqslant \sigma \lambda \hspace{0.17em} t (I) \geqslant Un \hspace{0.17em} t (I) \log H\)

as soon as \(\sigma \lambda \geqslant Un \log H\). This holds for all large \(s\), because \(\lambda / \log H \to \infty\) and \(\sigma, U, n\) are fixed. This is the support inequality of Lemma 14.36. That lemma gives the bound \(H^{Un} p_1 + p_2 \leqslant 2 H^{Un + \kappa - q} \leqslant 2 H^{- 2} \leqslant H^{- 1}\) at the weight \(\omega\) and the exponent \(e\), for \(H \geqslant 2\).\(\Box\)

Lemma 14.42. (Failed-mask regrouping and weight change) Assume (F), (S) and the hypotheses of Lemma 14.35, and let \(\eta_- > 0\) decay faster than every negative power of \(H\). Then, for all large \(s\), the following holds. Move the failed-mask pieces \(- (1 - \chi) R \hspace{0.17em} z_I\) of all modes into the forced alphabet and regroup them with the forced background. The result is an exact representation of the same density, whose regular letters are unchanged. Its forced site bound at the weight \(\omega\) and the support exponent \(e\) is at most

\(\displaystyle 4 H^{Un + 2^n} \eta_- .\)

Proof. Exactness. The marker algebra is commutative, so

\(\displaystyle \exp_{\ast} (\chi W - R) = \exp_{\ast} (\chi (W - R)) \exp_{\ast} (- (1 - \chi) R)\)

letter by letter. Each failed-mask piece \(\ell_f = - (1 - \chi) R \hspace{0.17em} z_I\) has \(I \ne \varnothing\), so \(\ell_f^2 = 0\) and \(\exp_{\ast} (\ell_f) = 1 + \ell_f\). It vanishes where its prescribed support \(X_{c, I}\) is healthy, so a nonzero failed-mask factor has a flag in \(X_{c, I}\). Flags are covered by the inventories of the old forced pieces. Hence, in the expansion of the product of the factors \(1 + \ell_f\) with the old forced gas, every nonzero summand has each failed-mask factor meeting the support of an old forced piece. Taking connected components of the full-support intersection graph and flattening them is the exact regrouping of Subsection 8.32, as in the mask change of the realignment subsection. The regular letters are not touched. Setting all markers to zero kills every failed-mask factor, so the background is unchanged.

Bounds of the failed-mask pieces. By Lemma 14.47 and its proof, every reference coefficient satisfies \(|R| \leqslant C_{\ast}\). A failed-mask piece of mode \((c, I)\) therefore has bound at most \(p_f = H^{1 / 8} C_{\ast} R_{e + 1}\) at the weight \(\omega\) and the exponent \(e + 1\), by (14.119). Put \(F = \log H\) and give it the weight \(w_f = e^{- F} p_f\). At a fixed site, all failed-mask pieces containing it belong to one cell, and there are at most \(2^n\) modes. So their site sum is at most \(2^n C_{\ast} R_{e + 1} H^{- 7 / 8}\).

Bounds of the old forced pieces. Let \(o\) be an old forced piece with full support \(X_o\), marker set \(I_o\) and \(t_o = t (X_o)\). By (F), the readers of its marks lie in \(X_o\), hence in the halos it meets, so \(|I_o | \leqslant nt_o\). Raising the weight from \(\omega_-\) to \(\omega\) multiplies its bound by \(H^{U |I_o |} \leqslant H^{Unt_o}\). Lowering the support exponent from \(e + 1 + \sigma\) to \(e + 1\) multiplies it by \(e^{- \sigma \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X_o}\). Give \(o\) the weight

\(\displaystyle w_o = e^g \hspace{0.17em} e^{2^n Ft_o} \hspace{0.17em} p_o,\)

where \(p_o\) is its bound at \((\omega, e + 1)\) and \(g \geqslant 0\) is chosen below. Then \(w_o \leqslant e^g H^{(Un + 2^n) t_o} e^{- \sigma \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X_o} p_o^-\), with \(p_o^-\) its bound at \((\omega_-, e + 1 + \sigma)\). If \(t_o \leqslant 1\) this is at most \(e^g H^{Un + 2^n} p_o^-\). If \(t_o \geqslant 2\), then \(\sigma \text{cov}_B \hspace{0.17em} X_o \geqslant \sigma \lambda t_o \geqslant (Un + 2^n) t_o \log H\) for large \(s\), by (S), and \(w_o \leqslant e^g p_o^-\). In both cases \(w_o \leqslant e^g H^{Un + 2^n} p_o^-\), so the old site sum is at most \(e^g H^{Un + 2^n} \eta_-\).

Choice of \(g\). Put \(\delta = H^{- 1 / 2}\) and \(e^g = \delta / (2 H^{Un + 2^n} \eta_-)\). This is at least \(1\) for large \(s\), because \(\eta_-\) decays faster than every negative power of \(H\). The total site sum of the weights at exponent \(e + 1\) is then

\(\displaystyle \Gamma \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} \delta + 2^n C_{\ast} R_{e + 1} H^{- 7 / 8} \leqslant \delta\)

for large \(s\).

Components. Every nonzero surviving component contains an old forced piece. Charge each of its failed-mask pieces \(f\), of cell \(c\), to an old piece \(o\) of the same component that it meets. Then \(X_o\) meets \(Y_c\), so \(c\) is one of the \(t_o\) cells counted for \(o\). At most \(2^n t_o\) failed-mask pieces are charged to \(o\). If the component has \(s_f\) failed-mask pieces and \(r \geqslant 1\) old pieces, then \(s_f \leqslant 2^n \sum_o t_o\), and the product of the true bounds over the component is

\(\displaystyle \prod_f p_f \prod_o p_o = e^{Fs_f - 2^n F \sum_o t_o - gr} \prod_f w_f \prod_o w_o \leqslant e^{- g} \prod_f w_f \prod_o w_o,\)

since \(g \geqslant 0\). The distinct connected-set bound (7.35), with reserve one and counting factor \((2 B + 1)^4\), then bounds the new site bound at the exponent \(e\) by \(e^{- g} (e^{\Gamma} - 1) \leqslant 2 \delta e^{- g} = 4 H^{Un + 2^n} \eta_-\), provided \((2 B + 1)^4 \delta \leqslant 1\) and \(\delta \leqslant 1\). The first condition holds for large \(s\) as in Lemma 14.31, since \((2 B + 1)^4 \leqslant \exp \{4 \log 9 + 16 \log \log s\} \leqslant H^{1 / 2}\) once \(\log \log s \geqslant 36\).\(\Box\)

Proposition 14.43. (The launch receipt) Assume the conclusions of Subsection 14.18.5 on the leading interval, with remainder \(d_{k - \ell} \leqslant H^{\kappa - q}\) and forced bound \(G (x_{\ell})\) at the weight \(\omega_-\). Assume Lemma 14.35 at the middle level, the hypotheses of Lemma 14.37, those of Lemma 14.39, and that the halos of distinct cells are at distance at least \(D'\) at the middle level, with \(D' / ((2 B + 1) \log H) \to \infty\). Assume \(q \geqslant \kappa + Un + 2\). Then, for all large \(s\), the pushed invoice at the middle level has an exact representation at the weight \(\omega\), without leading letters, with regular marker-root bound at most \(H^{- 3 / 4}\) and forced site bound at most \(H^{- q}\).

Proof. By the commuting reference factor, the pushed invoice at \(\ell\) is the pushed insertion product times \(\exp_{\ast} (- D_R)\). The representation from the leading interval has three parts. Their bounds are as follows.

Remainder. Lemma 14.41 gives the bound \(H^{- 1}\) at the weight \(\omega\) for the entire nonleading regular remainder. Hypotheses (F) and (S) hold: the first clause of (F) is Lemma 14.37, with \(\eta_- = G (x_{\ell})\), its second clause is Lemma 14.39, and (S) is (14.125).

Leading family. Its pieces are the exact prescribed principal polynomial. Multiplying by \(\exp_{\ast} (- D_R)\) and splitting each corrected coefficient by the failed-mask identity gives healthy pieces \(\chi (W - R)\) and failed-mask pieces. Drop the leading designation. The healthy pieces become regular letters of incidence bound at most \(cH^{- 27 / 32}\), by Lemma 14.35.

Forced part. The forced background has site bound at most \(\eta_- = G (x_{\ell})\) at the weight \(\omega_-\) and exponent \(e + 1 + \sigma\), by (F). This is the realigned forced bound of the recurrence, not the compiler output \(G (x_{\ell})^2\) before realignment. By Lemma 14.42, after the failed-mask pieces are moved and regrouped, the forced site bound at the weight \(\omega\) is at most \(4 H^{Un + 2^n} G (x_{\ell})\).

Adding the regular contributions gives at most \(H^{- 1} + cH^{- 27 / 32}\), which is at most \(H^{- 3 / 4}\) once \(H^{3 / 32} \geqslant c + 1\). Since \(G\) decays faster than every negative power of \(H\) uniformly on \([s / 2, s]\), eventually \(4 H^{Un + 2^n} G (x_{\ell}) \leqslant H^{- q}\).\(\Box\)

14.18.9The step counts without a block-length floor

Lemma 14.44. (The lower step count is free) Let the clock's cover size at level \(k\) be \(c_k = \lceil (\log \hat{\beta}_k)^2 \rceil\), and let the root depth be \(w = \max \{j \leqslant \ell : a_j / a_{\ell} \geqslant c_k^{10} \}\). Since all block lengths satisfy \(L_j \geqslant 3\), for every fixed \(\gamma > 0\) and all sufficiently large \(s = \hat{\beta}_k\), uniformly in the chart, \((\alpha + 1)^{\ell - w} \leqslant H^{\gamma}\). In particular the lower step count (14.124) holds.

Proof. If \(w = \ell\) there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, by maximality of \(w\), \(a_{w + 1} / a_{\ell} < c_k^{10}\). By Subsection 19.2, \(a_{w + 1} / a_{\ell} = \prod_{t = w + 2}^{\ell} L_{J (t)} \geqslant 3^{\ell - w - 1}\). For \(\log s \geqslant 1\) we have \(c_k \leqslant 2 (\log s)^2\). Hence

\(\displaystyle (\ell - w - 1) \log 3 < 10 \log c_k \leqslant 10 \log 2 + 20 \log \log s.\)

Therefore

\(\displaystyle (\ell - w) \log (\alpha + 1) \leqslant \log (\alpha + 1) \left( 1 + \frac{10 \log 2 + 20 \log \log s}{\log 3} \right) = A_0 + A_1 \log \log s,\)

with constants \(A_0, A_1 \geqslant 0\) fixed by \(\alpha\). Since \(\gamma > 0\) and \(\log H = (\log \log s)^2\), the right side is at most \(\gamma \log H\) for all large \(s\). No block-length floor and no stage condition is used.\(\Box\)

Lemma 14.45. (The upper step count at a fixed power) Let the base arc satisfy \(b \leqslant \bar{b}\), and put

\(\displaystyle \kappa = 1 + \frac{(\bar{b} + 2) \log (\alpha + 2)}{\log 3} .\)

For all sufficiently large \(s\), uniformly in the chart, \((\alpha + 2)^{k - \ell} \leqslant H^{\kappa}\). Consequently the remainder bound of the recurrence at the middle level is \(d_{k - \ell} \leqslant H^{\kappa - q}\) at the weight \(\omega_-\). If \(q \geqslant \kappa + 4\), this is at most \(H^{- 4}\), which is the window required in Lemma 14.13.

Proof. If \(\ell = k\) there is nothing to prove. Otherwise \(a_{\ell + 1} / a_k < m \mu_k^2 = H^{b + 2}\) by maximality of \(\ell\). Also \(a_{\ell + 1} / a_k = \prod_{t = \ell + 2}^k L_{J (t)} \geqslant 3^{k - \ell - 1}\). Hence \((k - \ell - 1) \log 3 < (b + 2) \log H\), and

\(\displaystyle (k - \ell) \log (\alpha + 2) \leqslant \log (\alpha + 2) + \frac{(\bar{b} + 2) \log (\alpha + 2)}{\log 3} \log H \leqslant \kappa \log H\)

once \(\log H \geqslant \log (\alpha + 2)\). Then \(d_{k - \ell} = (\alpha + 2)^{k - \ell} H^{- q} \leqslant H^{\kappa - q}\).\(\Box\)

The parameters are therefore fixed in the order \(e\), \(\sigma\), then \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_{e + 1 + \sigma}\), \(\bar{b}\), then \(U\) (by Lemma 14.14), then

\(\displaystyle q \geqslant \max \{6, \kappa + 4\},\)

which covers Lemma 14.13. The launch also requires \(q \geqslant \kappa + Un + 2\), for Lemma 14.41. All are fixed before \(s\).

Lemma 14.46. (The forced inputs of the lower rungs) In Lemma 14.33, hypothesis 3 can be dropped. More precisely, take the incoming forced budget of each lower rung to be the forced output \(G (y)\) of the preceding lower rung, and at the transition the forced output of the launch, which is at most \(H^{- q}\) by Proposition 14.43. Replace the bounds \(z_t\) by \(\max (z_t, H^{- q})\). Then the conclusions of that lemma hold for all large \(s\).

Proof. The lower calculation of Subsection 14.4 uses its incoming forced budget \(\eta\) in exactly two places: the marker-root term \(T_e \eta\), and the site term \(\mathcal{W} (B_c) L^4 T_e \eta\). With \(\eta = z \omega_j \leqslant z\), it bounds them by \(T_e z\) and by \(K / 16\) for \(z \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\). Both bounds hold verbatim for any \(\eta \leqslant z\). Now \(G\) decays faster than every power of \(H\) uniformly on \([s / 2, s]\), so for large \(s\) every incoming forced budget of a lower rung is at most \(H^{- q} \leqslant \max (z_t, H^{- q})\); at the transition this is the launch bound. Replacing \(z_t\) by \(\max (z_t, H^{- q})\) keeps the induction of Lemma 14.33: if \(H^{- q} \leqslant z_t \leqslant H^{- 1 / 2}\), then \(\max (\alpha z_t + H^{1 / 2} z_t^2, H^{- q}) \leqslant (\alpha + 1) z_t\), since \(\alpha \geqslant 1\). The starting value is \(\max (z_0, H^{- q}) \leqslant H^{- 3 / 4}\). The forced outputs are at most \(G (y_j) \leqslant 1\) as before.\(\Box\)

Lemma 14.47. (The amplitude at the middle level) Under the hypotheses of Lemma 14.35 there is a constant \(C_{\ast}\), depending on the fixed word and the compact window, such that for all large \(s\) every actual leading piece at the middle level is bounded by \(C_{\ast}\) on its healthy region.

Proof. The reference moment polynomial \(1 + X_R\) varies continuously on the compact window, which consists of configurations with distinct points. So \(\|X_R \|_1 \leqslant M_{\mathcal{W}}\) for a constant \(M_{\mathcal{W}}\), and \(\|D_R \|_1 \leqslant \sum_{j = 1}^n M_{\mathcal{W}}^j / j\) by submultiplicativity. By Lemma 14.35, each actual leading piece is within \(H^{- 31 / 32} \leqslant 1\) of a coefficient of \(D_R\). Take \(C_{\ast} = 1 + \sum_{j = 1}^n M_{\mathcal{W}}^j / j\).\(\Box\)

The lemma bounds the amplitude at the middle level only. At the other leading rungs the discrepancy lemma uses the power bound of Corollary 14.16.

Status of the propagation to the output.
Proposition 14.34 is proved from the following inputs, all on the common history of the chart:

  1. the four inputs listed at the end of Subsection 14.18.5 for the leading interval: applicability of the upper calculation; the hypotheses of Subsection 14.17 at every leading rung and the terminal-source purchase; the common insertion and bounded transport (CI), which gives the identification of the prescribed polynomial by Lemma 14.21 (the per-rung amplitude follows by Corollary 14.16); the reader separation \(2 QB\), which holds for the trace chart by Lemma 14.40;

  2. the two \(H^{- 3 / 2}\) inputs of Lemma 14.35 at the middle level, which are proved in Sections 14.16 and 14.17 under their stated hypotheses (the reader covariance and vertex bounds, the exact low-grade calibration of Subsection 14.14, the mixed compiler's per-step row and covariance comparison, the terminal paid insertion bound and the common purchases);

  3. the inputs of item 1 at the exponent \(e_{\ast} = e + 1 + \sigma\), which is (E). By Lemma 14.37 this gives the forced clause of (F) and, by Lemma 14.41, the regular remainder at the restored weight. Also needed is the structural property used in Lemma 14.39, that coarse footprints contain the projections of fine supports (Subsection 8.2). It gives the second part of (F). Hypothesis (S) and the reader separation \(2 QB\) hold for the trace chart by Lemma 14.40;

  4. applicability of the lower calculation on \([w, \ell)\);

  5. the lawful representation at the output level \(w\), with the suppositions of Subsection 14.19.

The rung couplings lie in \([s / 2, s]\) on the whole window \([w, k]\) by the plateau field of the chart witness, which is proved from the clock in Lemma 17.14; the same lemma proves the bracket. The step counts, the forced inputs of the lower rungs and the leading budget at the small weight are proved above. The polynomial transport purchase of Subsection 14.17 is separate from the step counts and remains one of the common purchases.

14.18.10The selected marked polynomial on the common history

The preceding abstract hypotheses can now be checked for the selected family. In making this check it is important to retain the higher coefficients already produced above the opening level. Write \(q_N\) for its terminal marked density and \(q_k\) for its actual push to the opening level. In general \(q_k\) is not the product of the unmarked density and its singleton insertions.

Lemma 14.48. (The complete opening polynomial) Fix the finite grade menu, the compact distinct-point window, the finite census of signed copies, and the requested support exponent \(e_{\ast} = e + 1 + \sigma\). For any fixed \(q\), the selected calibrated family admits, on its actual restarted endpoint, a presentation of \(q_k\) with regular remainder at most \(H^{- q}\) and forced site bound at most \(G (s)\), at a weight \(\omega_- = H^{1 / (8 u) - U}\) with \(U\) fixed before the coupling threshold. Its leading letters are exactly the masked calibrated raw singletons of Proposition 16.25; all other connected coefficients belong to the remainder or the forced family. The statement holds simultaneously at all smaller support exponents and uniformly in the permitted periods and cutoffs.

Proof. Start with the complete served polynomial, rather than constructing a new product at \(k\). The finite-menu push identity gives the ordinary and forced families \(A_{\nu}, K_{\lambda}\) of the original mixed-grade expansion in Subsection 17.2. Its proof preceding (17.19) uses only the selected service, its finite-menu propagation and the planted recombination identity; it does not use the present output estimate. It gives a rooted budget one for the entire higher-mode family at the original radius \(x = s^{- z / 2}\). The census and label payments there are verified from the calibrated coefficient norm, including the signed copies.

Put \(f = 1 / (8 u) - U\). Increase \(U\) so that \(f \leqslant 0\) and the weighted singleton column bound is at most \(H^{- 8}\). The change of weights (17.18), with \(g = q + 2\) and support exponent \(e_{\ast}\), bounds the sum of all higher ordinary modes at each marked root by \(H^{- q - 10}\). It applies to the forced modes as well, without deleting their original inventories or eligibility tests. This purchase uses \(C \sim (\log s)^2\), \(B = C^2\) and the separated origins, not a comparison of \(H^f\) with the much smaller radius \(x\). Its support reserve pays the conversion of marked roots to spatial hits, with factor \(W (B) = H^{o (1)}\).

For each singleton use the exact split (16.68), leaving its wide tail in the remainder. The near Taylor error has weighted bound \(H^{f + E - D}\) and the wide tail has bound at most \(H^{f + E} \exp (- \theta C)\), with \(E\) the fixed solved-lift exponent. Take \(D > E + q + |f| + 3\). Both are at most \(H^{- q - 2}\) eventually, including their fixed halo bounds. The finitely many contributions and the higher-mode bound therefore sum to at most \(H^{- q}\). The near polynomial has precisely the prescribed axial reads and mask; changing an old mask to that mask puts their difference among the failed-mask terms. Their weighted bound is at most a constant times \(H^{- 8}\), by increasing \(U\) once more if necessary.

This last bound is not by itself bounded by \(G (s)\). To obtain the forced bound, perform the endpoint expansion of Proposition 13.38 jointly with these marked letters. Retain its site factors \(X_z\), its optional factors \(W_Y\), and the old forced collection before summing connected components. Every failed-mask term meets a new flag. Every old forced letter, including a higher marked mode, contains an old flag and hence a new flag. Join intersecting enlarged supports, including the radius-\(4 B\) support of every selected \(X_z\). Thus each forced component contains an \(X_z\). Reserve its factor \(e^{- u} = G (s)\) once per component. Record the full marker mode and both old and new inventories; preserve all old compatibility and eligibility tests inside the component. Decomposition into components is reversible coefficient by coefficient in the finite squarefree algebra, just as in (13.46). In particular no higher coefficient of \(q_k\) has been discarded.

After reserving this factor the remaining weighted rooted sum is bounded by

\(\displaystyle r_s = 2 H^{- 3} + t_s + m_s + b_s,\)

where the four explicit endpoint bounds in the paragraph following (17.18) are taken at \(g = q + 2\) and \(e_{\ast}\). The singleton forced part uses its original \(\sqrt{\exp (- H^{1 / 5})}\) allowance; the higher forced part uses the proved change of weights. The new failed masks contribute to \(t_s\), not a separate unweighted forced gas. Those estimates give \(r_s \leqslant H^{- 1}\) and \(Z (B) r_s \leqslant 1\) on one tail. The rooted connected sum is consequently bounded by

\(\displaystyle G (s) (e^{r_s} - 1) \leqslant G (s) .\)

This argument also proves absolute convergence when original local labels are countable. All sums used to group equal supports, modes and inventories are dominated by these positive majorants. The exact unmarked coefficient and its scalar normalization are unchanged.\(\Box\)

Proposition 14.49. (Application to the selected chart) For the selected funded family, with the common chart purchase of Proposition 17.10, the calibrated readout of Proposition 16.25, and the clock of Lemma 17.14, all hypotheses of Proposition 14.34 hold on a tail of its purchased stages. They hold for the complete pushed marked polynomial \(q_k\) and the invoice formed from its prescribed Gaussian moments. The output coefficient bound and root test of that proposition therefore apply on this same family and with its original partition denominator.

Proof. Fix the word and window first. Choose \(e, \sigma\), the raw compiler reserve of Proposition 14.38, and \(\alpha \geqslant 8 T_{e + 2}\). Choose \(U\) to pay both Lemma 14.14 and Lemma 14.48; then choose \(q \geqslant \max (6, \kappa + 4, \kappa + Un + 2)\) and the Taylor order \(D\) in that lemma. These requests precede the stage orders, block sides and entry delays. In this order use the degree-independent exponent \(A = n (nz + 1) + 2\) of Lemma 14.15, with \(z\) the solved-lift coefficient exponent, which depends only on the fixed grade menu. After \(D\) and the smoothing orders, the row bound (14.78) and the normalized covariance bound of Subsection 14.13 give a common constant \(C\) before the block side. Thus the polynomial norm \(A_{nD} = [2 (nD + 2) C]^{nD}\) is fixed before imposing \(2 (\bar{b} + 2) \log A_{nD} \leqslant \log L\) on both adjacent sides. The endpoint support suppression can be requested at any fixed \(q\), so this ordering does not require changing \(U\) after \(q\).

Proposition 13.40 constructs the actual restarted course with the original paths, clips and fine-to-coarse maps. On each step its corrected alphabet has the body, deleted-support and forced identities at the raw reserved exponent. Proposition 14.38 therefore supplies the upper calculation at \(e_{\ast}\) and the lower one at \(e\), for this same alphabet. The original forced family is kept before aggregation at each forced address. This is exactly the family required by the planted expansion; an arbitrary presentation of the same aggregate would not suffice.

At a leading step use the one joint Gaussian reader of Subsection 14.9. Its sample and loading are those of the corrected principal integral. For a product, use the union of its read supports and keep every factor's guard on this same sample. Lemma 14.30 applies at a stage boundary with the minimum of the two strip widths and the separate metric floors. The mean is the actual installed transport row; its covariance is the short covariance of this same step. The polynomial comparison is defined by Wick contraction with these data. To identify the principal component explicitly, observe that an entirely leading component has no body letter and compatibility excludes background packets. Its integrand is a product of guarded principal values on the common Haar–Gaussian sample space. On a positive guard, the field is healthy on the full prescribed mask support, and that value is \(p_I (\xi_{t, I})\). Gauge invariance removes the patch gauge from each physical value. Whole-mode selection sums these products over partitions, with the same nonnegative forest/tag weights and loading for every product, including the empty product, whose integral is one. These are exactly the moments \(M_S\) of Lemma 14.28; Wick contraction with the same forecast and short covariance gives its prescribed moments \(M_S^{\ast}\). This identifies the principal component, without identifying the whole interacting density as Gaussian or replacing its loading by a second partition function. Taking the finite logarithm therefore gives the discrepancy to which that lemma applies. Corollary 14.29 pays their discrepancy at the requested power of \(H\).

The mean-row and covariance comparisons in Subsection 14.13 use these very rows and covariances. Their atlas is determined by the selected horizon, and Proposition 16.25 supplies its common calibrated axes. Hence its exact low-grade equalities are those of Subsection 14.14. Apply the polynomial transport comparison and the closed-cochain construction of Subsection 14.17 with these axes. The transport norm is bounded by Lemma 14.15; the per-step error is purchased before the final threshold to dominate that fixed power and the step count. The two middle errors are therefore the \(H^{- 3 / 2}\) errors in Lemma 14.35, for one prescribed moment element. The finite logarithm estimate of Subsection 14.16 then gives the stated \(H^{- 31 / 32}\) healthy comparison.

The opening lemma starts the upper recurrence at \(H^{- q}, G (s)\), including the original higher modes. Lemma 14.45 keeps it inside its domain. Run it also at \(e_{\ast}\); reader containment follows from Lemma 14.39, and the actual chart separation from Lemma 14.40. The spare-run and remainder-restoration lemmas thus apply to the same coefficients. Proposition 14.43 raises the weight once, at the middle level, to \(H^{1 / (8 u)}\). The lower step count and forced-input lemmas then give the lower propagation through \(w\), including a possible adjacent-stage transition.

Finally the all-level lawful family of Corollary 16.29 is obtained by the same unmarked endpoint rewrite and exact transitions. Its local lawfulness and root-normalization proof do not use the marked coefficient bound being established here. Apply Proposition 14.34 to that representation. Every polynomial operation above was applied to \(q_k\). More precisely, let \(D^{\text{ref}}_c\) be the prescribed reference cumulant polynomial in cell \(c\), and put \(J = \exp_{\ast} (- \sum_c D^{\text{ref}}_c)\). The factor to multiply into \(q_k\) is \(J\), not the full invoice, which already contains the insertions. At the terminal level the replicated invoice density is \(Jq_N\) by Lemma 14.32. Its push to \(k\) is \(Jq_k\): \(J\) is independent of the gauge field, and each transition acts linearly on each coefficient in the finite marker algebra. The same identity holds down to \(w\). Since the empty coefficient of \(J\) is one, the empty density remains the original unmarked density. This proves both the asserted identification and the normalization.\(\Box\)

14.19The volume cost of the rank tests

The replicated rank tests require the correct power of the coupling in both the numerator and the partition function. We prove the finite-torus estimate with that power. Write \(D = \dim_{\mathbb{R}} \mathfrak{g}\), \(\mu_G\) for probability Haar measure, and \(\chi (u)\) for the normalized chord of \(u \in G\) used in the action. Thus \(\chi (1) = 0\), it is unchanged by inversion and conjugation, and \(\chi (uv) \leqslant \chi (u) + \chi (v)\).

14.19.1Haar bounds in the physical dimension

There are constants \(0 < c_- \leqslant 1 \leqslant c_+ < \infty\), depending only on \(G\), such that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \mu_G \{\chi (u) \leqslant r / 4\} & \geqslant c_- r^D & & (0 < r \leqslant 1), \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.126)} \\ \int_G e^{- \chi (u) / t} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_G (u) & \leqslant c_+ t^D & & (t > 0) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.127)} \end{array}\)

To see this, use the actual Haar chart of Subsection 8.18. Its density is bounded above and below by positive constants near zero. Differentiability of the represented exponential, together with the normalization of the colour norm, gives, after shrinking the chart,

\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} \|z\| \leqslant \chi (\exp z) \leqslant 2 \|z\| .\)

A sufficiently small chord ball is contained in that chart: the faithful matrix embedding induces the topology of the compact group. Comparison with Euclidean balls of radii \(r / 2\) and \(3 r\) therefore gives \(ar^D \leqslant \mu_G \{\chi \leqslant r\} \leqslant br^D\) for \(0 < r \leqslant r_0\). For \(r \in [r_0, 1]\) the lower bound extends by monotonicity after decreasing \(a\). The upper bound extends to all \(r > 0\) by the total mass bound one, after increasing \(b\).

For \(k \geqslant 0\), on \(kt \leqslant \chi (u) < (k + 1) t\) the integrand in (14.127) is at most \(e^{- k}\). Summing over these measurable shells bounds the integral by

\(\displaystyle bt^D \sum_{k = 0}^{\infty} e^{- k} (k + 1)^D .\)

The series converges, since its successive-term ratio tends to \(e^{- 1}\). This proves the upper bound; rescaling the lower bound by \(1 / 4\) proves (14.126). The proof also covers \(D = 0\): powers of positive radii are then one. No connectedness of \(G\) is needed.

14.19.2An axial tree and the four unintegrated links

Let \(\Lambda = (\mathbb{Z}/ L\mathbb{Z})^4\), \(L > 1\), and \(V = | \Lambda |\). Represent each coordinate by \(0, \ldots, L - 1\). For \(x \ne 0\) let \(p (x)\) be its largest nonzero coordinate index. Join \(x\) to \(x - e_{p (x)}\).
