The sum of the coordinate representatives strictly decreases on every such edge, so these edges form a tree rooted at zero. The tree has \(V - 1\) positive links; there are \(4 V\) positive links altogether. Thus there are \(3 V + 1\) free links after fixing the tree links to one.

For completeness, tree gauge fixing preserves the integral of a gauge-invariant integrable function. Integrate successively from the leaves of the tree. At a leaf, use its tree link as the gauge variable at that vertex and multiply all its other incident links by that variable or its inverse. Conditional on the other links, these changes preserve their Haar measures. The tree link becomes one, and the remaining gauge variable integrates to one. Induction removes all \(V - 1\) tree variables. This proves the identity with product probability Haar measure on the free links, including for a disconnected group.

Keep also the four links \((- e_i, i)\), one wrap link in each direction. They are free, distinct, and are not in the tree. We now associate a different plaquette to each of the other \(3 V - 3\) free links so that their integrals can be performed successively. Here is an explicit ordering, which also deals with wrap links. Set \(p (0) = 0\), and for a positive link \(e = (x, i)\) put

\(\displaystyle t (e) = \max (i, p (x)), \qquad \pi (e) = \left( t (e), \hspace{0.17em} \boldsymbol{1}_{\{i = t (e)\}}, \hspace{0.17em} \left. \sum_j x_j \right), \right.\)

ordered lexicographically.

If \(i < t (e) = t\), choose the plaquette based at \(x - e_t\) in directions \((i, t)\). Its \(i\)-link at \(x\) is \(e\). Its two \(t\)-links are tree links, because \(x_t \ne 0\) and every coordinate above \(t\) vanishes. Its remaining \(i\)-link has smaller tier or the same tier and smaller coordinate sum. It therefore has smaller priority whenever it is free.

If \(i = t (e)\) and \(e\) is free, then \(x_i = L - 1\) and all coordinates above \(i\) vanish: otherwise it is a tree link. Put \(w = x + e_i\). Since \(e\) is not the retained wrap link, \(w \ne 0\). Let \(j = p (w) < i\) and choose the plaquette based at \(x - e_j\) in directions \((j, i)\). Its \(i\)-link at \(x\) is \(e\). The \(j\)-link based at \(w - e_j\) is a tree link. The other \(j\)-link has smaller tier or has the same tier with second priority coordinate zero. The remaining \(i\)-link has the same tier and second coordinate but smaller coordinate sum, since \(x_j \ne 0\). Thus again every other free link on this plaquette has smaller priority.

The chosen plaquettes are distinct. If two links shared a chosen plaquette, each would have smaller priority than the other. Moreover, a maximal-priority remaining link occurs in no plaquette chosen for another remaining link. With all other links fixed, its own plaquette holonomy has the form \(aub\) or \(au^{- 1} b\), so its distribution is Haar. Fubini and descending induction therefore show that for continuous \(F : G \to [0, 1]\),

\(\displaystyle \int \prod_{p \text{ plaquette}} F (U (\partial p)) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu (U) \leqslant \left( \int_G F \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_G \right)^{3 V - 3} .\) (14.128)

Indeed discard all unchosen factors, which are at most one, and then integrate the chosen factors in that order. The four edges of a plaquette are distinct for \(L > 1\), including \(L = 2\); thus each integration uses its selected variable exactly once, possibly inverted.

14.19.3The normalized estimate

Consider a lawful representation \(R\) on this torus, with gauge-invariant path action and activities, and with nonnegative density, coupling \(\beta\), threshold \(\theta \leqslant 1\), scalar exponent \(\sigma\), and clip constants satisfying \(0 < c_{\text{lin}} < c_{\text{dir}} / 16\). The path and activity bounds below are the bounds of that same representation. These assumptions ensure that its density and the action bounds used in tree gauge fixing are gauge invariant. Put

\(\displaystyle g = \beta^{- 1 / 2}, \quad 0 < g \leqslant \theta, \quad c = \min (1, c_{\text{lin}}), \quad b (g) = N_{\mathcal{A}} (c_{\text{dir}} g^2 / 2 + (Ag)^2 / 2) .\)

Here \(A\) bounds the linear curvature rows, and \(N_{\mathcal{A}}\) is the number of positive plaquette types. Let \(r\) be the per-site bound for the additive remainder and the cubic, quartic and loop terms. Those bounds, with their actual support weights, give

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \text{exponent}_R (U) & \geqslant \sigma - V \{r + \beta b (g)\} & & \text{if every link has chord at most } g / 4, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.129)} \\ \text{exponent}_R (U) & \leqslant \sigma + V (r + N_{\mathcal{A}}) - \sum_p \frac{\chi (U (\partial p))}{g / c} . & & \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.130)} \end{array}\)

Here is the local verification of these two uses of the path bounds. On the small-link set, every plaquette has chord at most \(g\), all flags vanish, and each linear curvature row has norm at most \(Ag\) by its absolute row bound. Summing its squared contribution and the direct plaquette contribution gives \(Vb (g)\). The other terms have absolute sum at most \(Vr\). For the upper estimate put \(a = \chi (U (\partial p))\). The clipped direct term obeys

\(\displaystyle ca / g - 1 \leqslant \beta \hspace{0.17em} \text{clipped} (a) .\)

In the quadratic region this follows from \(c (a / g - 1)^2 \geqslant 0\), \(c \leqslant 1\), and \(c \leqslant c_{\text{dir}}\). In the linear region use \(c \leqslant c_{\text{lin}}\), \(g \leqslant \theta\) and \(\beta g^2 = 1\). The other quadratic row term is nonnegative. The sum over plaquettes is unchanged by signed motions; hence averaging over them preserves this lower bound on the path action. Subtracting the remaining action and additive terms at their absolute bound gives (14.130).

Gauge-fix the axial tree and restrict its \(3 V + 1\) free links to \(\{\chi \leqslant g / 4\}\). The tree links are one, so (14.129) applies. All flags vanish there, and the forced gas equals one. Nonnegativity on the complement and (14.126) yield the strictly positive bound

\(\displaystyle Z_R := \int R \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \geqslant e^{\sigma - V (r + \beta b (g))} (c_- g^D)^{3 V + 1} .\) (14.131)

Now let \(f\) be an integrable complex function, \(z \geqslant 0\), and suppose

\(\displaystyle z |f (U) | \leqslant e^{\text{exponent}_R (U) + VX} .\)

Using (14.130), then (14.128) and (14.127), gives

\(\displaystyle z \left| \int f \hspace{0.17em} d \mu \right| \leqslant e^{\sigma + V (r + N_{\mathcal{A}} + X)} \{c_+ (g / c)^D \}^{3 V - 3} .\)

Define \(h = \log c_+ - D \log c \geqslant 0\) and

\(\displaystyle P_R (X) = 2 r + N_{\mathcal{A}} + \beta b (g) + X + 3 h - 4 \log c_- .\)

Division by (14.131) gives

\(\displaystyle \frac{z | \int f \hspace{0.17em} d \mu |}{Z_R} \leqslant \exp \{VP_R (X) - 4 D \log g\} .\) (14.132)

To check the constants, the powers of \(g^D\) differ by exactly four. Also \((3 V - 3) h \leqslant 3 Vh\) and \(- (3 V + 1) \log c_- \leqslant - 4 V \log c_-\), since \(V \geqslant 1\) and \(\log c_- \leqslant 0\). These are the only enlargements in this division.

The same estimate holds for the auxiliary history of Subsection 13.12. If its additive terms have value bound \(\delta\) per site, delete them temporarily to obtain \(R^0\). Then \(e^{- V \delta} R^0 \leqslant R \leqslant e^{V \delta} R^0\) because \(R \geqslant 0\), and \(R^0 = e^E R \geqslant 0\). Apply the preceding estimate to \(R^0\) with numerator expense \(X + \delta\), and use \(Z_{R^0} \leqslant e^{V \delta} Z_R\). The extra numerator and denominator expenses total \(2 V \delta\). This is exactly the contribution of \(\delta\) to \(2 r\) in \(P_R\). Derivative estimates on those auxiliary additive terms are not needed.

14.19.4Taking the replica root

Let a terminal tiling have \(q\) cells, and push its full marked density to a torus with exactly \(q\) sites along the auxiliary history. The unnormalized scalar integral remains the original partition function \(Z\). Suppose each coefficient of the pushed marked density has the local estimate needed above with coefficient bound \(a^{q |I|}\) and per-site expense \(X \leqslant 1 + M\), where \(M\) is the maximum number of markers per cell and \(I \ne \varnothing\) is the chosen marker subset. The signed orientation copies use conjugation on the reversed cells. Thus the coefficient carrying \(I\) in every cell is exactly \(Z\) times the replicated Wilson expectation. Equation (14.132) gives

\(\displaystyle a^{q |I|} |\mathbb{E}(\text{replicated coefficient}) | \leqslant e^{qP_R (X) - 4 D \log g} .\)

Taking the positive \(q\)th root proves

\(\displaystyle \| \text{coefficient } I\|_{\text{root}} \leqslant \frac{\exp \{P_R (X) - 4 D \log g / q\}}{a^{|I|}} .\) (14.133)

This retains the finite-volume harmonic correction \(- 4 D \log g / q\).

The clock buys this last expense uniformly in the period. At an output level \(m\), \(\beta \leqslant 4 \hat{\beta}_m\), so \(- 4 D \log g = 2 D \log \beta \leqslant 8 D \hat{\beta}_m\). The clock coupling grows linearly in its logarithmic spatial scale, whereas the minimal torus site count grows at least exponentially in that scale. Consequently \(8 D \hat{\beta}_m / q \leqslant 1\) eventually, even at the smallest period. For the remaining terms, use the same clip and row bound \(A\) as the clock stage, and suppose

\(\displaystyle \theta_R \leqslant \theta_{\text{clock}}, \qquad \Lambda \leqslant \Lambda_{\text{clock}}, \qquad \delta \leqslant 1.\)

The late-scale purchases are

\(\displaystyle \hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}}^3 \leqslant 1, \qquad \hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}}^4 \leqslant 1, \qquad \Lambda_{\text{clock}} \theta_{\text{clock}}^2 \leqslant 1.\)

Consequently the actual remainder bound satisfies

\(\displaystyle r = \delta + \beta (A \theta_R^3 + A \theta_R^4) + \Lambda \theta_R^2 \leqslant 1 + 8 A + 1, \qquad \beta b (g) = \frac{N_{\mathcal{A}}}{2} (c_{\text{dir}} + A^2) .\)

With \(X \leqslant 1 + M\), a fixed stage ticket bounding \(P_R (X) + 1\) is therefore

\(\displaystyle T_M = 2 (1 + 8 A + 1) + N_{\mathcal{A}} + \frac{N_{\mathcal{A}}}{2} (c_{\text{dir}} + A^2) + (1 + M) + 3 h - 4 \log c_- + 1.\)

The final \(1\) pays for the harmonic correction, rather than enlarging an already spent ticket. Choose the comparison depth \(k\) on a constant-profile interval and purchase

\(\displaystyle T_M \leqslant I_k = \frac{\log \mathcal{M}_k}{16 M},\)

where \(\mathcal{M}_k\) is the diverging clock monitor. This is a post-block-length scale delay. Set \(a = e^{2 I_k}\). Since \(|I| \geqslant 1\), (14.133) is at most \(e^{I_k} / a = e^{- I_k} \to 0\). Equivalently \(a =\mathcal{M}_k^{1 / (8 M)} \geqslant 1\). This is the constant marker weight (14.116) of Subsection 14.18, with \(u = M\) and monitor \(\mathcal{M}_k\). That weight is used from the launch at the middle level down to the output torus, so the coefficient bound \(a^{q |I|}\) above is the weight of the replicated coefficient in the norm in which the marked density is estimated there. The leading interval above the middle level is run at a smaller weight, and the single change of weight at the launch is paid in that section. For the rank detectors \(M = 2\). These estimates apply to the one-point and corrected two-point coefficients themselves. The local marked construction must supply their displayed coefficient bound on the same auxiliary density; no estimate for a different denominator is used here.

14.19.5The ticket and the harmonic premise at a chart's output level

The purchase \(T_M \leqslant I_k\) above was stated for a comparison depth on a constant-profile interval. We now prove it, together with the premise

\(\displaystyle P_R (X) - 4 D \log g / q \leqslant T_M,\) (14.134)

at the output level of a chart of level \(k\). That level need not lie on the same constant-profile interval as \(k\). Here \(q\) is the cell count of the terminal tiling, as above.

Recall the selected stage clock \(J (m)\) and stage profiles \(\pi_j\) of Subsection 19.5. At level \(m\) the clip constants, the row bound \(A\) and the path constants of the clock stage are those of \(\pi_{J (m)}\). The ticket \(T_M\) displayed above is computed from these constants, from \(N_{\mathcal{A}}\), and from the Haar data \(h, c_-\) of \(G\). Write \(T_M (\pi)\) for the value computed from a profile \(\pi\); it is a real number fixed once \(\pi\) and \(M\) are fixed. The clock monitor is \(\mathcal{M}_k = \exp ((\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)^2)\), as in Subsection 18.1, so

\(\displaystyle I_k = \frac{\log \mathcal{M}_k}{16 M} = \frac{(\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)^2}{16 M} .\)

Proposition 14.50. (Ticket and premise at the output level) Include in the countable family of numerical requests of (19.8) the requests

\(\displaystyle F_{M, i} = 1 + \sqrt{16 M \hspace{0.17em} \max \{T_M (\pi_i), 0\}} \qquad (M \geqslant 1, i \geqslant 0) .\) (14.135)

Let \(w (k) \leqslant k\) be levels with \(w (k) \to \infty\). Then for every \(M \geqslant 1\) there is \(K_M\) such that for all \(k \geqslant K_M\):

  1. the ticket at the profile of the output level is bought: \(T_M (\pi_{J (w (k))}) \leqslant I_k\);

  2. (14.134) holds at the output level \(m = w (k)\), with \(T_M = T_M (\pi_{J (w (k))})\). It holds for every period, every lawful representation \(R\) on the torus of level \(w (k)\) satisfying the suppositions \(\theta_R \leqslant \theta_{\text{clock}}\), \(\Lambda \leqslant \Lambda_{\text{clock}}\), \(\delta \leqslant 1\) and \(\beta \leqslant 4 \hat{\beta}_{w (k)}\) of the preceding subsection with the clip and row bound of \(\pi_{J (w (k))}\), and every expense \(X \leqslant 1 + M\).

Consequently, for every coefficient of the pushed marked density at the output level with the coefficient bound \(a^{q |I|}\), \(a = e^{2 I_k}\), and expense \(X \leqslant 1 + M\), the right side of (14.133) is at most \(e^{- I_k} =\mathcal{M}_k^{- 1 / (16 M)}\) for \(k \geqslant K_M\).

Proof. Ticket. Requests added to the countable family before the sequence is constructed are paid in the sense of (19.8): for fixed \(M\) there is \(m_M\) such that for every \(m \geqslant m_M\) and every \(i \leqslant J (m + 1)\),

\(\displaystyle F_{M, i} \leqslant 1 + \log \log \hat{\beta}_m .\)

The conclusion of that payment uses only a lower bound on the entry thresholds actually paid. Enlarging the family therefore preserves the earlier purchases of Subsection 19.2. Take \(K'_M = m_M\) and \(k \geqslant K'_M\). Since \(J\) is nondecreasing and \(w (k) \leqslant k\), the index \(i = J (w (k))\) satisfies \(i \leqslant J (k) \leqslant J (k + 1)\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \sqrt{16 M \max \{T_M (\pi_i), 0\}} \leqslant \log \log \hat{\beta}_k .\)

The left side is nonnegative, so squaring preserves the inequality:

\(\displaystyle 16 M \hspace{0.17em} T_M (\pi_i) \leqslant 16 M \max \{T_M (\pi_i), 0\} \leqslant (\log \log \hat{\beta}_k)^2 .\)

Dividing by \(16 M > 0\) gives \(T_M (\pi_i) \leqslant I_k\).

Premise. The preceding subsection proves (14.134) at an output level \(m\) for every representation satisfying the stated suppositions with the stage's clip and row bound. It uses three facts: \(\beta \leqslant 4 \hat{\beta}_m\), the harmonic bound \(8 D \hat{\beta}_m / q \leqslant 1\), and the late-scale purchases \(\hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}}^3 \leqslant 1\), \(\hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}}^4 \leqslant 1\) and \(\Lambda_{\text{clock}} \theta_{\text{clock}}^2 \leqslant 1\). Together they give \(r \leqslant 1 + 8 A + 1\), hence \(P_R (X) + 1 \leqslant T_M\) for \(X \leqslant 1 + M\), and the final \(1\) of \(T_M\) pays \(- 4 D \log g / q \leqslant 8 D \hat{\beta}_m / q \leqslant 1\). The harmonic bound and the late-scale purchases hold for all \(m\) beyond a threshold \(m'\) that is uniform in the period, because the minimal site count grows at least exponentially in the logarithmic scale, as shown there. The threshold \(m'\) does not depend on \(M\), since \(M\) enters only through \(X \leqslant 1 + M\) and the term \(1 + M\) of \(T_M\). Since \(w (k) \to \infty\), there is \(K''\) with \(w (k) \geqslant m'\) for \(k \geqslant K''\).

Put \(K_M = \max (K'_M, K'')\). For \(k \geqslant K_M\) both items hold. The consequence follows from (14.133) and the two items: its right side is at most \(\exp \{T_M \} / a^{|I|} \leqslant e^{I_k} / a = e^{- I_k}\), because \(|I| \geqslant 1\) and \(a \geqslant 1\).\(\Box\)

For a funded chart at level \(k\) with base arc \(b\), the output level \(w (k)\) is its root depth. Every level \(j\) with \(w (k) \leqslant j \leqslant k\) lies in the current or the previous clock stage: \(J (k) - 1 \leqslant J (j)\). In particular \(J (w (k)) \geqslant J (k) - 1 \to \infty\), and since \(J\) is nondecreasing this forces \(w (k) \to \infty\). So the proposition applies at the root depth, with \(M\) the marker allowance of the chart. This bracket property is a field of the chart witness, and it is proved from the clock, for every sufficiently large level and uniformly in charts with bounded base arc, in Lemma 17.14. The lawful-representation suppositions at the output level and the coefficient bound are supplied by the output transport of the marked density, not here.

14.19.6The clock threshold and loop capacity

The suppositions above use a clock threshold \(\theta_{\text{clock}}\) and a clock loop capacity \(\Lambda_{\text{clock}}\) at the output level \(m\). They are the following functions of the clock of Subsection 19.2:

\(\displaystyle \theta_{\text{clock}} (m) = \hat{\beta}_m^{- 1 / 2} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{M}_m, \qquad \Lambda_{\text{clock}} (m) = 2 \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{M}_m^{\vartheta_{J (m)}},\)

where \(\mathcal{M}_m = \exp ((\log \log \hat{\beta}_m)^2)\) and \(\vartheta_j = \theta_j\) is the protection exponent of stage \(j\) selected in Subsection 19.2. It is positive, nonincreasing, and fixed after the block lengths, in the order of choices made there. The proof below uses only \(\vartheta_j \geqslant 0\).

Lemma 14.51. (The late-scale purchases) Include the thresholds \(H_j\) below for each stage \(j\) in the all-stage payment (19.9). Then at every level \(m\) with \(J (m) > j_{\ast}\),

\(\displaystyle \hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}} (m)^3 \leqslant 1, \qquad \hat{\beta}_m \theta_{\text{clock}} (m)^4 \leqslant 1, \qquad \Lambda_{\text{clock}} (m) \theta_{\text{clock}} (m)^2 \leqslant 1.\)

Proof. Write \(x = \hat{\beta}_m\) and \(u = \log \log x\). The three quantities are

\(\displaystyle x^{- 1 / 2} e^{3 u^2}, \qquad x^{- 1} e^{4 u^2} \quad \text{and} \quad 2 x^{- 1} e^{(\vartheta_{J (m)} + 2) u^2} .\)

Each is at most one as soon as

\(\displaystyle (\vartheta_{J (m)} + 4) u^2 + \log 2 \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} \log x = \tfrac{1}{2} e^u .\)

For fixed \(j\) this holds for all \(x\) beyond a threshold \(H'_j\), because \(u^2 e^{- u} \to 0\). Put \(H_j = \max (H'_j, H'_{j - 1})\). The all-stage payment gives \(H_{J (m + 1)} \leqslant \hat{\beta}_m\). Since \(J (m) \leqslant J (m + 1) \leqslant J (m) + 1\), this covers the stage \(J (m)\) of level \(m\).\(\Box\)

14.20Three comparison scales and weak convergence

The comparison estimate is initially available at grid points whose coordinate projections are distinct. A test function may cross the excluded hyperplanes. We use three scales to pass from the former estimate to the latter test: one for the numerical comparison, one for a bound everywhere on a compact noncoincident set, and one for a derivative bound away from thin strips. Their different costs are essential to the argument.

14.20.1The geometric and analytic estimates

Fix an arity \(m \geqslant 1\) and use the Euclidean norm on \(E_4^m = (\mathbb{R}^4)^m\), retaining the maximum norm for pair separation. For \(R > 0\) set

\(\displaystyle K_R = \{x : \|x\|_2 \leqslant R, \quad \|x_i - x_j \|_{\infty} \geqslant R^{- 1} (i \ne j)\} .\)

This is a compact subset of the noncoincident configuration space. Every compact subset of that space is contained in some \(K_R\). Define

\(\displaystyle \Omega_{\sigma} = \{x : |x_{i, \nu} - x_{j, \nu} | > 8 \sigma \text{ for every } i \ne j, 1 \leqslant \nu \leqslant 4\} .\)

Thus the complement of \(\Omega_{\sigma}\) is a union of coordinate strips, not a neighborhood of just the actual coincidence set.

Let \(S\) be the smooth interacting table already extracted along the ordered mesh and volume filter \(\mathcal{F}\), and let \(Q (x)\) be the free correlation for a fixed word of labels. Fix a comparison index \(k\). Its physical radius is \(r_k > 0\); its normalized grid spacing is \(h_k > 0\); its strip width is \(\sigma_k > 0\). The calibrated insertion maps may still depend on the finite cutoff pair. Suppose they are eventually bounded in their finite-dimensional operator space. On any convergent subfilter of these maps write \(Y\) for the limiting tuple and

\(\displaystyle U_{k, Y} (x) = S (r_k x ; Y) .\)

All the following estimates are to hold for every such \(Y\), with constants independent of that subfilter. The three required estimates are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} |U_{k, Y} (y) - Q (y) | & \leqslant e_k & & \text{at generic grid points } y \in K_{2 R}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.136)} \\ |U_{k, Y} (x) | & \leqslant A_k^m & & (x \in K_R), \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.137)} \\ \|DU_{k, Y} (x)\| & \leqslant 4 mB_k^m / \sigma_k & & (|x_{i, \nu} - x_{j, \nu} | > 4 \sigma_k) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.138)} \end{array}\)

The last estimate is needed only in the noncoincident domain. The quantities \(e_k, A_k, B_k\) are nonnegative. Assume

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} e_k \longrightarrow 0, \qquad \sigma_k \longrightarrow 0, \qquad 4 mh_k \leqslant \sigma_k \quad \text{eventually}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.139)} \\ A_k^m \sigma_k \longrightarrow 0, \qquad B_k^m h_k / \sigma_k \longrightarrow 0. \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.140)} \end{array}\)

We first prove the weak comparison from these estimates, then explain the three scale choices that give exactly these powers.

Choose the coordinatewise lattice address \(y\) of \(x\), so \(\|y - x\| \leqslant 4 mh_k\). If \(x \in \Omega_{\sigma_k}\), the closed ball of radius \(\sigma_k\) about \(x\) has every coordinate difference larger than \(4 \sigma_k\): moving its two endpoints changes a difference by at most \(2 \sigma_k\). In particular the segment from \(x\) to \(y\) remains in the region of (14.138). The fundamental theorem of calculus on that segment gives

\(\displaystyle |U_{k, Y} (x) - U_{k, Y} (y) | \leqslant 16 m^2 B_k^m h_k / \sigma_k .\)

For all sufficiently large \(k\), this \(y\) belongs to \(K_{2 R}\) whenever \(x \in K_R\). Indeed the compact set \(K_R\) lies in the interior of \(K_{2 R}\), and \(\|y - x\| \leqslant \sigma_k \to 0\). It is also generic by the strict coordinate separation just proved. The free function \(Q\) is uniformly continuous on \(K_{2 R}\). If \(\omega_Q (t)\) is its modulus of continuity there, the three-term triangle inequality gives

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x \in K_R \cap \Omega_{\sigma_k}} |U_{k, Y} (x) - Q (x) | \leqslant \alpha_k := 16 m^2 B_k^m h_k / \sigma_k + e_k + \omega_Q (\sigma_k) \longrightarrow 0.\) (14.141)

This convergence is uniform over the limiting tuples \(Y\) under consideration.

There is an explicit linear bound for the volume of the omitted strips:

\(\displaystyle |K_R \setminus \Omega_{\sigma} | \leqslant C_{m, R} \sigma, \qquad C_{m, R} = 64 m (m - 1) (2 R)^{4 m - 1} .\) (14.142)

For a fixed ordered pair \(i \ne j\) and coordinate \(\nu\), make the linear change replacing \(x_{j, \nu}\) by \(x_{j, \nu} - x_{i, \nu}\) and leaving the other coordinates unchanged. Its determinant is one. The restricted coordinate has interval length \(16 \sigma\) and each of the remaining \(4 m - 1\) coordinates lies in an interval of length \(2 R\). This bounds the volume of that strip intersected with \(K_R\) by \(16 \sigma (2 R)^{4 m - 1}\). There are \(4 m (m - 1)\) choices. Subadditivity proves (14.142), including intersecting strips. For \(m = 1\) the union is empty and the constant is zero.

Let \(f\) be a smooth test supported in \(K_R\), and put \(C_Q = \sup_{K_R} |Q| < \infty\). Split its integral into the clear region and its complement. Equations (14.137)–(14.142) give the quantitative bound

\(\displaystyle \left| \int f (x)\{U_{k, Y} (x) - Q (x)\} \hspace{0.17em} dx \right| \leqslant \|f\|_{\infty} \{ |K_R | \alpha_k + C_{m, R} (A_k^m + C_Q) \sigma_k \} .\) (14.143)

The right side tends to zero. For fixed \(R\) and \(M > 0\), the same bound therefore holds uniformly over all smooth tests supported in \(K_R\) with \(\|f\|_{\infty} \leqslant M\); it imposes no bound on their derivatives. This uniformity is used in the stress comparison after its readout maps and normalizations have been matched to these panels. Both integrands are continuous on a neighborhood of the support, so these are ordinary integrals; no extension across coincident configurations is involved.

14.20.2Returning to cutoff-dependent insertions

It is necessary to apply this estimate to the actual insertion maps, whose values depend on the cutoff. Fix \(k\). Their eventual boundedness gives a compact closure in their finite-dimensional operator space. We use the ordered point and smeared limits of the same table \(S\); these limits also hold on subfilters. If insertion maps converge to a map \(T\) along a subfilter, multilinearity in a fixed finite basis proves that their point and smeared correlations converge to those with the limiting labels. For example, expanding an \(m\)-tuple gives finitely many terms

\(\displaystyle \sum_{a_1, \ldots, a_m} t_{1, a_1} (N, p) \cdots t_{m, a_m} (N, p) U_{N, p} (e_{a_1}, \ldots, e_{a_m}) .\)

Every scalar coefficient converges and every fixed basis correlation has the specified limit. Finite sums and products therefore give the claimed convergence for varying insertions. For the smeared expression, use the fixed dilated test \(r_k^{- 4 m} f (\hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em} / r_k)\); a change of variables identifies its limiting integral with \(\int f (x) U_{k, Y} (x) \hspace{0.17em} dx\).

Denote the right side of (14.143) by \(E_k (f)\). We claim that the cutoff smeared comparison error is eventually at most \(E_k (f) + \eta\), for every \(\eta > 0\). If not, the set where it exceeds this number is frequent in \(\mathcal{F}\). Restricting to that set gives a nontrivial filter. Compactness of the insertion maps supplies a further convergent subfilter. Along it the smeared error converges, by the preceding finite expansion, to a number at most \(E_k (f)\). But its values are all larger than \(E_k (f) + \eta\), so their limit is at least that latter number. This is a contradiction. Consequently the actual cutoff errors have the same asymptotic upper bound, and that bound tends to zero as \(k \to \infty\). This compactness argument is performed separately at each fixed \(k\) and fixed test; its eventual mesh cutoff may depend on that test and on the period index. The earlier bound is uniform in the tests after passing to the limiting insertion tuple, but this argument does not claim one cutoff that works for all tests at once. It does not replace the original ordered extraction by a new continuum table.

14.20.3The three scale inequalities

We describe precisely the estimates needed from the marked construction. Fix an upper arity \(n \geqslant 2\), a maximum grade \(D\), and a word of length \(m \leqslant n\) in grades at most \(D\). Let \(H_k > 1\) be the diverging comparison monitor. Choose three exponents \(a, b, e > 0\) and a strip exponent \(c < 0\), and set

\(\displaystyle r_k = a_k H_k^a, \qquad h_k = H_k^{- a}, \qquad \sigma_k = H_k^c .\)

At a fixed comparison depth, all three panels must use the same calibrated readout map \(T\). The lift at exponent \(a\) and the lift at exponent \(b\) then differ by the grade weight \(H_k^{(a - b) j}\) on grade \(j\). With a common finite basis, the sum of the absolute expansion coefficients of a fixed label of grade at most \(D\) is bounded by a constant times \(H_k^{(a - b) D}\). The constant depends on the label, not on \(k\). This uses the same map \(T\) on the two sides; two unrelated calibrations would not give the stated identity.

The marked comparison at the \(a\)-panel supplies (14.136) with \(e_k = C \varepsilon_k \to 0\). The centered one-point bounds at the \(b\)-panel, expanded in that common basis and in the finitely many signed and conjugated orientations, supply the bulk coefficient bound with

\(\displaystyle A_k = C_b H_k^{(a - b) D} .\)

The analogous centered difference bound at the \(e\)-panel supplies

\(\displaystyle B_k = C_e H_k^{(a - e) D} .\)

Here the product and difference estimates are the centered cell estimates of Subsection 4.1. Their use requires actual cell widths, not just these coefficient bounds. If \(w_b, w_e\) are the output levels of the latter two panels, the schedule estimates are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} a_{w_b - 1} / a_k \leqslant H_k^{b + 6}, \qquad 6 ma_{w_b} < a_{w_b - 1}, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.144)} \\ a_{w_e} / a_k \leqslant H_k^{e + 5} . \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.145)} \end{array}\)

The first gives a bulk cell strictly smaller than \(r_k / (2 R)\) once \(b + 6 < a\); the second gives a difference cell smaller than \(r_k \sigma_k\) once \(e + 5 < a + c\). Applying the centered bounds and their continuum limits then gives (14.137) and the directional bound \(B_k^m / \sigma_k\). Summing over the \(4 m\) coordinate directions gives exactly (14.138).

The width calculation also covers a change of stage inside a panel. Let \(L_{\max}\) bound every block factor between its output predecessor and \(k\), and let \(V\) be the clock cover parameter. On the selected window the bracket of Lemma 17.14 permits only the two adjacent stages. Their paid monitor bound gives \(L_{\max} \leqslant H_k\), and \(V^{10} \leqslant H_k\) eventually. By maximality of the middle level \(\ell_b\) and the root level \(w_b\), the first level on the other side of each threshold fails its defining inequality. Restoring that one block costs at most \(L_{\max}\), so

\(\displaystyle \frac{a_{\ell_b}}{a_k} \leqslant L_{\max} H_k^{b + 2}, \qquad \frac{a_{w_b}}{a_{\ell_b}} \leqslant L_{\max} V^{10} .\)

Multiplication gives \(a_{w_b} / a_k \leqslant H_k^{b + 5}\). Its predecessor costs at most one further block factor, proving the first inequality of (14.144). The smaller of the two adjacent block factors tends to infinity along the selected stages, so \(a_{w_b - 1} / a_{w_b} > 6 m\) eventually for each fixed arity. This proves its strict second inequality. Repeating the same argument for the \(e\)-panel proves (14.145). Only the two adjacent block bounds and the maximality of the levels are used; a constant-block interval is unnecessary. A common bounded offset of the three arcs leaves this calculation uniform, since it only changes the displayed powers \(b + 2\) and \(e + 2\) by that offset.

It remains to choose the exponents so that both integral errors decay. The exact sufficient inequalities are

\(\displaystyle \ \begin{aligned} c & < 0, \qquad b + 6 < a, \qquad e + 5 < a + c,\\ (a - b) Dn + c & < 0, \qquad (a - e) Dn - a - c < 0. \end{aligned}\) (14.146)

They imply \(- a < c\) and hence \(4 mh_k \leqslant \sigma_k\) eventually. They also give

\(\displaystyle A_k^m \sigma_k = C_b^m H_k^{(a - b) Dm + c} \to 0, \qquad B_k^m h_k / \sigma_k = C_e^m H_k^{(a - e) Dm - a - c} \to 0.\)

Indeed \(a > b, e\), so replacing \(m\) by \(n\) increases the exponents. For a concrete choice, write \(q = Dn\), \(x = 7 q + 1\), \(y = 6 + x\), and take

\(\displaystyle a = 10 + D + x + yq + y + 7, \qquad b = a - 7, \qquad e = a - y, \qquad c = - x.\)

Then \(b + 6 = a - 1\), \(e + 5 = a - x - 1\), and the last two exponents in (14.146) are respectively \(- 1\) and \(- 17 - D - y\). Also \(a, b, e > D + 6\), so the additional lower bounds on panel exponents are respected. These choices depend only on the finite grade and arity bounds. They make no assertion of a uniform estimate over all words.

Finally, if these common-panel data are available at arbitrarily large comparison depths, choose an increasing sequence of those depths. All the eventually valid numerical inequalities remain valid along it. There are only finitely many basis words under fixed grade and arity bounds; take the maximum of their finitely many thresholds. The preceding proof applies to each word on this one sequence. We make the extension to arbitrary labels quantitative. For the finitely many reference grades at most \(D\), choose bases and a constant \(C_D \geqslant 1\) that bounds the sum of the absolute basis coefficients of a label by \(C_D\) times its norm. Let \(\epsilon_k\) be the maximum of the finitely many basis-word errors in (14.143), with \(\|f\|_{\infty}\) factored out. Then \(\epsilon_k \to 0\). For one limiting tuple \(Y\) of insertion maps, write \(U_{k, Y} (x ; v_1, \ldots, v_m)\) and \(Q (x ; v_1, \ldots, v_m)\) for the interacting and reference correlations with those labels. Multilinearity then gives, for every word of length \(m \leqslant n\) and every test supported in the same \(K_R\),

\(\displaystyle \left| \int f (x)\{U_{k, Y} (x ; v_1, \ldots, v_m) - Q (x ; v_1, \ldots, v_m)\} \hspace{0.17em} dx \right| \leqslant C_D^m \epsilon_k \hspace{0.17em} \|f\|_{\infty} \prod_{i = 1}^m \|v_i \| .\)

At each fixed arity, the bound uses one limiting tuple of insertion maps on every basis word and is uniform in those tests and admitted tuples when the basis estimates are uniform there. Its application to the stress maps still requires the same readout and normalization identification.

Suppose the common-panel data are available at arbitrarily large depths for each finite grade, arity and window request, and the corresponding radii tend to zero. At request \(h \geqslant 2\), take \(n = D = h\) and a window containing the first \(h\) members of a prescribed compact exhaustion at each arity at most \(h\). Write \(\epsilon_k^{(h)}\) for the finite basis maximum for that request. Choose the next depth \(k \geqslant h\), strictly larger than the preceding chosen depth, so large that those bounds hold and \(C_h^h \epsilon_k^{(h)} \leqslant 1 / (h + 1)\); also make its radius smaller than the previous one and at most \(1 / (h + 1)\). This gives one increasing comparison sequence with the bound \((h + 1)^{- 1} \|f\|_{\infty} \prod_i \|v_i \|\) for that finite request. For the actual cutoff expression the same finite-basis expansion uses the ordered-filter upper bound separately for each fixed test and label tuple: divide the desired positive slack by one plus the sum of the absolute expansion coefficients, and intersect the finitely many basis-word tails. The resulting mesh tail may depend on the period. This diagonal choice does not identify its maps or filter with those of a different construction.

The empty word is the constant one and needs no strip estimate. Unit, conjugation and signed-symmetry properties of the insertion maps pass through this construction whenever they hold eventually at the finite cutoffs. Producing the common marked panel data on the selected Wilson history is a separate upstream task; the argument here proves their exact weak-convergence consequence.

14.21Applying the convergence estimates to the selected histories

The preceding convergence arguments concern particular arrays, paths and stationary branches. We now identify these objects along one construction. This identification is needed because equality of the actions at one period would not identify their coefficient arrays, and because a cutoff-dependent change of the top boundary need not preserve a limit unless it eventually leaves each fixed scale unchanged.

14.21.1One simultaneous stationary construction

Fix the scale schedule, clipping data and coordinate conventions. Denote the terminal cutoff by \(N\) and the scale by \(m \leqslant N\). The finite stationary construction of Subsection 11.21 gives paths \(R_{N, m}\), scalar corrections \(\alpha_{N, m}\) and stationary maps \(\rho_{N, m}^{p, F}\), where \(p\) is a period and \(F\) an allowed forest. All these objects are chosen together. Their one-step identities use \(R_{N, m + 1}\) as input and \(R_{N, m}\) as output. The path bounds hold at the prescribed scale widths and allowances.

For the convergence assertion we use the strengthened version of this construction. At scale \(m\) it fixes three coefficient Hilbert spaces, with encoding widths \(\delta_{m, 2}, \delta_{m, 3}, \delta_{m, 4}\) strictly larger than the paid width \(w_m\). The nonlinear path terms are encoded in their canonical selected subspaces. It also supplies the following uniformity: every admissible replacement of the input path with the same attached row and with all path and loop allowances enlarged by \(64\) has an output in these same selected spaces, at the ordinary output allowances. The output has the same classical and thermal matching identities on every period and forest. Finally, the underlying free horizon converges at each scale in all of its retained components, and the root rows have an eventual bound at some width \(v_m > w_m\). These are the extended-domain, canonical-readout and retained-horizon hypotheses used in Subsection 12.1. They belong to the one-step analytic construction; neither finite backward induction nor the argument below supplies them.

Here is why its coefficient representation is the one needed in that section. Select the three coefficient arrays of each path once, at \((N, m)\), and use that same selection in every occurrence of the path. For \(m + 2 < N\), the input arrays of the step at \(m\) are then exactly the output arrays of the step at \(m + 1\). The row at either occurrence is also the same attached root row. Thus the input/output equality holds in the coefficient spaces, before taking any limit. The one-step identities and bounds are available for all these chosen arrays. For a replacement of one array the other arrays remain fixed; their closed linear constraints and their separate budgets are preserved. The enlarged input domain therefore supplies exactly the additional steps used in the contraction argument.

Apply Subsection 12.1 to these data. At every fixed scale there are one limiting path \(R_m\) and one scalar \(\alpha_m\) such that every coefficient of \(R_{N, m}\) converges to the corresponding coefficient of \(R_m\), and \(\alpha_{N, m} \to \alpha_m\). The limiting path has the original paid bounds. For every fixed period, forest and derivative order \(r\),

\(\displaystyle D^r \rho_{N, m}^{p, F} (0) \longrightarrow D^r \rho_m^{p, F} (0) .\) (14.147)

The limiting stationary germ is determined by \(R_{m + 1}\), its retained quadratic data and its forest. Its Hessian is positive with the same lower bound. Consequently no choice of a new subsequence for a forest or derivative order is involved in (14.147). The scalar limit is the one obtained from these same paths and thermal identities.

14.21.2A single width for all four coefficient families

The common dictionaries are part of the conclusion just obtained. Write \(r_{N, m} (a)\) and \(r_m (a)\) for row coefficients on the canonical row dictionary, and \(c_{N, m, q} (a, \nu)\) and \(c_{m, q} (a, \nu)\) for the actual, exponentially encoded coefficients of the atoms of degree \(q = 2, 3, 4\). Their supports, lengths \(\ell (a)\) and color multi-indices \(\nu\) are independent of \(N\) on a tail. Choose

\(\displaystyle w_m < \tilde{w}_m < \min \{v_m, \delta_{m, 2}, \delta_{m, 3}, \delta_{m, 4} \} .\)

Then, for every \(\varepsilon > 0\), all sufficiently large \(N\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \sum_a |r_{N, m} (a) - r_m (a) | e^{\tilde{w}_m \ell (a)} & \leqslant \varepsilon, \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.148)} \\ \sum_{a, \nu} |c_{N, m, q} (a, \nu) - c_{m, q} (a, \nu) | e^{\tilde{w}_m \ell (a)} & \leqslant \varepsilon \qquad (q = 2, 3, 4) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(14.149)} \end{array}\)

For the row statement one may either sum the finitely many plaquette directions or impose the estimate separately on each; shrinking the requested \(\varepsilon\) by their number gives the displayed version.

For completeness, the two tail bounds that permit this simultaneous choice are different. If the row budget at \(v_m\) is \(B_m\), its coefficient difference at the smaller width is bounded by

\(\displaystyle 2 B_m e^{- (v_m - \tilde{w}_m) \ell (a)} .\)

For an atom, remove its encoding factor \(e^{- \delta_{m, q} \ell (a)}\). The resulting coefficient arrays have a common Hilbert bound \(C_{m, q}\), so their individual differences have absolute value at most \(2 C_{m, q}\). Their weighted majorant is therefore \(2 C_{m, q} e^{- (\delta_{m, q} - \tilde{w}_m) \ell (a)}\). Both majorants are summable on the canonical dictionaries by the geometric sums in Subsection 10.13. The coordinate limits and a finite-head, summable-tail argument prove (14.148)–(14.149). There are only four families and finitely many row directions, so their eventual estimates hold on one tail. This gives one path limit with one strictly larger width, as required in the local integral comparison.

14.21.3Changing the terminal normalization

The final Wilson normalization replaces the top path at \(m = N\). Let \(\tilde{R}_{N, m}\), \(\tilde{\alpha}_{N, m}\) and \(\tilde{\rho}_{N, m}^{p, F}\) denote the resulting data. The change of coordinates retains the exact identities

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \tilde{R}_{N, m} & = R_{N, m} & & (m < N),\\ \tilde{\alpha}_{N, m} & = \alpha_{N, m} & & \text{for all } N, m,\\ \tilde{\rho}_{N, m}^{p, F} & = \rho_{N, m}^{p, F} & & (m + 1 < N) . \end{array}\)

It also leaves the free bed unchanged for \(m < N\) and the entire associated tower unchanged for \(m + 1 < N\); its transport row is unchanged already for \(m < N\). These are identities of the maps and coefficient data, not merely equalities of their evaluated actions.

For fixed \(m\), all the identities hold once \(N > m + 1\). Deleting this finite initial segment changes none of the limits above, none of the eventual larger-width bounds and none of the retained free horizon limits. Equality of the stationary maps gives equality of every derivative at zero. Thus the final normalized construction has exactly the same \(R_m\), \(\alpha_m\) and stationary jets. In particular, replacing the terminal path has not introduced a second background for the activity estimates.

14.21.4The auxiliary stationary service

We supply the auxiliary input used in Subsection 12.9. Fix one adjacent stage pair, its paths and clips, its block side \(L\), and a derivative order \(r\). Write \(j = J_{m + 1}\) for the incoming stage. All constants below may depend on these purchased data, but not on the auxiliary period, forest or cutoff. The incoming loop allowance is denoted by \(\Lambda \geqslant 1\). The auxiliary coarse side is at least two; its fine side \(Lc\) satisfies the larger-datum room bound \(Lc \geqslant 100 (j + 7) L\). These are restrictions on \(c\) after the stage has been fixed. The selected kernel has the branch compatibility of Subsubsection 11.21.2.

Lemma 14.52. (Stationary matching on auxiliary periods) The paths of the selected retained plan admit the uniform stationary service on every sufficiently large auxiliary one-step torus. Its critical graph agrees as a germ with the selected plan wherever that plan is defined. On a coarse auxiliary side \(c\), its classical matching is exact and its thermal quadratic matching has bound

\(\displaystyle |D^2 (\mathcal{T}_c -\mathcal{D}_c) (0) [h, h'] | \leqslant | \text{Site} (c) | (A + \Gamma_j) \Lambda e^{- \min (\nu, c_j) c} \qquad (\|h\|_{\infty}, \|h' \|_{\infty} \leqslant 1),\) (14.150)

for the tested directions in the plan. Here \(A < \infty\), \(\nu > 0\) are auxiliary service constants; \(\Gamma_j, c_j > 0\) are the retained plan's thermal constants; and \(\mathcal{D}_c\) is the draft formed from its same outgoing path and scalar. The chart table is the plan's local-ratio chart. These choices are uniform over the admissible incoming paths with the purchased bounds.

Proof. Uniform local data. Use the tree-gauge coordinates of Subsubsection 11.21.2. A fixed block has a bounded number \(K\) of free coordinates per site, uniformly in the period and allowed forest. Exponential path bounds give the local derivative estimate (10.3) at every fixed required order. Here is the length payment in that assertion. Differentiating a path word \(r\) times selects at most \((1 + \ell)^r\) positions. If the selected coordinates are prescribed, the word visits all their anchors; the sum of their distances from any one of them is at most \(r \ell\). From its weight \(e^{\sigma \ell}\) reserve \(e^{\sigma \ell / 2}\) for summation and use the rest to pay those distances and \((1 + \ell)^r\). Thus one can use a positive decay width smaller than \(\sigma / (4 r)\) and a finite constant depending only on \(r, \sigma\) and the path budget. Products of a bounded number of words obey the same estimate after splitting the width between their factors. The block coordinate maps, clip and local Haar logarithm have bounded fixed-order derivatives on a fixed smaller coordinate ball; their stencils have bounded diameter at this fixed \(L\). Composition only changes these constants and the decay width. Summing free derivative indices costs the shell sum \(K \sum_{n \geqslant 0} (2 n + 1)^4 e^{- an}\), independently of the torus. This proves the local derivative input for the actual path action. The classical action uses the fixed path allowance; the loop derivatives have in addition their factor \(\Lambda\).

The positive Hessian gap is the all-period tree-gauge gap of the same selected horizon. Subsection 10.2 now constructs a common coordinate ball and the unique critical graph \(\rho_c\), with positive smaller protection radii, smoothness through the requested order, and a uniform inverse bound. Increase the local order to at least \(\max (r + 2, 4)\) when necessary. The argument there uses supremum norms for the radii, so none shrink with the auxiliary volume. Its estimates for loop and Haar derivatives give the remaining chart bounds, with at most the stated loop factor.

We shall also need exponential entry bounds for the Hessian and its first two derivatives along \(\rho_c\). These follow from the same local derivative estimate, not from a dimension-dependent norm conversion. Indeed \(D \rho = - H^{- 1} D_x F\), and differentiating the critical equation twice expresses \(D^2 \rho\) as \(H^{- 1}\) times a sum of third derivatives of the action evaluated on first graph derivatives. The inverse decays exponentially by Subsection 8.13. Root each derivative at its unsummed matrix index and sum the remaining indices with the preceding shell bound, reducing the decay width at each of these finitely many convolutions. The chain rule for \(H (x, \rho_c (x))\) through order two then yields constants \(d, B, T, Q > 0\), uniform in the period and forest, such that its inverse, first jets and second jets have entry bounds respectively \(Be^{- d \text{dist}}\), \(Te^{- d \text{dist}}\) and \(Qe^{- d \text{dist}}\), for external directions of supremum norm at most one. The gap fixes \(B\); these constants involve classical path derivatives only, so the loop factor is not squared.

Replication and uniqueness. Choose a scheduled coarse side \(C = nc\), beyond the plan's volume threshold, using Subsection 19.2. Lift the auxiliary forest and its local coordinate conventions periodically. There are \(n^4\) copies of each free coordinate. Replication copies the same path words, block maps and local chart ratios. The stationary equation on a replicated field is therefore the replication of the smaller stationary equation. Both critical graphs tend to zero and belong to the common uniqueness zone near zero. Their pullbacks under replication agree there. The scheduled plan's graph has the same criticality and lies in that zone after restricting its germ, so it too is the same graph. This proves branch identification, rather than choosing a new branch for the auxiliary estimates. Classical action and outgoing path sums on replicated fields are exactly \(n^4\) copies. The plan's classical matching therefore descends after division by \(n^4\).

The determinant error. We give the finite-matrix calculation because the thermal term does not replicate exactly. Let \(\pi\) map large free coordinates to their small-period addresses and let \(E_{ia} =\boldsymbol{1}_{\{\pi (i) = a\}}\). Write \(S (x)\) for the large Hessian on replicated coarse fields and \(T (x)\) for the small Hessian. Equality of the replicated stationary equations, differentiated in the free variables, gives

\(\displaystyle S (x) E = ET (x) .\)

Equivalently, the row sum of \(S\) over a fibre equals the corresponding entry of \(T\). It is not an equality of individual inverse blocks. Inverting and differentiating once or twice preserves this intertwining relation, as does multiplication. If \(PE = ER\), the equal fibre sizes imply the exact trace identity

\(\displaystyle n^4 \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} R - \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} P = \sum_i \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} a \ne i\\ \pi (a) = \pi (i) \end{array}$}}} P_{ia} .\) (14.151)

Distinct coordinates in one fibre have anchor distance at least \(c\). With \(S_K (t) = K \sum_{r \geqslant 0} (2 r + 1)^4 e^{- tr}\), an entry bound \(|P_{ia} | \leqslant C_P e^{- 2 t \text{dist} (i, a)}\) consequently gives

\(\displaystyle |n^4 \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} R - \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} P| \leqslant |I_C | C_P S_K (t) e^{- tc} .\)

This follows by extracting \(e^{- tc}\) and summing the remaining \(e^{- t \text{dist}}\) over all coordinates, which only enlarges the sum over the fibre.

For clarity, exponential entry bounds are stable under the products needed here: if two matrices have bounds \(Ae^{- a \text{dist}}\) and \(B' e^{- a \text{dist}}\), then their product has bound \(AB' S_K (a / 2) e^{- (a / 2) \text{dist}}\). Extract the last exponential by the triangle inequality and sum a remaining exponential over the intermediate index. Set \(I = S (0)^{- 1}\) and use

\(\displaystyle D^2 \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det S [h, h'] = \tfrac{1}{2} \text{tr} (IS_{hh'} - IS_h IS_{h'}) .\)

The first product is bounded at width \(d / 4\) by \(BQS_K (d / 4)\), and each \(IS_h\) is bounded at width \(d / 2\) by \(H_0 = BTS_K (d / 2)\). Thus the second product is bounded at width \(d / 4\) by \(H_0^2 S_K (d / 4)\). Apply (14.151) with \(t = d / 8\) to the two products. The absolute difference of the determinant jets, with the small jet multiplied by \(n^4\), is at most

\(\displaystyle |I_C | [BQ + H_0^2] S_K (d / 4) S_K (d / 8) e^{- dc / 8} .\)

The local Haar logarithm and the incoming loop sum replicate exactly along the identified graphs, so their jets cancel in this difference. There are at most \(KL^4 C^4\) free coordinates. Dividing by \(n^4\) therefore gives constants \(A < \infty\), \(\nu = d / 8 > 0\) for which

\(\displaystyle |D^2 \mathcal{T}_c - n^{- 4} D^2 (\mathcal{T}_C \circ E_c) | \leqslant c^4 Ae^{- \nu c},\)

where \(E_c\) is coarse-field replication and the directions have norm at most one. This also explains the local-ratio requirement: the Jacobian terms must be the same copied coordinate terms in both expressions.

Transfer of the retained estimate. Proposition 11.37, on the selected horizon and paths, retains the scheduled estimate with amplitude \(\Gamma_j \Lambda\) and rate \(c_j\). Its hypotheses are the same purchased one-step path construction and quadratic horizon used at the beginning of this section; no new stationary plan is selected for the auxiliary torus. Apply that estimate to the replicated tested directions. They retain supremum norm one, and the draft copies exactly. Division by \(n^4\) turns its site factor into \(c^4\). The triangle inequality yields

\(\displaystyle |D^2 (\mathcal{T}_c -\mathcal{D}_c) | \leqslant c^4 \{Ae^{- \nu c} + \Gamma_j \Lambda e^{- c_j C} \} .\)

Since \(C \geqslant c\) and \(\Lambda \geqslant 1\), this implies (14.150). All constants were fixed before \(c, C\) and the forest. The same reasoning applies to any allowed auxiliary forest and its periodic lift; uniqueness identifies any other admissible germ on it without changing the constants.\(\Box\)

14.21.5The activity and coupling limits on this background

Fix a period \(p\). The regular supports, forced inventories and norms at scale \(m\) depend on the fixed scale and period, not on \(N\). Embed their actual arrays into the one completion \(\mathcal{H}_{m, p}\) of Subsection 12.8. The representative of each finite history has exactly its prescribed activities; the embedding is the same one used in the finite-test estimates. Its norm is at most \(1 / 8\). At every retained scale its path is \(\tilde{R}_{N, m}\), by the construction's input/output identities. The scalar in its coupling recursion is \(\tilde{\alpha}_{N, m}\), not a newly chosen correction.

The finite real-test estimates and their ray consequence in Subsection 12.7 give the two one-step comparison bounds in (12.52)–(12.53). Their constants are \(8 / 128\) for the activity and \(4 s_m\) for the scalar correction. The entry-threshold calculation at the end of Subsection 12.8 gives \(\sum_{j < m} s_j \leqslant B_m / 128\) for these same allowances. The larger-width path limit above, the convergent stationary jets and the retained free horizon supply the background data in the common-variable comparison. The compensated parameter estimates of Sections 9.8 and 9.9, with the suppliers of Subsection 9.10, then give its local continuity, including the projected scalar output. Each application retains the funded transition and output identities stated there. To apply this continuity to every auxiliary history with a prescribed coupling script and zero terminal activity, one must verify those transition hypotheses for its actual input at each step. In particular, the protected chart, funding and renewed bounds, downward-map bounds and output identification must refer to that input and the same path and dictionaries. Availability of a one-step map on the allowed ball alone does not establish these supplier hypotheses. For the auxiliary zero-array inputs, Lemmas 14.52 and 14.53 below verify this matching. The ordered derivative bounds of the cited comparison argument are not a further ingredient beyond this matching. Their supplier construction in Subsection 9.10 is stated at a fixed derivative order \(j\) for the fine representation satisfying the local hypotheses of Subsection 8.28. It yields the jet bounds at every order through \(j\) by the same local estimates that give the value bounds: the elementary compensated factors with their support-dependent derivative scale, the cluster jet bounds, and the cluster criterion for \(2^j u_P\) with its reserve. The order needed in the continuity argument is at most the order \(j\) supplied. Hence no value-order continuity theorem is required. What remains is a matching statement, not a new estimate. The auxiliary dictionary of Subsection 12.9, that is the chartlet with zero regular activity at the stage coupling, must satisfy the local hypotheses of Sections 8.28 and 9.10, on the same input, path and dictionaries, with the healthy-mask, width and cutoff premises retained there. Lemma 14.52 supplies its stationary service on these same paths, and Lemma 14.53 checks the remaining local hypotheses. Thus the auxiliary matching required by the convergence argument is available on this construction.

Lemma 14.53. (The auxiliary chartlet meets the comparison hypotheses) Fix a stage and a point \(x\) of an input interval. Consider the auxiliary torus of Subsection 12.9, carrying the same block bridge, the same two paths and clips, and the fine threshold, radius and band at \(\beta_F (x_0)\). Consider its reference fine representation, the bare path action with the zero activity array. Use the auxiliary stationary service of Lemma 14.52, on the same incoming path, one-step datum and local-ratio chart. Then this representation satisfies the incoming hypotheses of Sections 8.28 and 9.10, at every fixed derivative order \(j\), as follows:

  1. Incoming activity budgets. The regular and forced arrays are zero. Hence their regular and forced costs vanish, and every activity budget, packet hypothesis and cluster hypothesis imposed on them holds trivially.

  2. Sector and shell access. The block equation, the two adjacent kernel bounds and the all-period positive lower bounds are those of the selected horizon. They apply on every auxiliary torus, so the local expansion and its differentiated remainder bounds are available there. The shell-access constants are those of the same sector construction.

  3. Warnings. The deterministic safety and plateau inequalities of Subsection 8.3 hold, with their safety conditions produced in Subsection 8.15. That production concerns the fixed sector data and does not involve the activity array.

  4. Protected chart. The comparison chart is the stationary chart of Subsection 10.2, restricted to the coarse horizon. Its identification with the graph used in the stationary estimates is the branch identification in Lemma 14.52, on the same block, path and clipping data.

  5. Running-curve data. The renewed estimates are the zero costs of (1) together with the fine path bounds and the clock inequality for the loop allowance. The geometric bounds on the downward map are the kernel bounds of (2). The initial action is the admissible bare path action. The inequality \(\text{preExponent} / B \leqslant \text{width} / 2\) is (12.59), which gives \(A_c / B_c \leqslant \sigma_c / 4\). The clipping, the path and the regular and forced dictionaries are those of the physical comparison, with elementary labels identified by the identity. The displayed output is the constructed output of the local expansion on that torus.

The healthy-support condition on a tested family of labels is not a property of the representation. It is a premise of Subsection 8.28 for each family to which the product estimate is applied, and it is retained there unchanged. Consequently the supplier construction of Subsection 9.10 applies to the auxiliary chartlet at order \(j\). It yields the ordered derivative bounds at every order through \(j\) by the same estimates as the value bounds.

Proof. Each item is read off from the construction of the auxiliary data in the subsection “Keeping the physical and auxiliary data fixed” of Subsection 12.9, together with the sections cited in the statement.

(1) The array is zero, so every coefficient vanishes. Regular and forced costs are suprema of sums of nonnegative multiples of the absolute values of these coefficients, hence zero. Every incoming budget of the two cited sections is an upper bound by a nonnegative allowance, so it holds.

(2) The cited subsection states that the auxiliary tori carry the same block bridge and paths, and that the all-period kernel bounds apply on every such torus, so that the local expansion and its differentiated remainder bounds are available there. The shell-access constants are constants of the sector construction on the selected horizon, which is the same on these tori.

(3) The safety and plateau inequalities are deterministic statements about the fixed sector, warning catalogue and thresholds, produced in Subsection 8.15. They do not involve the activity array.

(4) This is the protected-chart requirement of Subsection 9.10. That section identifies a stationary chart with the graph of its estimates exactly when its representative is a critical point of the same tested action in the fine zone. On the auxiliary torus, this is the identification of the auxiliary stationary branch with the plan's branch, proved in Lemma 14.52. Its eventual protected-field condition holds because \(\tau (x) \to 0\).

(5) Each clause is quoted from the cited subsection and from (12.59), since \(A_c / B_c \leqslant \sigma_c / 4 \leqslant \sigma_c / 2\). The output identification holds because the auxiliary output is, by definition, the output of the local expansion applied to this representation.

Finally, Subsection 9.10 chooses its thresholds before the incoming representation is supplied, and then instantiates its constants at that representation. Its conclusions therefore apply to every representation satisfying (1)–(5), and in particular to the chartlet.\(\Box\)

With these same-input verifications, all hypotheses of Subsection 12.8 are on one and the same family. For any terminal sequence \(N \to \infty\) and any corresponding finite trajectories whose bottom couplings tend to \(b\), there are, at every fixed \(m, p\), limits

\(\displaystyle \beta_{N, m} \longrightarrow \beta_m, \qquad x_{N, m} \longrightarrow x_m \text{in } \mathcal{H}_{m, p}, \qquad q_{N, m} \longrightarrow q_m .\)

Here \(q_{N, m}\) is the scalar activity correction, distinguished from the path correction \(\alpha_{N, m}\). They obey

\(\displaystyle \beta_m - \beta_{m + 1} - \alpha_m = q_m .\)

Two sequences with the same bottom limit \(b\) give the same limits: interleave them, whose terminal indices still tend to infinity, and apply the same uniqueness proof. No compactness of a ball in \(\mathcal{H}_{m, p}\) is used. The norm convergence and the positive partition lower bound in Subsection 10.13 now apply to the actual normalized densities at that scale.

This application retains the strengthened stationary construction, its convergent free horizon and the funded nonlinear transition. The argument establishes compatibility of their consequences; the analytic production of these inputs remains the responsibility of the corresponding construction estimates.
